### **Directory to Species**

Families are presented in taxonomic order which is the order they are presented in, but genera and species are in alphabetical order.

### **Preface**

 The author gathered the information for this book during his work for the Department of Marine & Wildlife Resources in American Samoa from late 2003 to the present.

--- SENTENCE 33 ---
Almost all the work was done in American Samoa and most of the description of reefs is of reefs in American Samoa, with much less from independent Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 34 ---
However, they are one island chain, built by the same geological processes (except Rose Atoll and Swains), with the same culture, and with almost all of the same coral species.

--- SENTENCE 35 ---
So this book applies equally to both areas.

--- SENTENCE 36 ---
Studying coral species is not for the faint-hearted.

--- SENTENCE 37 ---
It is a formidable task, especially where diversity is high.

--- SENTENCE 38 ---
Corals are relatively easy to learn in a place like Hawaii (Fenner, 2005) or the Caribbean (Humann, 2002; Sheppard, 2007), where there are only about 60 species.

--- SENTENCE 39 ---
Over 260 coral species have been seen by the author in the Samoan archipelago and over 400 species names have been reported previously by others (an unknown number of which are correct).

--- SENTENCE 40 ---
This is much less than the number of fish species, with 945 reef fish species now known from the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 41 ---
But corals are highly variable, with variation within species often very great, making it very hard to tell species apart, since the differences between species may not be easily discerned among the riot of variation.

--- SENTENCE 42 ---
Variation occurs within a single colony as well as between colonies within a species, and between locations within a species.

--- SENTENCE 43 ---
Hybridization between some species is easy to produce in the lab and has been proposed to be common, and the amount of genetic connectivity between corals at different islands may change over longer periods of time due to changing currents that change connectivity (Veron, 1995, 2000).

--- SENTENCE 44 ---
Looking at living corals in the water has advantages and disadvantages.

--- SENTENCE 45 ---
One advantage is that you can see the whole coral not just a fragment like a sample in a museum.

--- SENTENCE 46 ---
Another is that you can see many colonies (if it isn't rare) and learn what the variation is within the species.

--- SENTENCE 47 ---
A third is that you can see the typical tissue color and any tissue clues like tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 48 ---
The disadvantages include the fact that the skeleton on which the taxonomy is based is covered by living tissue you usually can't see through so you can't see the things the taxonomy is based on.

--- SENTENCE 49 ---
In addition, you can't see the fine skeletal details because you have no microscope.

--- SENTENCE 50 ---
Add to that being thrown around by waves, condensation in your mask making things blurry, and running out of air and having to surface.

--- SENTENCE 51 ---
All of which make it especially difficult to distinguish species when working at a new location, where many species look different from elsewhere.

--- SENTENCE 52 ---
Because coral identification is so difficult, you need all the help you can get.

--- SENTENCE 53 ---
Although there are much more comprehensive coral identification books such as that by Veron (2000), many of the species in that book may not occur in American Samoa (and at the very least are not common), and you have to search through pictures of many corals you haven't seen to find the ones you have seen.

--- SENTENCE 54 ---
A book that only has the corals

found in the Samoan Archipelago will be much easier to work with.

--- SENTENCE 55 ---
Further, the photos in a comprehensive book like Veron (2000) were not taken in the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 56 ---
Because some corals look different in different locations, the corals in such a guide book may not look like those in the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 57 ---
All the photos in this book were taken in American Samoa except for two species photographed in independent Samoa, and the descriptions give what they look like here, not other places.

--- SENTENCE 58 ---
And last, an encyclopedic guide like Veron (2000) is unwieldy and heavy, a huge set of three volumes, while the present guide is more easily portable (depending on the computer you have it on).

--- SENTENCE 59 ---
Mind you, Veron (2000) is a marvelous accomplishment and a huge step forward.

--- SENTENCE 60 ---
This book benefits greatly from the Veron book and others such as Wallace (1999), Hoeksema (1989) and Randall and Myers (1983).

--- SENTENCE 61 ---
Because many coral species have wide distributions, many of the species in this book will not only be in the Samoan archipelago, but widespread in the Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 62 ---
Many of the species illustrated may look very similar on nearby archipelagos particularly.

--- SENTENCE 63 ---
Unfortunately there are very few English common names or Samoan names for most of these coral species, and the English names at least are not applied consistently.

--- SENTENCE 64 ---
For most species, it will be necessary to learn the Latinized scientific names, but then these are the only accurate names.

--- SENTENCE 65 ---
To help in identification, the description of most species includes both a picture of a whole colony and a closeup of the details of the coral surface.

--- SENTENCE 66 ---
Also, more than one picture of a species was included to illustrate some of the different shapes and colors of the species.

--- SENTENCE 67 ---
In addition, common colors are illustrated in addition to beautiful colors which are rare.

--- SENTENCE 68 ---
The text indicates what the differences are between each species and the species that appears most similar to it, as well as how common the species are, and which colors or shapes are the most common for that species.

--- SENTENCE 69 ---
Also, the species within a genus are placed in an order to try to place similar looking corals one after another, to facilitate comparisons.

--- SENTENCE 70 ---
Genera are also placed in an order within a family in order to try to place similar looking corals together, even though at times the order does not correspond to the conventional taxonomic ordering.

--- SENTENCE 71 ---
Because you need all the help you can get, there is a section showing photos of eight of the most common species on the island, followed by list of the most common species by reef one, and then a coral genus directory showing thumbnails of each of the genera.

--- SENTENCE 72 ---
For a large genus like *Acropora*, a few of the different types of shapes are illustrated.

--- SENTENCE 73 ---
These directories refer to the pages on which the genera are found, and can help you find the coral you are looking for.

--- SENTENCE 74 ---
A good way to study corals is to look in the book before getting in the water, and then review the book after getting out of the water and getting dry.

--- SENTENCE 75 ---
This book was published in part to help workers better monitor the reefs in coming years, as well as to build pride in the Samoan people for the richness of their biological heritage and a desire to see to it that the reefs are handed to the next generation in as healthy a condition as the present generation received them or even healthier.

--- SENTENCE 76 ---
Of course, the book is also intended for amateur naturalists, snorkelers and divers of all ages and levels of familiarity with coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 77 ---
On a personal note, the author likes to relate that he lives on a volcanic rock about 17 miles long that lies near the center of an ocean that covers nearly half of the planet, and the nearest big university library is as far as New York is from London.

--- SENTENCE 78 ---
The Samoan archipelago lies about 14 degrees south of the equator in the tropics, and American

Samoa is the only U.S. territory south of the equator.

--- SENTENCE 79 ---
It is a beautiful and fascinating place where few outsiders have ventured, and which still has a relatively healthy reef.

--- SENTENCE 80 ---
The opinions expressed in this book are entirely those of the author, and not necessarily those of the Dept. of Marine & Wildlife, or the American Samoa Government.

#### **Introduction: The Samoan Volcanic Islands**

The Samoan archipelago is an east-west chain of volcanic islands produced by a "hot spot," or "mantle plume" much like that in Hawai'i.

--- SENTENCE 103 ---
The archipelago consists of two large volcanic islands in the independent country of Samoa: Savai'i and Upolu, and five smaller volcanic islands in American Samoa: the medium sized island of Tutuila (about 24 km or 17 miles long), and four small islands, Aunu'u, Ofu, Olosega and Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 104 ---
There are also several smaller islands in independent Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 105 ---
The Territory of American Samoa also includes two atolls, Rose Atoll 87.5 miles (140 km) east of Ta'u, and Swains Atoll, 204 miles (327 km) north of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 106 ---
Both of these atolls are much older than the volcanic islands, and may be geologically part of the Tokelau Islands, north of the Samoan Archipelago, but in any case are not part of the Samoan hotspot chain.

--- SENTENCE 107 ---
Swains is closer to Tokelau and was originally inhabited by Tokelauans.

--- SENTENCE 108 ---
Both Swains and Rose are tiny, with Swains about 2 miles diameter and Rose about one mile diameter (with the only land it has being two tiny islands much smaller than the atoll).

--- SENTENCE 109 ---
Although the islands of American Samoa are small, they are actually the tops of enormous mountains.

--- SENTENCE 110 ---
Tutuila rises 2860 m (9439 ft) just to reach the water surface, and another 2,142 feet above that for a total height of 3509 m (= 3.5 km or 11,580 ft).

--- SENTENCE 111 ---
It has a volume of 4957 cubic kilometers (1210 cubic miles)!!

--- SENTENCE 112 ---
Ta'u reaches 961 m (3,170 ft, the highest spot in American Samoa) above water for a total height of 3821 m (3.8 km or 12,608 ft).

--- SENTENCE 113 ---
A hot spot of molten lava lies below the solid plate that forms the floor of the Pacific Ocean.

--- SENTENCE 114 ---
The hot spot is currently located under a submarine volcano located 28 miles east of Ta'u at the eastern end of the archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 115 ---
This submarine volcano (named Vailulu'u in recent times) was probably the location of submarine eruptions in 1866 that caused dense clouds of smoke and pumice to erupt from the ocean for several months.

--- SENTENCE 116 ---
It was found to reach to within about 2000 feet of the surface of the ocean when it was studied in 1999.

--- SENTENCE 117 ---
The top of the mountain has a large caldera (crater), which is 2000 m (2 km) wide and 400 meters deeper in the center than along the rim (Luazon and Dybas, 2000), and has many "black smokers" in it.

--- SENTENCE 118 ---
These are places where very hot water (about 490° F or 200° C) comes out, carrying large amounts of minerals, which turn the water coming out black and deposits minerals that form a chimney-like structure.

--- SENTENCE 119 ---
The water is well above the boiling temperature at sea level, but doesn't boil because of the greater

pressure at that depth.

--- SENTENCE 120 ---
In a subsequent study in 2005, a new cone was found in the caldera which rises to within 1800 feet (550 m) of the surface.

--- SENTENCE 121 ---
The new cone is made of pillow lava, and was named Nafanua (Life Science Staff, 2005; Staudingel et al., 2006; Than, 2006).

--- SENTENCE 122 ---
So this is a very active volcano, which may reach the surface in the relatively near future and form a new volcanic island (or could fall silent for millennia).

--- SENTENCE 123 ---
Since the seafloor around it is 4790 m deep, this is already a large volcano, 13,494 ft or 4089 m tall and 20 km long by 10 km wide at the base, with a total volume of 3613 km<sup>3</sup> (882 mi<sup>3</sup>, figures from the seamount catalog, http://earthref.org/SC/) (the differences in calculated height with the other islands likely stems from choosing a different seafloor depth-

--- SENTENCE 124 ---
it is hard to choose a seafloor depth since it varies some from area to area.)

--- SENTENCE 125 ---
That makes it nearly as tall as Mauna Loa and Mauna Kea in Hawaii are from the water surface, or the tallest mountains in the continental U.S. (Mount Rainier and Mount Whitney) and Europe (Mt. Blanc).

--- SENTENCE 126 ---
Evidence indicates that the large caldera at the summit was produced by a collapse of a previous higher summit, and that previous summit probably came close to the surface (Staudingel et al., 2006).

--- SENTENCE 127 ---
The present cone may be rebuilding the summit, much as a lava dome grew in the crater of the 1980 explosion that blew the top off Mt. St. Helens in Washington State.

!

--- SENTENCE 128 ---
[](_page_8_Picture_1.jpeg)

Map of the depths around Tutuila, produced by the Coral Reef Ecosystem Division of NOAA.

--- SENTENCE 129 ---
The ages of the volcanic islands in the Samoan chain are in a sequence, with the youngest in the east, and ages increasing toward the west.

--- SENTENCE 130 ---
Vailulu'u in the east is presently active, Ta'u is 100,000 years old, Ofu-Olosega is 300,000 years old, Tutuila is about 1.28-1.54 million years old, Upolu in Western Samoa is 1.7-2.8 million years old (McDougall, 1985), Savai'i is 5 million years old (Jackson et al. 2007; Koppers et al. 2008) and seamounts to the west of Savai'i are up to 40 million years old (Hart et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 131 ---
This sequence of ages is produced by the movement of the floor of the Pacific over a hotspot of molten lava.

--- SENTENCE 132 ---
Ocean floors are made of solid plates of rock, floating on

top of a gooey, viscous, semi-fluid of hot rock.

--- SENTENCE 133 ---
Some of the plates, such as the huge one that covers almost all of the North Pacific and the eastern part of the South Pacific, move.

--- SENTENCE 134 ---
This Pacific plate is moving toward the west-northwest at about 7 cm a year, about as fast as toenails grow or half as fast as fingernails grow.

--- SENTENCE 135 ---
The hotspot of molten lava that produced the island chain is nearly stationary.

--- SENTENCE 136 ---
From time to time the molten lava erupts through the solid plate, forming a volcano that is built up on top of the plate.

--- SENTENCE 137 ---
Since the hotspot stays nearly still and the plate moves, the result is a row of volcanoes which increase in age with distance from the currently erupting volcano which is over the hotspot.

--- SENTENCE 138 ---
The Hawaiian chain was built in the same fashion and has the same sequence of ages of the volcanoes.

--- SENTENCE 139 ---
The ancient Hawaiians actually figured this out first, and believed that the volcano god Pele started on the older islands and moved to the newer islands ending on the big island of Hawaii at Kiluea, where she lives today.

--- SENTENCE 140 ---
If anyone doubts that geologic features on earth are this old, consider that the first scientists indeed thought they were very young.

--- SENTENCE 141 ---
But as geologists accumulated information, more and more information indicated these things are very old indeed.

--- SENTENCE 142 ---
Initially, in continental areas layers of sedimentary rock were found to be one on top of each other, in some places miles thick!

--- SENTENCE 143 ---
Observation of rivers that release the sediments that get compressed into these sedimentary layers revealed it is very slow, way too slow to produce miles thick sedimentary layers in anything but a very long time.

--- SENTENCE 144 ---
Geologists learned to identify the same sedimentary rocks at different locations based on the types of animal shells in them (like snails and clams).

--- SENTENCE 145 ---
They were then able to put together even longer sequences.

--- SENTENCE 146 ---
Then along came radioactive isotope dating.

--- SENTENCE 147 ---
This uses the radioactive decay of isotopes of chemical elements.

--- SENTENCE 148 ---
The speed with which they decay can be measured in labs all around the world.

--- SENTENCE 149 ---
Carbon 14 is very useful because all you need is some carbon to date something, like ashes from a campfire, or a skeleton, plus it works on relatively short time scales out to about 20,000 years.

--- SENTENCE 150 ---
There are a whole collection of different isotopes with different decay rates that can be used.

--- SENTENCE 151 ---
The results they produce are consistent with all the previous evidence, and they often overlap with each other so you can date something with two different isotopes, and guess what: the results are right on top of each other.

--- SENTENCE 152 ---
This is physics, not something someone made up.

--- SENTENCE 153 ---
It is real, hard, science.

--- SENTENCE 154 ---
Further it fits very well with other ways of figuring out how old the solar system is by astronomers working with physicists.

--- SENTENCE 155 ---
Astronomers can measure how far it is to nearby stars using a method from geometery called "parallax."

--- SENTENCE 156 ---
Then you can use that to figure out how far some nearby stars called "Cepheid Variables" are away.

--- SENTENCE 157 ---
Then you use the known intrinsic brightness of Cepheid Variables to determine how far away Cepheid Variables are which are much farther away.

--- SENTENCE 158 ---
Several other such techniques can be used to extend knowledge of distant objects in the universe, and calibrate the "red shift" of stars and galaxies, which varies with the distance, and can be used for the most distant objects (but not near objects).

--- SENTENCE 159 ---
Huge numbers of stars are more than 10,000 light years away (the distance light travels in a year), and so sent that light out over 10,000 years ago.

--- SENTENCE 160 ---
Other objects are over 100,000 light years away, a million light years away, and so on, with the most distant objects known nearly 13 billion light years away, and so the universe has been here for over 13 billion years (our sun is only about 4.5 billion years old).

--- SENTENCE 161 ---
The result of radioactive isotope dating is that the oldest rocks on earth are about 4 billion years old.

--- SENTENCE 162 ---
1.5 million years for Tutuila may sound old, but actually in geologic terms it is quite young.

--- SENTENCE 163 ---
When I lived long ago in Colorado, I could see a mountain that had rocks

3.5 billion years old near the top.

--- SENTENCE 164 ---
Doesn't mean at all that God didn't create the universe.

--- SENTENCE 165 ---
Modern science all points to the universe starting as a huge "big bang" that resembles a creation event, about 13.7 billion years ago.

--- SENTENCE 166 ---
All this scientific evidence is cross checked and confirmed many many times.

--- SENTENCE 167 ---
It is not wrong, though the universe may be 13.6 billion or 13.8 billion years old.

--- SENTENCE 168 ---
It is, in any case, "astronomically" old (that's where that saying comes from, because things in the universe are so incredibly BIG, far away, and old).

--- SENTENCE 169 ---
If you choose to believe in the creation of the universe by God, there is nothing to stop you, in fact it is easy to see how the scientific facts could fit with that.

--- SENTENCE 170 ---
It's just that things are a lot older than most people realize.

--- SENTENCE 171 ---
No worries, update your understanding, and live happily ever after!

--- SENTENCE 172 ---
One small note is that everything on sea floors is relatively young, except chunks of continent like New Caledonia.

--- SENTENCE 173 ---
Because all the seafloor gets subducted back down into the earth in about 100 million years or so, the seafloor and any volcanoes on it are younger than that.

--- SENTENCE 174 ---
Much much younger than rocks on land in some places.

--- SENTENCE 175 ---
In 100 million years or so, our own islands will go down a trench and go deep into the earth, melt, and bubble up as an Andesite volcano.

--- SENTENCE 176 ---
Maybe some place like the Philippines or perhaps New Guinea.

--- SENTENCE 177 ---
Nothing to worry about.

--- SENTENCE 178 ---
The ages of the volcanic islands of Samoa are not only in sequence, but the differences in age depend on the distances between the islands, such that the data is consistent with our knowledge that the Pacific Plate moves west northwestward in this area at about 3 inches or 7.3 cm a year.

--- SENTENCE 179 ---
So as the plate slowly moves west northwestward, the hot spot punches through from time to time, forming volcanic islands or seamounts.

--- SENTENCE 180 ---
Olosega which is one of the most eastern volcanic islands in the chain had some bubbling activity in the water on its eastern side in the 1960's.

--- SENTENCE 181 ---
Ofu, just to the west of Olosega, has cool bubbles coming out of the bottom in shallow water off the west end of the airport runway.

--- SENTENCE 182 ---
A ridge connects Ofu-Olosega and Ta'u, and activity along it has been reported in the past.

--- SENTENCE 183 ---
The westernmost island in western Samoa, Savai'i, has also experienced recent volcanism, with the most recent lava flows occurring in 1906-1911.

--- SENTENCE 184 ---
All rocks on the surface of the island of Savai'i are very young.

--- SENTENCE 185 ---
Some lava flows are so young that there is almost nothing growing on them yet.

--- SENTENCE 186 ---
One suggestion is that since it is located fairly close to the northern end of the Tongan Trench, it may be undergoing stress which has broken openings for lava.

--- SENTENCE 187 ---
North of the Tonga Trench the Pacific plate stays flat and continues moving westward, while east of the trench it bends down and is thrust deep into the earth at an angle.

--- SENTENCE 188 ---
In between, the plate must be bent down into a continuation of the Tonga Trench that goes westward.

--- SENTENCE 189 ---
It seems likely that plate near that is under significant stress, which might open cracks for lava to flow upward in.

--- SENTENCE 190 ---
If this is true, the recent lavas should by lying on top of lavas over 2 million years old.

--- SENTENCE 191 ---
Recent dating of rocks dredged from the deep slopes of Savai'i give dates of 4.9-5.1 million years old from three different locations, confirming that it is the oldest island in the Samoan hotspot trail (Jackson et al. 2007; Koppers et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 192 ---
Although there have been no eruptions on Upolu in recorded history, most of the island is covered with relatively young lava flows that are nearly flat (shield volcano shape) with cinder cones along the center ridge.

--- SENTENCE 193 ---
Savai'i is one big shield volcano just like those in Hawaii, and from a distance looks low and very wide.

--- SENTENCE 194 ---
It is indeed very wide, and the outer edges of the lava are probably on top of coral reef that formed on the edge of the original, older

island that was 5 million years old.

--- SENTENCE 195 ---
No one has confirmed that to my knowledge.

--- SENTENCE 196 ---
Savai'i at its peak is the highest point in the chain, and thus is actually the tallest mountain in the chain, even though it doesn't look that way.

--- SENTENCE 197 ---
There is a row of small cinder cones along the crest of Savai'i, in line with the trend of the Samoan chain, just like they are on Upolu.

--- SENTENCE 198 ---
Only the east end of Upolu has the tall, steep, eroded mountains typical of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 199 ---
Thus, the same process that produces the eruptions on Savai'i has likely produced eruptions on Upolu.

--- SENTENCE 200 ---
Ta'u has much steeper slopes than Savai'i, even where the slopes are intact.

--- SENTENCE 201 ---
This might be because the lava was more viscous on Ta'u than Savai'i.

--- SENTENCE 202 ---
If the theory about the stress on the plate producing cracks which lava can pass up through is correct, than that suggests that magma chambers under a volcano continue to have molten lava in them at least 5 million years after the first volcano was formed, capable of producing fluid Pahoehoe (ropy surface) flows.

--- SENTENCE 203 ---
In some places like the cinder cones of Haleakala on Maui or Diamond Head on Oahu, cinder eruptions occur long after the volcano was formed, in what is called the post-erosional phase.

--- SENTENCE 204 ---
The eastern and northern wall of Pago Pago harbor is a steep curving cliff which is part of the original main caldera on Tutuila (McDougall, 1985).

--- SENTENCE 205 ---
The rest of the caldera rim can no longer be seen.

--- SENTENCE 206 ---
Rainmaker Mountain appears to be a volcanic plug in the caldera wall.

--- SENTENCE 207 ---
Volcanic plugs are where the throat of a volcano gets filled with lava that forms hard rock, which subsequently resists erosion as material around it is eroded away, leaving it standing on its own.

--- SENTENCE 208 ---
The tallest spot on Tutuila is surely another plug, as is another pyramid-shaped rock near the road in Nu'uuli.

--- SENTENCE 209 ---
Tutuila is an elongated shape, even though a single volcano is likely to form a circular cone.

--- SENTENCE 210 ---
The elongated shape came from additional vents to the west and east of the main harbor (McDougall, 1985).

--- SENTENCE 211 ---
A large cockscomb extending out into the ocean on the northern shore at Vatia is a dike, formed when lava filled a vertical crack in the volcano and solidified into a harder mass than the volcano around it, and was left when the rest of the volcano eroded away.

--- SENTENCE 212 ---
Much smaller dikes than the one at Vatia are fairly common in the rocks of Tutuila, and can be seen in some of the sea cliffs.

--- SENTENCE 213 ---
The cockscomb dike shows that the original island was larger than that present today, perhaps about twice the size, and the original shoreline probably was close to where the edge of the submarine shelf around the island now ends, about 1.6 km (a mile) offshore and 4.8 km (3 miles) offshore at the western end of the island.

--- SENTENCE 214 ---
While the island above water was eroded away, the part below the ocean was not subject to the erosion from waves on the shoreline and stream erosion, and remained more intact, and indeed was built up by the growth of coral reef.

--- SENTENCE 215 ---
In addition, gradual slumping and sudden slides often occur on volcano flanks (as they have in Hawaii), and have likely contributed to the erosion of the island.

--- SENTENCE 216 ---
Small slides above water on the steep slopes of Tutuila are a common occurrence even now.

--- SENTENCE 217 ---
These processes can occur underwater as well as above water.

--- SENTENCE 218 ---
When the sea level was lower during ice ages, a terrace or shelf was eroded by waves down to a lower level (Chamberlin, 1921; Davis, 1921; Daley, 1924).

--- SENTENCE 219 ---
The sea level was lower during the ice ages because water was removed from the oceans and locked up in the form of ice in gigantic ice sheets on land in Canada and northern Eurasia, much like the ice sheets on Antarctica and Greenland today.

--- SENTENCE 220 ---
Also, the water in the oceans was colder and thus contracted.

--- SENTENCE 221 ---
The ocean level was as much as 500 feet (150 m) lower during the last ice age, just 22,000 years ago.

--- SENTENCE 222 ---
The outer edge of the shelf is now about 330 feet (100 m) deep.

--- SENTENCE 223 ---
The shelf is composed of limestone, at least 825 feet (250 m) thick, so the original shelf cut into the basalt lava is now over 1155 feet (350

meters) deep.

--- SENTENCE 224 ---
The shelf would have had to have been cut long ago, then subsidence of the island would have carried it into deeper water as coral reefs grew on the shelf.

--- SENTENCE 225 ---
It seems logical to think that the shelf was built by coral reefs, but the layers in it look very solid indeed unlike most reef.

--- SENTENCE 226 ---
Perhaps lowered sea level actually carved the upper surface of the limestone shelf instead of the volcanic basalt below it.

--- SENTENCE 227 ---
The layer of limestone that is 825 plus feet thick is topped by a reef that forms sort of a broken ring around the island near the edge of the shelf.

--- SENTENCE 228 ---
This reef could be called a drowned barrier reef (Daley, 1924) or a bank barrier reef.

--- SENTENCE 229 ---
The island has subsided or sunk enough that the tops of the banks are now about 30 ft (10 m) or more below the sea surface.

--- SENTENCE 230 ---
On Tutuila, the only large area of flat land is the "Tafuna Plains" on the southwest side of the island.

--- SENTENCE 231 ---
This area is an area of relatively new and fluid lava flows, probably less than 10,000 years old (S. Hart, personal comm.).

--- SENTENCE 232 ---
Volcanic islands, such as those in Hawai'i, commonly produce a few smaller flows and cinder cones long after their main activity ceases, such as Diamond Head, Koko Head, Punchbowl and Hanauma Bay on Oahu, and the cinder cones in the crater at Haleakala on Maui.

--- SENTENCE 233 ---
In the Tafuna Plains, Fagatele Bay and Larson's Bay are recent calderas much like Hanauma Bay on Oahu.

--- SENTENCE 234 ---
These features are called post-erosional, since they were formed from activity long after the main mountain-building activity, and after considerable erosion of the mountain (Blay and Siemers, 2004).

--- SENTENCE 235 ---
Fagatele and Larson's are at the southern end of a line of calderas that run north-south from the crest of the mountains.

--- SENTENCE 236 ---
The calderas may be along a rift through which the lava flowed.

--- SENTENCE 237 ---
The area around the calderas is the highest on the Tafuna Plains, and the likely source of the lava in the plains.

--- SENTENCE 238 ---
The lava of the plains lies on top of the older coral reef limestone that forms the submarine shelf around Tutuila, and the plains reaches the outer edge of the limestone shelf.

--- SENTENCE 239 ---
Aunu'u also appears to be younger than the main island, since the caldera outline has not yet eroded away, and might be on a similar rift running south from the east end of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 240 ---
Aunu'u actually is two calderas, one taller and sharper looking and thus likely younger than the other.

--- SENTENCE 241 ---
The older, more northern caldera on Aunu'u which is lower and where the village is, has a wide reef flat that ends abruptly when the reef flat reaches the newer, southern, caldera.

--- SENTENCE 242 ---
So the southern caldera is newer, not very old because it has no reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 243 ---
Actually, there is now a report that some human artifacts in Nu'uuli were found underneath an ash layer (Addison et al 2006).

--- SENTENCE 244 ---
This shows that at least that ash erupted after humans started inhabiting the island.

--- SENTENCE 245 ---
The Tafuna plains also have no soil layers between rock layers indicated that there were no pauses in the eruptions long enough for soils to form (Addison, personal communication).

--- SENTENCE 246 ---
The pottery dating indicates that the Tafuna plains are most likely just about 1300 years old, so very recent.

--- SENTENCE 247 ---
The lavas of the Tafuna Plains cannot be dated directly because the radioactive isotope dating methods available for lava only work on much older lava.

--- SENTENCE 248 ---
But human artifacts like charcoal can be dated by the Carbon 14 dating method which works on much shorter time spans, perfect for this situation.

--- SENTENCE 249 ---
The volcanic islands of Samoa, like those of Hawai'i and all volcanoes within the Pacific Plate, are made of basalt, a dark rock that can form relatively fluid flows, and produces less explosive activity than other kinds of lava.

--- SENTENCE 250 ---
Hawai'i rarely has explosive events, and has some of the safest volcanoes in the world.

--- SENTENCE 251 ---
In Hawai'i, the islands formed are very broad and rounded, and are called "shield volcanoes" because of their low domelike shape that resembles a rounded shield.

--- SENTENCE 252 ---
The volcanoes of the Samoan chain are

likewise shield volcanoes, which can be seen when viewing Ta'u or Savai'i at a distance.

--- SENTENCE 253 ---
On Tutuila, the shield shape is less obvious due to erosion, but it is particularly obvious on Savai'i where the surface lavas are very young.

--- SENTENCE 254 ---
Outside the Pacific Plate, volcanoes are often formed in island arcs by the Pacific Plate going under another plate in a process called subduction.

--- SENTENCE 255 ---
The plate melts when it reaches great depths in the earth, and lighter parts of that molten rock rise and erupt as volcanoes.

--- SENTENCE 256 ---
The lava in these volcanoes is andesite, a rock with a higher silica content, which is stiffer and therefore often forms more steep-sided volcanoes with a greater frequency of explosive activity than basalt volcanoes within a plate.

--- SENTENCE 257 ---
Andesite was originally named after volcanoes in the Andes Mountains of South America.

--- SENTENCE 258 ---
In Darwin's time, a line called the "Andesite Line" was distinguished in the Western Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 259 ---
West of the line all volcanic islands were made of Andesite, east of it all were made of Basalt.

--- SENTENCE 260 ---
The Andesite Line is the western boundary of the Pacific Plate.

--- SENTENCE 261 ---
Examples of island arcs formed by subduction are the Tonga and the Mariana archipelagos (including Guam), an arc of islands in the eastern Caribbean, and the Aleutian Islands.

--- SENTENCE 262 ---
Indeed, just east of Tonga is the Tongan Trench, where the Pacific Plate is subducted (slides beneath) the Indian-Australian Plate, and pushes several hundred kilometers deep into the earth, and where some of the world's deepest earthquakes occur.

--- SENTENCE 263 ---
Where the plate is subducted below a continent or set of islands made of continental material, volcanoes occur on the continent or islands.

--- SENTENCE 264 ---
On a continent the volcanoes are interspersed with continental mountains.

--- SENTENCE 265 ---
This is the case in areas like the Andes of South America, the Cascades of the U.S. Pacific Northwest, the Philippines, Indonesia, and Japan.

--- SENTENCE 266 ---
Plate subduction is a process which produces frequent earthquakes, so these are generally areas of major earthquake activity and the potential for producing tsunamis such as the disastrous tsunami of Ache, Sumatra, Indonesia in 2005, and the earthquake in the Tongan Trench on the morning of September 29, 2009 that produced the tsunami that hit Tutuila and Upolu minutes later and caused so much damage, and then the big earthquake and tsunami in Japan.

--- SENTENCE 267 ---
The andesite volcanoes often form "stratovolcaoes" which are steep sided volcanoes made of alternating layers of stiff lava and pumice.

--- SENTENCE 268 ---
They typically form the beautiful (but dangerous if they erupt!)

--- SENTENCE 269 ---
shape of Mt. Fuji in Japan, Mayong Volcano in the Philippines, and the Cascade volcanoes in the US Northwest.

--- SENTENCE 270 ---
But they can also produce powerful eruptions that can blow the mountain to smithereens.

--- SENTENCE 271 ---
Crater Lake in Oregon was made that way, and ash from that eruption is all over the US west, sometimes in layers feet thick.

--- SENTENCE 272 ---
This happened about 3500 BC, so not long ago (as geology goes).

--- SENTENCE 273 ---
Yellowstone is a giant cladera which has had at least 3 near-apocalypic eruptions, and it has a LOT of hot rock under it (hence all the guysers, mud pots, and ponds at the boiling point.

--- SENTENCE 274 ---
When Yellowstone erupts again (not if!), it will be catastrophic for the US.

--- SENTENCE 275 ---
But that could be millions of years from now.

--- SENTENCE 276 ---
Surprisingly, the Tongan Trench is not stationary.

--- SENTENCE 277 ---
The place where the Pacific Plate is subducted, the Tongan Trench, is actually moving eastward, about twice as fast as the Pacific Plate is moving westward!

--- SENTENCE 278 ---
Thus, in the future (perhaps a few million years from now), eruptions should begin on Tutuila, and it will grow into a larger island like Upolu and Savai'i, with a smooth shield shape.

--- SENTENCE 279 ---
In fact, that may already have begun, in form of the Tafuna Plains (which are at the east end which might be affected first).

--- SENTENCE 280 ---
The fact that they were fluid lava flows argues against their being post-erosional eruptions, since those are usually or almost always cinders and pumice eruptions.

--- SENTENCE 281 ---
The chain of islands from Savai'i to Upolu to Tutuila lie in a row going roughly from west northwest to east southeast.

--- SENTENCE 282 ---
Then the small cluster of islands called Manu'a (Ofu, Olosega, and Ta'u) are just slightly north of straight east of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 283 ---
Savai'i, Upolu and Tutuila lie in a nearly straight line which extends as a submarine ridge southeast of Tutuila, and the Manu'a islands and the undersea volcano Vailulu'u form a second parallel nearly straight line, which also extends westward from Ofu.

--- SENTENCE 284 ---
Both curve southward at their east ends in parallel.

--- SENTENCE 285 ---
A similar situation occurs in Hawai'i, where there are currently two rows of volcanoes on the Big Island of Hawai'i – Kohala, Mauna Kea and the currently erupting vent are in one line, and parallel to that are Hualalai, Mauna Loa, and the undersea volcano Loihi.

--- SENTENCE 286 ---
Both lines appear to be curving southward in Hawaii as well as American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 287 ---
At Kaui, there were actually three parallel volcanoes, one each at Kaui, Niihau, and Kaula Island (Dalrymple et al. 1973).

--- SENTENCE 288 ---
The Mauna Kea chain extends to Maui and Moloka'i, while the Mauna Loa chain extends to Kaho'olawe and Lana'i.

--- SENTENCE 289 ---
Apparently the hotspot of molten lava can split into two on its way to the surface, or the lava may flow upward in parallel rifts in the plate.

--- SENTENCE 290 ---
Kiluea and the Chain of Craters, are on an east-west rift on the side of Mauna Loa, and do not appear to be along the two main rows of volcanoes on the Big Island of Hawai'i, but the newest eruption location is straight in line with Mauna Kea as shown in an amazing space image.

--- SENTENCE 291 ---
Eruptive activity is intermittent over the time span of millions of years, as separate large chambers of magma slowly rise, producing a row of separate volcanoes instead of a continuously erupting single elongated volcano (though Tutuila, Upolu and Savai'i are actually rows of craters and cinder cones close together, there are still gaps between the islands).

--- SENTENCE 292 ---
A map of the seafloor reveals that there are seamounts along the trend line of the Samoan island chain, built by the same volcanic activity but not reaching the surface.

--- SENTENCE 293 ---
The Tutuila ridge ends in the east southeast at a seamount named Southeast Bank, which is surely the youngest part of the Tutuila ridge.

--- SENTENCE 294 ---
The hotspot is currently active at the Vailulu'u seamount at the eastern end of the Manu'a ridge, but the eastern end of the Tutuila ridge does not appear to be active at Southeast Bank.

--- SENTENCE 295 ---
The island of Tutuila itself is oriented on a northeast to southwest line, out of alignment with the overall trend of the chain.

--- SENTENCE 296 ---
Wright et al. (2012) suggest this may be because it followed a rift in the plate going at that angle.

!

--- SENTENCE 297 ---
[](_page_15_Picture_0.jpeg)

Credit: Courtesy of the Hawaii Synergy project of the Hawaii Institute of Geophysics and Planetology, University of Hawaii at Manoa.

--- SENTENCE 298 ---
The production of two chains might come from faults in the plate.

--- SENTENCE 299 ---
But the faults don't parallel the fracture zones in the plate that are known, they go nearly east-west.

--- SENTENCE 300 ---
But there must be some mechanism for dividing the plume into two or even three nearby plumes.

--- SENTENCE 301 ---
Maybe the pressure from below of the buoyant hot lava pushes up a dome which cracks and these are the cracks?

--- SENTENCE 302 ---
Such cracks form around the tops of volcanoes such as in Hawaii, but they do not seem to be known in the plate.

--- SENTENCE 303 ---
Or maybe the multiple rows occur over parallel short offsets in the fracture zones that would be nearly north-south.

--- SENTENCE 304 ---
They surely are fed by the same ultimate deep magma source.

--- SENTENCE 305 ---
There doesn't appear to be evidence that the plate has changed direction, which could explain the row of volcanoes curving southward.

--- SENTENCE 306 ---
The change of direction could be accounted for by the continued movement of the plate as always plus a movement of the plume westward.

--- SENTENCE 307 ---
A new paper reported that the deep part of the plume is far to the west, maybe the lava conduits could be moving to the west as more plume rises to the west.

--- SENTENCE 308 ---
It might be some much more temporary effect, a wobble of the plume, or even that the two faults play a major role in determining the final location of the eruption, and the faults for some reason having to do with the plate are curving.

--- SENTENCE 309 ---
Kiluea volcano on the Big Island of Hawaii has rifts or cracks extending east and west from it; the eastern rift is the Chain of Craters, with the currently active vents well beyond that to the east, probably in the same fracture zone.

--- SENTENCE 310 ---
Rifts radiating from a caldera are common on volcanoes, and often there are three rifts produced as magma underneath the ground swells, forcing the mountain to crack and expand along rifts.

--- SENTENCE 311 ---
In the space image of the Big Island of Hawaii above, you can see three huge radiating ridges on Mauna Loa, these are likely the three rift zones.

--- SENTENCE 312 ---
The chain of craters running south to Fagatele and Larson's Bay in the western part of Tutuila, and the two craters of Aunu'u Island are probably from similar rifts, as pointed out above.

--- SENTENCE 313 ---
Most of the smaller islands of American Samoa have several ridges running down their submarine slopes that are likely to have been produced by rifts filling with lava that harden as dikes.

--- SENTENCE 314 ---
We already mentioned the big dike north of Vatia, which was likely a rift on the sides of the main caldera which then filled with lava from the eruption.

--- SENTENCE 315 ---
To the west of Savai'i, there are a number of seamounts, some of which (Pasco, Lalla Rootch, Waterwitch and Combe) appear to be in a continuation of the line of the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 316 ---
Others (Horseshoe and Field) may be a parallel chain to the north.

--- SENTENCE 317 ---
Three of the seamounts have been dated from rocks dredged from the seamounts, and for each of these the composition of the rocks matched that of the Samoan Archipelago, and the ages were progressively older to the west, and consistent with a plate motion of 7.3 cm per year.

--- SENTENCE 318 ---
The oldest seamount tested was 40 million years old, so the Samoan hotspot has been in existence for at least 40 million years.

--- SENTENCE 319 ---
They also report that the hotspot has not been totally still, but in fact has wobbled in a line that nearly forms a circle (Hart et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 320 ---
That's probably the origin of the curving of the lines of volcanoes east of Tutuila, and the curving southward of volcano lines on the Big Island of Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 321 ---
The small eastern island of Ta'u appears at a distance to be the rounded form of a relatively steep shield volcano.

--- SENTENCE 322 ---
It is actually the tallest mountain in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 323 ---
On the south side, there is a large concave feature.

--- SENTENCE 324 ---
Daley (1924) suggested three possible ways in which this feature may have been formed.

--- SENTENCE 325 ---
He says it could be a volcanic caldera (crater), or produced by a catastrophic collapse (slide), or by gradual slumping.

--- SENTENCE 326 ---
He points out that there are no ejecta scattered around on the upper surfaces of the mountain that remain that would indicate explosive activity.

--- SENTENCE 327 ---
Explosive activity is rare in Hawaii, but the shield shape of Ta'u is steeper than those of the Hawaiian volcanoes, suggesting more viscous lava and thus possibly a higher chance of explosive events.

--- SENTENCE 328 ---
The shape of Fagatele Bay, particularly the undersea portion, looks like an explosive event blasted a hole in the edge of the previously existing limestone shelf.

--- SENTENCE 329 ---
In addition, ejecta can be produced by non-exposive lava fountains such as those which have occurred in Hawaii from time to time, and can form cinder cones.

--- SENTENCE 330 ---
Blobs of lava shot into the air fall as pieces of various sizes with hardened exteriors.

--- SENTENCE 331 ---
Further, if the feature on Ta'u were a caldera, the southern half would be completely missing (same thing is true of the caldera that forms the harbor of Tutuila).

--- SENTENCE 332 ---
The existing feature has a bench in it, about half way down the escarpment.

--- SENTENCE 333 ---
Observation of the feature from a boat reveals that this bench is curved exactly parallel to the upper surface of the surviving mountain.

--- SENTENCE 334 ---
Thus, the bench is not a "bathtub ring" reflecting a level of a lava lake in the caldera, since if it was it would be level.

--- SENTENCE 335 ---
Instead, the bench appears to be a hard layer of the original volcano which resisted the process that formed the crescent.

--- SENTENCE 336 ---
And last, the shape of the crescent is closer to a parabola than to half of a circle or elipse.

--- SENTENCE 337 ---
A caldera is most likely to be nearly

circular, while some kind of slippage event is more likely to produce a more parablolashaped feature.

--- SENTENCE 338 ---
Further, the top of the mountain is not where the big chunk is out of the side.

--- SENTENCE 339 ---
It seems more likely that the top would be around the caldera, as it is in many volcanoes which retain a caldera (such as Aunu'u, the calderas in line with Fagatele, and the wall of the Tutuila harbor).

--- SENTENCE 340 ---
The caldera is what ejects the material that builds the mountain, and most of it lands close or flows downhill.

--- SENTENCE 341 ---
Lava could not flow uphill from this feature to the top of the mountain.

--- SENTENCE 342 ---
There are several smaller calderas in different places on Ta'u, including a fairly big one that the harbor and Ta'u village are located in.

--- SENTENCE 343 ---
Instead of being formed by eruptive events, the feature on the south side of Ta'u may be the result of a catastrophic collapse of the southern flank of this volcano.

--- SENTENCE 344 ---
Volcanoes are formed of relatively soft rocks which are often not well bonded together.

--- SENTENCE 345 ---
Further, layers of rock are commonly interspersed with layers of ash or pumice, which may be particularly soft.

--- SENTENCE 346 ---
On the Big Island of Hawai'i, as lava spills into the ocean it shatters into sand.

--- SENTENCE 347 ---
Subsequent lava builds on top of the sand until the sand gives way and the sequence starts over.

--- SENTENCE 348 ---
This suggests that some of the Hawaiian Islands may be partly built on volcanic sand that is very weak and unstable.

--- SENTENCE 349 ---
As a result, some volcanoes are not able to support their own weight for millions of years.

--- SENTENCE 350 ---
Weathering such as hydration and oxidation of the minerals can weaken the rock just as it does on land.

--- SENTENCE 351 ---
The north sides of Oahu and Molokai in Hawai'i both slid off into the ocean in catastrophic collapses that happened long ago.

--- SENTENCE 352 ---
Maps of the seafloor north of these two islands show huge lumps on the seafloor that were the northern parts of the islands that slid off.

--- SENTENCE 353 ---
Mt. Shasta in northern California has experienced similar collapse, and the pieces from that event form a series of hills spreading out from the volcano about 32 km (20 miles) to the north, and which Highway I-5 cuts through; the total volume of the slide was 6.5 cubic miles.

--- SENTENCE 354 ---
The fact that chunks travelled so far indicates that it wasn't a gradual slumping but rather a sudden catastrophic collapse, only very rapid motion could move those chunks so far.

--- SENTENCE 355 ---
The same is true for the north sides of Oahu and Molokai.

--- SENTENCE 356 ---
Mt. St. Helens had a similar but smaller slide in the 1980 eruption (Harris, 1988).

--- SENTENCE 357 ---
There have been many slumps and slides in the Hawaiian Islands, and there is evidence that some generated mega-tsunamis.

--- SENTENCE 358 ---
The volumes of rock that slid range up to about 10,000 km<sup>2</sup> for a slide on the south side of the big island of Hawaii, an incredible amount of rock.

--- SENTENCE 359 ---
There are deposits 72 m (238 feet) up on the south side of Molokai that are ascribed to a mega-tsunami, and deposits 326 m (1076 feet) up on Lanai that are ascribed to a mega-tsunami.

--- SENTENCE 360 ---
The scar left on the island where the rock slid away typically has benches and is roughly a parabolic shape (Whelan and Kelletat, 2003).

--- SENTENCE 361 ---
It may be that a similar collapse shaped the south side of Ta'u (Daley, 1924).

--- SENTENCE 362 ---
The scar on the south side of Ta'u has benches, and has a parabolic shape.

--- SENTENCE 363 ---
If the south side of Ta'u did slide off into the ocean, whether as a sudden catastrophic failure of the volcano or as a slow gradual slumping, the material that moved should be present on the seafloor near the volcano base.

--- SENTENCE 364 ---
On the underwater sloping side of Ta'u there is a large protruding feature southwest of the missing section of the south side of Ta'u, and another to the southeast.

--- SENTENCE 365 ---
These are much more likely to be a small eruptive feature from a rift on the side of Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 366 ---
Each of the islands and seamounts has a star-like shape, with the points of the stars being ridges going down the side of the mountain, which are surely produced by eruptive activity in the building of the mountain.

--- SENTENCE 367 ---
Subsea maps do not show obvious lumps at the base of the south side of Ta'u like off the north shores of Oahu and Molokai.

--- SENTENCE 368 ---
However, other slides on Hawaii have not left large

lumps on the seafloor.

--- SENTENCE 369 ---
The south side of Ta'u below the water line shows a large rounded valley going down the side below the concave feature above water.

--- SENTENCE 370 ---
Then down near the base of the mountain spreading out below the valley is a wide low mound.

--- SENTENCE 371 ---
Thus, it appears that the concave feature above water represents what is left when a large amount of material slid off into the water, gouging a large rounded valley going down the mountain slope, deposited as a large, wide, low mound on the seafloor spreading out from the base of the mountain.

--- SENTENCE 372 ---
This suggests that the collapse was rapid as it had the power to gouge out the valley and spread the material widely across the floor of the ocean.

--- SENTENCE 373 ---
The material would have had to disintegrate into a relatively fine material to leave no large chunks on the seafloor.

--- SENTENCE 374 ---
Gradual slumping can involve the disintegration of the rock into much smaller lumps which slide slowly.

--- SENTENCE 375 ---
Such a slumping process can be seen on the road between Alofau and Amouli and a larger slump on the way to Malota.

--- SENTENCE 376 ---
The outer part of the road simply starts to slowly slide down toward the sea.

--- SENTENCE 377 ---
So both landslides which are sudden and slumping occur on land, though landslides may be more common.

--- SENTENCE 378 ---
In any case, if the rock disintegrated into small pieces, it would spread out as it slid down and not leave large lumps on the sea floor.

--- SENTENCE 379 ---
If it happened slowly the material would be left at the bottom of the chute at the base of the mountain, piled up against the base of the mountain.

--- SENTENCE 380 ---
Only if it was a sudden catastrophic failure would the material have the momentum to flow away from the base of the mountain.

--- SENTENCE 381 ---
Ofu and Olosega form a pair of nearly joined islands, with a rather strange butterfly shape.

--- SENTENCE 382 ---
It is hard to see the shape of a volcano in either of these steep islands, and no caldera is apparent above water.

--- SENTENCE 383 ---
These two islands may be the remains of a single volcano, which subsequently experienced two catastrophic slides, one on the north side and one on the south, cutting into the volcanic cone and leaving a pair of butterfly-shaped islands (Daley, 1924).

--- SENTENCE 384 ---
The curvature of the south shore of Ofu-Olosega is almost identical to the south shore of Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 385 ---
The east side of Olosega appears to be the intact lower slope of a larger single volcano that the two islands were formed from.

--- SENTENCE 386 ---
Daley (1924) reports that the streambeds on that eastern slope continue right up to the summit edge, and have rounded stones in them up at that edge, even though the streams do not flow that high on the slope.

--- SENTENCE 387 ---
Rounded stones are formed by flowing streams, so this indicates that originally there was higher slope above the present highest level.

--- SENTENCE 388 ---
Daley (1924) also considers the western slope of Ofu to fit with the original volcano shape.

--- SENTENCE 389 ---
At the east end of Ofu, just before the small bridge that crosses the shallow reef waters between Ofu and Olosega, there is a sharp pinnacle, that appears to be some kind of volcanic plug left after the two giant slides.

--- SENTENCE 390 ---
The catastrophic collapse of the north sides of Oahu and Molokai would have been incredibly huge events, and have produced gigantic tsunamis that would have swept northward in the Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 391 ---
The collapse of the south side of Ta'u would likewise have produced a giant tsunami, as would Ofu-Olosega if it collapsed.

--- SENTENCE 392 ---
There is an unconfirmed report of a limestone boulder at 400 m elevation on Tutuila which might have been thrown that high by the Ta'u or Ofu-Olosega tsunamis (S. Hart, personal comm.).

--- SENTENCE 393 ---
But it is also possible that the two slides on Ofu-Olosega occurred not as sudden catastrophic slides, but rather as a gradual slumping process.

--- SENTENCE 394 ---
The steep slopes of Tutuila were produced by erosion from the heavy rainfall that the island experiences.

--- SENTENCE 395 ---
Tutuila is more eroded than volcanoes of a similar age in the Hawaiian chain, such as the Kohala Mountains on the Big Island.

--- SENTENCE 396 ---
This is because the Kohala Mountains do not experience as much rain as American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 397 ---
The valleys in

Tutuila are all V-shaped, very typical of stream erosion (glaciers produce rounded valleys).

--- SENTENCE 398 ---
Also, there are no ampitheater-headed valleys on Tutuila such as those that are typical in parts of Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 399 ---
There are, however, several ampitheater-headed stream valleys on the steep northern shore of Ta'u that can most easily be seen in areal photographs or from a boat offshore.

--- SENTENCE 400 ---
It may be that this shape valley is produced by just the right combination of a steep slope and soft material.

--- SENTENCE 401 ---
At Tutuila, the erosion process has gone a lot further than in the Manu'a Islands, because of Tutuila's greater age, so Tutuila has many deep V shaped stream valleys, while Ta'u is still very smooth and rounded.

--- SENTENCE 402 ---
Erosion by rainwater runoff and surf has removed much of the original island of Tutuila, and is in the very slow process of removing all of the remaining island.

--- SENTENCE 403 ---
Oxygen in the air oxidizes rock that is exposed, and water hydrates the minerals.

--- SENTENCE 404 ---
Like many chemical reactions, these processes occur more rapidly at high temperatures than at low temperatures, and so the warm year-round temperatures speed these processes in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 405 ---
These two processes cause the slow breakup and decomposition of rock into loose rocks, gravel, sand, and silts.

--- SENTENCE 406 ---
Plant roots help break up rocks and decomposing plants mixed with sand and silts form soil.

--- SENTENCE 407 ---
Silts and soil are particularly easily eroded by rainwater, and this happens particularly rapidly when the silts and soils are exposed.

--- SENTENCE 408 ---
The moist warm conditions support rapid plant growth, and cleared areas that are abandoned are recovered with vegetation relatively quickly.

--- SENTENCE 409 ---
In places on steep mountain sides, the loose rocks and soil give way, and in a landslide carry the vegetation down the steep slope with them.

--- SENTENCE 410 ---
Usually the shape of the landslide is a narrow vertical band that forms a brown streak on a slope that is otherwise covered with forest.

--- SENTENCE 411 ---
This happens periodically in or after heavy rainstorms on naturally vegetated steep slopes, illustrating the active process of natural erosion.

--- SENTENCE 412 ---
On October 28, 1979, there were 70 landslides on the west end of Tutuila, most likely due to heavy rain in the previous days acting on slopes that were steepened by erosion at their bases and road construction (Buchanan-Banks, 1980).

--- SENTENCE 413 ---
When it rains heavily, many streams turn very muddy, and the process of erosion becomes very obvious.

--- SENTENCE 414 ---
Poor land practices have no doubt increased the amount of mud carried by some streams and the increased mud runoff may damage reefs, though most reefs (outside the harbor) continue to look pretty healthy.

--- SENTENCE 415 ---
The active erosion process in the streams can also be seen in the small deltas that the streams produce.

--- SENTENCE 416 ---
At the mouth of each stream there is a low pile of black volcanic (basalt) rocks and sand, carried down by the stream and deposited on top of the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 417 ---
The magnitude of the erosion over the life of the island can be appreciated by realizing that the cockscomb at Vatia is a dike that formed in a crack in the original volcano, so the island originally extended as far as the dike.

--- SENTENCE 418 ---
The dike is already eroded, so originally it extended farther than it does today.

--- SENTENCE 419 ---
The original island probably extended to near the edge of the shelf, or an average of about 1 mile beyond present shorelines.

--- SENTENCE 420 ---
All that material that was eroded away passed out of streams into the water over the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 421 ---
Cubic miles of material came out the streams over the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 422 ---
But the reefs survived and flourished, because that happened over such a long time period, 1.5 million years, that it rarely was intense enough to cause much damage to the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 423 ---
The ocean also produces rapid erosion of shorelines in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 424 ---
Around the islands of American Samoa, there is a zone just above the water surface that has no plants growing on it, because salt spray keeps them from growing.

--- SENTENCE 425 ---
The volcanic layers are especially easy to see here from a boat, particularly on the north side of the island.

--- SENTENCE 426 ---
From

a distance it is easy to see on Ta'u that there are cliffs at the water's edge.

--- SENTENCE 427 ---
These cliffs are produced by erosion by the sea.

--- SENTENCE 428 ---
The surf pounds the shores of the Samoan archipelago, located near the center of an ocean that covers nearly half of our planet.

--- SENTENCE 429 ---
There are no land masses around it to protect it from waves coming from any direction.

--- SENTENCE 430 ---
That large ocean provides the space and wind required to produce large waves.

--- SENTENCE 431 ---
In order to produce large waves, there must be strong wind blowing steadily for many hundreds of miles.

--- SENTENCE 432 ---
Along the shoreline of sheer volcanic rock at the edge of the Tafuna Plains, crashing waves send spray up 2-3 stories in the air on a normal day.

--- SENTENCE 433 ---
If you look toward the sun when it is low along the coast in areas where the waves are much smaller, you can see clouds of fine salt spray blowing inland from the waves.

--- SENTENCE 434 ---
Those waves ceaselessly erode the shores of the volcanoes over the eons.

--- SENTENCE 435 ---
Several cement "pillbox" gun emplacements were built along the southern shoreline of Tutuila during World War II.

--- SENTENCE 436 ---
Some of these are now separated from the shore rocks by a gap, due to the shoreline erosion in that time, and/or the waves washing the pillboxes away from the shore.

--- SENTENCE 437 ---
The north side of Tutuila has cliffs along much of the shoreline, more than on the south shore, which is why a road could be built along the south shore but not the north shore.

--- SENTENCE 438 ---
This indicates greater surf erosion on the north shore than the south.

--- SENTENCE 439 ---
From about May to November, winds and waves steadly come from the Southeast, but during the November to May period, wind and wave direction is much more variable.

--- SENTENCE 440 ---
It is during this period that monsoon troughs and hurricanes bring the strongest wind and waves.

--- SENTENCE 441 ---
It is possible that hurricanes strike the north shore more often than the south, eroding cliffs, but most likely hurricanes pass along the south side as often as the north side.

--- SENTENCE 442 ---
But the south side is protected by fringing reefs which absorb much of the power of the waves and reduce their ability to erode the shoreline.

--- SENTENCE 443 ---
On the north shore, reefs are restricted to bays, and so do not protect the shoreline outside bays from rapid erosion, which results in cliffs.

--- SENTENCE 444 ---
In Hawaii, reef growth is restricted to bays, because storm waves rip the corals off the bottom outside of bays (Grigg, 1983; 1988).

--- SENTENCE 445 ---
On the north side of Tutuila there are no reef flats outside of bays to break the power of the waves, and underwater small or encrusting corals grow directly on the volcanic basalt rock in some places outside of bays, while there are mounds of reef in other areas.

--- SENTENCE 446 ---
So it may be that the waves of hurricanes and other storms do strike the north shore harder than the south.

--- SENTENCE 447 ---
Hurricanes hit the island about once in five years on the average, and so over the course of the 1.5 million year life of the island, about 300,000 hurricanes have hit it.

--- SENTENCE 448 ---
They are natural, and the existing reefs have survived them, so they are not a long-term threat to the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 449 ---
They do damage reefs, but then the reefs recover, and reefs go through an endless process of damage and recovery that is natural.

--- SENTENCE 450 ---
Climate change, however, is predicted to increase the power of hurricanes, which would likely impact reefs.

--- SENTENCE 451 ---
Interestingly, the north shore of Upolu has well developed reefs with reef flats that extend far from land.

--- SENTENCE 452 ---
Further, Ofu and Olosega have reef flats on the north side, and well developed reefs on the slopes in places like Sili on Olosega.

--- SENTENCE 453 ---
The north shore of Ta'u has some reef buildup on the slope even though it is the youngest island in the archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 454 ---
So if waves rip the corals off the north reef slopes of Tutuila, leaving the shoreline exposed to erosion from waves, then something makes

that happen more on Tutuila than either Upolu or Ofu-Olosega.

--- SENTENCE 461 ---
The cause of this pattern remains a mystery.

--- SENTENCE 462 ---
For further information on the geology of the Samoan islands and coral reefs, see Davis (1921), Daley (1924), McDougall (1985), Hart et al. (2004), Jackson et al (2007), Birkeland et al. (2008) and Koppers et al. (2008).

--- SENTENCE 463 ---
For other introductions to the coral reefs of American Samoa, see Wells, (1988), Craig (2005), Craig et al. (2005), Birkeland et al. (2008), Brainard et al. (2008) and Fenner et al. (2008).

### **The Coral Reefs of the Samoan Archipelago**

 There are three major kinds of coral reefs, called fringing reefs, barrier reefs, and atolls.

--- SENTENCE 468 ---
Fringing reefs grow right along the shoreline.

--- SENTENCE 469 ---
Nearly all the reefs of the Samoan archipelago are fringing reefs.

--- SENTENCE 470 ---
Barrier reefs are separated from the shoreline by a lagoon.

--- SENTENCE 471 ---
A lagoon is around 20-150 feet deep and usually has a sandy bottom.

--- SENTENCE 472 ---
It is much shallower than the open ocean, which is usually about 3 to 4 miles (4-5 km) deep.

--- SENTENCE 473 ---
An atoll is a ring of coral in the open ocean with no high island.

--- SENTENCE 474 ---
The atoll may have small

low sandy islands scattered along the ring of coral.

--- SENTENCE 475 ---
Rose Atoll in American Samoa has only two tiny islands (one made only of sand, and the other is sand with vegetation indicated it has been there longer), but Swains Island is a continuous ring of sand with a completely enclosed lagoon or salt lake in the center.

--- SENTENCE 476 ---
At one time it had a small opening to the ocean, but that was closed by a storm during the lifetimes of older residents.

--- SENTENCE 477 ---
Charles Darwin in 1851 proposed that the three major types of reefs formed a sequence that was produced by oceanic volcanoes subsiding.

--- SENTENCE 478 ---
He proposed that first a volcano is built up, and then a fringing reef grows up around the shoreline of the volcanic island.

--- SENTENCE 479 ---
He said that the volcano will then very slowly sink, and the reef will grow upward where it began growing.

--- SENTENCE 480 ---
As the volcano sinks, a lagoon will form between the island and the reef.

--- SENTENCE 481 ---
This can be seen most clearly on an island like Moorea in French Polynesia, where a lagoon separates the high volcanic island from the reef.

--- SENTENCE 482 ---
The reef marks the outline of the original shoreline, and where streams came down to the shore, the sediment carried by the stream kept the reef from growing and passes in the reef resulted.

--- SENTENCE 483 ---
Darwin said that eventually the island will sink out if sight in the center of the ring of reef, forming an atoll.

--- SENTENCE 484 ---
The entire sequence can be seen in Hawai'i, with an active volcano on the Big Island in the southeast, fringing reefs on the larger high islands which are relatively young, a couple of small barrier reefs on Oahu and Kaui, then tiny high islands with barrier reefs around them in the Northwest Hawaiian Islands, followed by atolls to the northwest.

--- SENTENCE 485 ---
The island ages fit this sequence, increasing in age to the northwest, as the original Hawaiians knew.

--- SENTENCE 486 ---
The sequence can also be seen in the Tahitian chain.

--- SENTENCE 487 ---
The Samoan chain only has the early stages with fringing reefs, because the island chain is relatively short and young.

--- SENTENCE 488 ---
The reef flat at Ta'u is missing around much of the island and narrow where it does exit, as Ta'u is the youngest island.

--- SENTENCE 489 ---
Reef flats are wider at Ofu and Tutuila and quite wide on Upolu.

--- SENTENCE 490 ---
There is another feature that is not so easy to see, a shelf around some of the islands, which is about 30-100 m (100- 300 feet) deep.

--- SENTENCE 491 ---
Around Tutuila it has a ring of interrupted reefs which appear to be a sunken barrier reef.

--- SENTENCE 492 ---
The shelf around Ta'u is very narrow if it exists at all, it is wider at Ofu-Olosega, much wider on Tutuila, and widest around Upolu and between Upolu and Savai'i consistent with Darwin's proposal.

--- SENTENCE 493 ---
Darwin's theory was tested by drilling to see what was under the coral in an atoll.

--- SENTENCE 494 ---
His theory predicted that volcanic rock would be underneath the coral, and indeed when holes were finally drilled through an atoll (Enewetok in the Marshall Is.)

--- SENTENCE 495 ---
long after Darwin passed away, volcanic rock was found at a depth of 1200 meters (about ¾ mile), and the coral just above the volcanic rock was 65 million years old.

--- SENTENCE 496 ---
Reef-building corals cannot live deeper than about 100 m (330 feet) at the very most, and most live in water shallower than about 50 m (160 feet), so the reefs could not possibly have grown up from the top of a volcano 1200 meters deep, instead the volcano had to have subsided after the reef started growing.

--- SENTENCE 497 ---
Plus there are remains deep in the drill hole that indicate that the rock there was exposed to air, such as plant debris.

--- SENTENCE 498 ---
None of the seamounts in the Samoan chain west of Savai'i reached close enough to the sea surface to have coral reefs grow on them, so none of them formed atolls, although they are old enough.

--- SENTENCE 499 ---
Waves and wave surge have had an impact on the reefs as well as on the shoreline.

--- SENTENCE 500 ---
Reef development is generally greater within bays and less on points on the north side of the island (NOAA, 2005; Sabater and Tofaeono, 2006).

--- SENTENCE 501 ---
Although corals grow on the points, hurricane wave surge probably often breaks and removes the corals, so that the

coral skeletons don't have a chance to build a reef.

--- SENTENCE 502 ---
On the south side, reefs grow on points as well as bays, probably because exposure to hurricane waves is not as intense.

--- SENTENCE 503 ---
Reef growth is also restricted to bays in Hawai'i for the same reason (Grigg, 1983; 1988).

--- SENTENCE 504 ---
In a few bays on Tutuila, such as the harbor on the south side and Vatia and Fagasa on the north side, wave action and circulation within the bays are so restricted that sediment runoff from the land is sufficient to affect the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 505 ---
This is most true in the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 506 ---
The harbor is huge and deep-

--- SENTENCE 507 ---
it is the finest harbor in the South Pacific, and the main reason that the US wanted these islands (it was a Navy coaling station from early in the 20th century up to the 1950's, but no longer).

--- SENTENCE 508 ---
The harbor is so large the Queen Elizabeth II ocean liner is dwarfed by the harbor when it visits, and it appears you could anchor several aircraft carriers in it comfortably.

--- SENTENCE 509 ---
Some reefs in the harbor have been dredged, some built over, and others are heavily sedimented and all were exposed to heavy nutrient discharge in the early years of the tuna canneries (that discharge has been redirected out of the harbor).

--- SENTENCE 510 ---
Most of the coral reefs present in the harbor at the turn of the century no longer exist or no longer have coral on them, but good coral communities still exist near the mouth of the harbor on reef flats at Utelei and Onesesopo.

--- SENTENCE 511 ---
The reef flat at Aua on the north shore of the harbor was surveyed in 1916, and there was coral most of the way out to the edge of the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 512 ---
Subsequently, coral cover declined, and now it is all coral rubble except at the very outer edge, where there is good coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 513 ---
The reef slopes have very little sand on them, but the shelf at 30-100 m deep has lots of sand (in some areas of the southwest coast of Tutuila west of Leone, the shelf is as little as 10 m deep).

--- SENTENCE 514 ---
Coral reefs usually generate lots of sand.

--- SENTENCE 515 ---
The coral and algae produce the white carbonate rock, and then a variety of organisms grind it into sand in a process called "bioerosion."

--- SENTENCE 516 ---
Parrotfish bite at algae that grow on the reef rock surfaces, and in the process the larger species of parrotfish also scrape off some of the rock.

--- SENTENCE 517 ---
They have a grinding apparatus in their throat that grinds the bits of rock they injest into sand, and then later after they have digested the algae they defecate sand.

--- SENTENCE 518 ---
Small parrotfish scrape off very little rock with the algae they eat, but the larger parrotfish which scrape off much more rock are called "excavators."

--- SENTENCE 519 ---
The largest parrotfish (bumphead parrotfish) actually bite off pieces of live coral, crush them and eat them, as well as scraping algae off of dead surfaces.

--- SENTENCE 520 ---
Sea urchins also grind off reef rock as they scrape off algae with their hard teeth.

--- SENTENCE 521 ---
Some clams bore into corals and live embedded inside the live coral.

--- SENTENCE 522 ---
Also, some sponges bore into the reef rock, etching tiny chips of limestone out, and expelling the tiny chips.

--- SENTENCE 523 ---
Where boring sponges are common, like in the Florida Keys, the bottom is dusted with the fine dust of these tiny chips from boring sponges.

--- SENTENCE 524 ---
Although small parrotfish are common on American Samoan reefs, the larger parrots are not, so parrotfish actually do relatively little bioerosion here.

--- SENTENCE 525 ---
Larger urchins are also rare, and the author has yet to see any boring sponges.

--- SENTENCE 526 ---
The reefs here have none of that dusting of fine white silt from boring spoinges.

--- SENTENCE 527 ---
On many reefs, sand is common on reef slopes, accumulating in dips and grooves.

--- SENTENCE 528 ---
In American Samoa there are beaches made of sand from the reef and pools with sandy bottoms, but not a lot of sand on many reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 529 ---
The likely reason is that the surge from heavy waves continually re-suspends small particles, and moves larger particles down the slope.

--- SENTENCE 530 ---
The sand gets cleaned off the slopes, and deposited on the shelf below.

--- SENTENCE 531 ---
Although the water on the reef slopes is much clearer than in the harbor, and can average around 75 feet visibility, it is not as clear as out at the banks on the shelf, where it is well over 100 feet.

--- SENTENCE 532 ---
Coral reefs around the Samoan islands have several zones.

--- SENTENCE 533 ---
The fringing reefs of Tutuila and Ofu-Olosega have very shallow reef flats that begin at the shoreline.

--- SENTENCE 534 ---
Either black volcanic rock (basalt) or white coral sand beaches form the shoreline, but at low tide line the beach ends and the reef flat begins.

--- SENTENCE 535 ---
The reef flat is built by coral and crustose coralline algae.

--- SENTENCE 536 ---
Crustose coralline algae is a plant which produces thin sheets of pink or purple which have a high calcium content, which cements rubble together and adds to the rock of the reef.

--- SENTENCE 537 ---
It slowly builds solid white carbonate rock underneath it.

--- SENTENCE 538 ---
Corals and coralline algae grow upward until they reach the surface.

--- SENTENCE 539 ---
Periodically, unusually low tides bring the water surface a few inches below the normal low tide line, and leave the living corals and algae exposed to air.

--- SENTENCE 540 ---
If this happens mid-day on a sunny day, they are also exposed to high temperatures.

--- SENTENCE 541 ---
The drying and high temperatures kill them, but corals and coralline algae that are low enough to be covered with water survive.

--- SENTENCE 542 ---
The net effect is like mowing grass: the upper tips die but the rest survives.

--- SENTENCE 543 ---
The result is that coral in depressions continues to grow, while coral that has grown high gets mowed flat at the low tide line.

--- SENTENCE 544 ---
Depressions get filled in until the entire reef flat becomes very flat at the low tide line.

--- SENTENCE 545 ---
This is how the reef flat is formed and why it is so flat.

--- SENTENCE 546 ---
The most common corals on the reef flats are encrusting *Montipora*, the small bushy staghorn, *Acropora aspera*, and *Pavona frondifera*.

--- SENTENCE 547 ---
There are very few visible algae (called macroalgae or frondose algae) on the reef flats, other than the crustose coralline algae.

--- SENTENCE 548 ---
Waves breaking on the reef crest pump water onto the reef flat, which then flows over the reef flat into cracks in the reef called "avas," where it drains back out into the ocean.

--- SENTENCE 549 ---
The waves on the reef flats quickly move sand inward from the crest, where it accumulates in low spots near the shore.

--- SENTENCE 550 ---
So there is little or no sand out near the crest, but some sand near the shore.

--- SENTENCE 551 ---
An ava is usually a relatively small cleft that is wider and deeper at the reef crest and narrows and becomes shallower as it runs into the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 552 ---
Typically the sides are near vertical, and the bottom is often covered with fairly large rounded carbonate rock reef rubble.

--- SENTENCE 553 ---
One of the larger avas is at Fagaalu, where a fairly large stream runs into the reef.

--- SENTENCE 554 ---
Avas may have been formed when the ocean level was much lower (as low as 500 feet below the present level) by the stream eroding the exposed dead reef rock.

--- SENTENCE 555 ---
However, once the ava was covered by sea water, coral growth could rapidly fill in such a gap.

--- SENTENCE 556 ---
Although fresh water kills coral, the fresh water in the stream floats on the surface which is now well above the coral, so fresh water from the stream could not keep the coral on the bottom from growing and filling in the ava.

--- SENTENCE 557 ---
Sediment is carried out some streams during rain storms, and currents in the ava carry the sediment out the ava.

--- SENTENCE 558 ---
For streams that release moderate amounts of sediment, the sediment is swept rapidly out the ava and does not settle on the bottom.

--- SENTENCE 559 ---
The fine silt fraction stays suspended in the fresh water floating at the surface for some time, long enough to be carried well out to sea and for the fresh water to spread over a very large area so the sediment is extremely dilute as it settles.

--- SENTENCE 560 ---
If a stream releases very large quantities of sediment, as in Faga'alu, sediment does settle within the bay, and can kill coral.

--- SENTENCE 561 ---
The sediment there not only settles in the ava, but also on the north side of the bay on the reef flat, where it has killed most of coral that lived there.

--- SENTENCE 562 ---
This appears to be a relatively recent event, due to human activities on land such as a quarry uphill in the valley.

--- SENTENCE 563 ---
But the spatial pattern of sediment settlement is not the same as the ava, suggesting that the sediment is not the primary cause of the ava staying open.

--- SENTENCE 564 ---
In most avas (and on reef slopes), strong wave surge also keeps any sediment that settles

resuspended, so it doesn't smother the coral.

--- SENTENCE 565 ---
