The primary factor keeping avas open may be the rubble on the floor of the ava.

--- SENTENCE 566 ---
The rubble is rounded, though it is clearly made of coral rock.

--- SENTENCE 567 ---
Thus the sharp edges of the coral have been ground away.

--- SENTENCE 568 ---
Heavy wave action during storms moves the rubble back and forth, grinding off sharp edges, breaking off any live corals that may have attached, and grinding away at the reef rock under the rubble.

--- SENTENCE 569 ---
This process was seen and photographed by the author in the Philippines, where corals were found which were unattached and had the bumps worn down (and with no living tissue on them) while concave areas still had living coral tissue.

--- SENTENCE 570 ---
They were found in smooth gullies with rounded sides.

--- SENTENCE 571 ---
Clearly storms had moved the corals back and forth, wearing away bumps on them and wearing a smooth gully in the reef.

--- SENTENCE 572 ---
Thus, the main process keeping avas open is the same process that produces the narrow smooth grooves running down the upper reef slope, which have similar rounded rubble on their floors.

--- SENTENCE 573 ---
They also have smooth floors with a smooth rounded junction between the floor of the groove and the side, all consistent with being produced by the grinding action of boulders moved by storm waves.

--- SENTENCE 574 ---
The rubble on the bottom of avas likely are pieces of corals ripped off the reef by storms, and carried into the avas by the strong currents that flow off of the reef flat and into the ava and then out to sea.

--- SENTENCE 575 ---
During storms, those currents are even stronger than normal, and can move loose rubble blocks.

--- SENTENCE 576 ---
There may also be some of the basalt blocks washed off the reef flat and into the ava.

--- SENTENCE 577 ---
The grinding action in the avas produces sand, which the currents then carry out of the ava onto the shelf.

--- SENTENCE 578 ---
A few avas have a fair number of healthy looking corals on the bottom of the ava and may be in the process of starting to fill in.

--- SENTENCE 579 ---
The currents on reef flats are stronger at high tide and when waves are larger.

--- SENTENCE 580 ---
They can be quite strong even in non-storm conditions, and in avas the water usually runs outward very fast.

--- SENTENCE 581 ---
It is easy to be carried out an ava by the current, but very hard to get back in, making avas very dangerous even to a strong snorkeler with fins.

--- SENTENCE 582 ---
That same current pattern keeps fresh oceanic water flowing over the reef flat and keeps the reef flat clean of terrestrial sediments.

--- SENTENCE 583 ---
In some areas, the reef flats have many conspicuous large black volcanic basalt boulders sitting on top of them that stick out at medium-low tides.

--- SENTENCE 584 ---
These are pieces of basalt which have been eroded away from the shoreline.

--- SENTENCE 585 ---
Most of that erosion is likely to occur during hurricanes, when storm surge due to the low atmospheric pressure raises the water level, allowing the huge waves to sweep across the reef flat and hit the shoreline.

--- SENTENCE 586 ---
Some of the boulders are up to about 3 meters (10 feet) tall, and weigh many tons, plus they can be up to about 30 meters (100 feet) from shore.

--- SENTENCE 587 ---
The waves required to move such boulders must be huge indeed.

--- SENTENCE 588 ---
Hurricane waves of 10 meters (30 feet) have been documented pounding reefs (e.g., Woodley et al. 1981); waves of 20 meters (60 feet) have been documented in the eye of the most powerful hurricanes (e.g., Fenner, 1991).

--- SENTENCE 589 ---
Basalt rocks similar to those on the reef flat may be imbedded in the reef limestone where they may get washed over the crest or out the ava and stop moving when they reach flatter bottom.

--- SENTENCE 590 ---
If the reef flat grows upward with rising sea level, those on the reef flat could get incorporated into the upward growing reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 591 ---
The streams also carry much larger material than the suspended fine silt that colors the water brown.

--- SENTENCE 592 ---
They carry rocks and gravel made from the erosion of the volcanic basalt.

--- SENTENCE 593 ---
The larger the piece of rock, the faster the current must be to move it.

--- SENTENCE 594 ---
Thus, at stream mouths as the speed of the moving water decreases as the water moves into the ocean water, the largest rocks stop moving first, then smaller rocks, gravel, and finally

sand.

--- SENTENCE 595 ---
The silt is the microscopic particles that are most easily carried by the water and take the longest time to settle out, and so are carried farthest from the stream mouth.

--- SENTENCE 596 ---
The effect of all this is that the streams that carry the most water and sediment usually have a small river delta at their mouth, composed of black basalt rocks rocks and gravel.

--- SENTENCE 597 ---
The delta is deposited on the reef flat at the stream mouth, and over time the waves move the rocks and gravel around some, in between rainstorms when more gravel is carried down and deposited.

--- SENTENCE 598 ---
Compared to the size of the whole reef flats, the stream deltas are relatively small.

--- SENTENCE 599 ---
So while the gravel delta covers the solid reef flat and no corals or algae can live under it, the delta occupies a relatively small area.

--- SENTENCE 600 ---
But it is another sign of the active process of erosion of the island.

--- SENTENCE 601 ---
Along some of our shores, there is a band of relatively smooth grey rock that parallels the shoreline.

--- SENTENCE 602 ---
This is called "beachrock."

--- SENTENCE 603 ---
It is located between the high and low tide lines.

--- SENTENCE 604 ---
It is made of calcium carbonate, so it is a light color like coral, not black like volcanic basalt.

--- SENTENCE 605 ---
In places you can see corals embedded in it.

--- SENTENCE 606 ---
It appears that some chemical process cements the beach sand together into rock, and any coral skeletons in the sand get embedded in it.

--- SENTENCE 607 ---
It may be that at low tide, evaporation makes calcium carbonate in the salty moisture precipitate, cementing the sand together.

--- SENTENCE 608 ---
The reef flats vary greatly in width around the islands.

--- SENTENCE 609 ---
On the south side of Tutuila, the reef flat varies from zero at the Tafuna Plains to about 500 m wide in Fagaitua Bay, measured from the reef crest to the shore.

--- SENTENCE 610 ---
Most of Tutuila's south side reef flats are about 200-400 m wide.

--- SENTENCE 611 ---
Reef flats on the north side of Tutuila are mostly 100-300 m wide, with the maximum at Aoa at about 400 m wide.

--- SENTENCE 612 ---
Most of the north coast has no reef flat at all, and all the reef flats are within bays.

--- SENTENCE 613 ---
On Ofu-Olosega, reef flats are generally about 50-300 m wide, with the average being about 250 m, and the widest 500 m at Ofu village.

--- SENTENCE 614 ---
On Ta'u, much of the coast has no reef flat, but where reef flats exist, they average about 50 m wide with the widest about 150 m wide (NOAA, 2005).

--- SENTENCE 615 ---
In some places around Tutuila there are some deeper spots in the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 616 ---
These are called back reef pools.

--- SENTENCE 617 ---
There are such pools on the outside of the airport runway, and at Coconut Point in Nuuuli, and in Alofau, and Faga'alu, and Utelei.

--- SENTENCE 618 ---
These pools are up to about 20 feet deep, and have corals as well as sandy areas.

--- SENTENCE 619 ---
The airport pool was dredged out of reef flat to get material to build the base of the airport runway.

--- SENTENCE 620 ---
The pools in Alofau and Coconut Point, Nuuuli were dredged for material to add to the village land, and this may also be the cause of the pools at Faga'alu and Utelei.

--- SENTENCE 621 ---
At Fagaitua and Onesosopo there are pools that are shallower, yet deeper than the reef flat, and appear to be natural.

--- SENTENCE 622 ---
At Fagaitua there are dips as much as about 6 feet (2 m) deep, and at Onesosopo much of the pool area is about 2-3 feet (0.6-1 m) deep, but it is more like 1.5m deep closer to the little boat ramp.

--- SENTENCE 623 ---
Utelei and Fagaalu have areas that are about 1- 15 feet (0.3 – 5 m) deep with corals.

--- SENTENCE 624 ---
In independent Samoa, there are many places with wide sandy natural areas about 2-6 feet (.6-2 m) deep.

--- SENTENCE 625 ---
These areas are sometimes called "moats."

--- SENTENCE 626 ---
They are most common when a fringing reef is particularly wide, or the reef crest is high enough to block the waves at high tide.

--- SENTENCE 627 ---
In independent Samoa, it is likely because the distance from the crest to the shore is so great.

--- SENTENCE 628 ---
Most of these backreef pools around Tutuila are dominated by staghorns and finger coral (*Porites cylindrica*).

--- SENTENCE 629 ---
One staghorn with blue branch tips (*Acropora muricata,* formerly called *A. formosa*) is by far the most common of the three staghorn corals in the lagoons, the other two being

!

--- SENTENCE 630 ---
[](_page_28_Picture_0.jpeg)

A reef flat on Tutuila

!

--- SENTENCE 631 ---
The smooth grey in the middle is beachrock

!

--- SENTENCE 632 ---
Shoreline erosion at Faga'itua

!

--- SENTENCE 633 ---
[](_page_28_Picture_6.jpeg)

Basalt blocks on top of a reef flat

!

--- SENTENCE 634 ---
A stream delta at Amaluia

!

--- SENTENCE 635 ---
[](_page_28_Picture_10.jpeg)

Muddy water going out a stream and ava

*Acropora pulchra* and *A. intermedia*

--- SENTENCE 636 ---
The moats in Upolu and Savai'i of independent Samoa are also often dominated by *A. muricata*.

--- SENTENCE 637 ---
On the south shore of Ofu, there are a series of backreef pools that appear to be totally natural.

--- SENTENCE 638 ---
These pools or moats are about 1-10 feet (0.3-3 m) deep.

--- SENTENCE 639 ---
The way they were formed is a bit of a puzzle.

--- SENTENCE 640 ---
The central pool in the series, at Hurricane House, is the largest and deepest, and others vary in depth.

--- SENTENCE 641 ---
The lack of such pools elsewhere around Ofu suggests that subsidence didn't cause them, since there would be pools all around the island.

--- SENTENCE 642 ---
The fact that they are at different depths suggests that they weren't produced by tilting of the island, since then all on the south would be about the same depth.

--- SENTENCE 643 ---
One possibility is that it is due to slow slippage, much like hillside slippage that can lead to part of a road slowly sliding down.

--- SENTENCE 644 ---
Such slippage could occur at different rates in different areas, producing different depth pools, and only produce pools in one area, the south side of Ofu.

--- SENTENCE 645 ---
Slippage would have to be slower than corals can grow, since the reef flat has kept up with sea level and many *Porites* corals have reached the surface and formed microatolls.

--- SENTENCE 646 ---
One remaining puzzle is that if it is slow slippage, there should be a crack where it is separating from the rest of the island, like the crack in the road where the road is slipping away, or the crack at the top of a glacier (called a "bergshrund") where the sliding glacier pulls away from material above it.

--- SENTENCE 647 ---
Such a crack has not been reported.

--- SENTENCE 648 ---
The Ofu pools have a wide variety of coral species in them, over 100 species, and are not dominated by any one species, though species of the genus *Porites* that form mounds (called "massive") dominate some of the pools.

--- SENTENCE 649 ---
At low tide waves do not make it over the reef flat into the pools, and they become still and clear.

--- SENTENCE 650 ---
It may be that the south side of Ofu has experienced a small amount of uplift, perhaps about 10 cm, just enough to not allow waves over the reef flat at low tide.

--- SENTENCE 651 ---
Midday in summer on a sunny day they also become hot, hotter than corals can normally withstand (Craig et al., 2001).

--- SENTENCE 652 ---
Studies are underway to determine how they are able to withstand these high temperatures (Smith and Birkeland, 2007; Barshis et al. 2010; Oliver and Palumbi, 2009; 2011a; 2011b).

--- SENTENCE 653 ---
It may be that bleaching mortality from high temperatures in the Ofu pools keeps *A. muricata* (which is more sensitive than other corals) from dominating the Ofu pools, and allows other more slow-growing but more resistant species, such as massive *Porites* species, to be common, and a higher diversity to be present.

--- SENTENCE 654 ---
Temperatures in Tutuila lagoon pools also reach higher temperatures than the ocean, but not nearly as hot as in the Ofu pools, except at Fagaitua.

--- SENTENCE 655 ---
The staghorns in some lagoon pools in Tutuila have begun to bleach every summer, mainly in Alofau and Nuuuli, but also at the airport (Fenner and Heron, 2009).

--- SENTENCE 656 ---
The corals turn white when water temperatures are too high and/or the sunlight is too strong.

--- SENTENCE 657 ---
They turn white because tiny single-celled colored algae inside the coral cells leave them, leaving the thin living tissue clear and the white skeleton visible through the tissue.

--- SENTENCE 658 ---
The tiny algae (called "zooxanthellae") are what give the coral color, and they also provide the coral with food.

--- SENTENCE 659 ---
If the water gets too hot, the corals can die.

--- SENTENCE 660 ---
Although the staghorns in the backreef pools bleach each summer now, few have died since 2003.

--- SENTENCE 661 ---
Global warming means that more and more corals may bleach and die in future summers.

--- SENTENCE 662 ---
In Tutuila, water continues to flow through most of the lagoon pools even at low tide, though circulation is very minimal in Alofau and Coconut Point pools.

--- SENTENCE 663 ---
In Alofau and Coconut Point, the water in the pools is turbid, and near shore there are periodic algae blooms.

--- SENTENCE 664 ---
This is probably from nutrient runoff in streams from on land, coupled with the slow circulation to replace the water.

--- SENTENCE 665 ---
Waves break on the reef in an area known as the reef crest.

--- SENTENCE 666 ---
The reef crest is not actually any higher here than the reef flat, but the waves keep this narrow area wet even at low tide.

--- SENTENCE 667 ---
There is a bit more variety of coral here, with *Pocillopora verrucosa, P. damicornis and P. setichelli* being common in some areas, and *Acropora nana* being abundant and very characteristic of this zone.

--- SENTENCE 668 ---
*Acropora nana* forms small clumps of thin branches that are quite close together.

--- SENTENCE 669 ---
It seems surprising that a coral with such thin branches would live right where the big waves crash (since the branches surely are more delicate than thicker branches), but it appears that the fact that they are close together may reduce their chance of breaking.

--- SENTENCE 670 ---
Because they are very close together, each branch is protected by its neighbors from the full force of the waves; water speeds between branches no doubt decrease greatly as you move down between the branches of the coral.

--- SENTENCE 671 ---
Thus, each branch is exposed to the full force of the wave only on a small area near the branch tip.

--- SENTENCE 672 ---
The three *Pocillopora* species all have branches close together and form small clumps much like *A*.

--- SENTENCE 673 ---
*nana* on the crest.

--- SENTENCE 674 ---
*P. setichelli* has especially close branch spacing for a *Pocillopora* species.

--- SENTENCE 675 ---
So it appears that they benefit from the same hydrodynamic features that *A*.

--- SENTENCE 676 ---
The strong surge means that water movement between the closely spaced branches is much greater than for a colony in calm water.

--- SENTENCE 677 ---
A major problem for corals with branches close together is that water circulation deep between the branches slows so much that exchange of nutrients, oxygen, etc. with the water are so reduced that in many species the lower parts of branches die and only the tips remain alive.

--- SENTENCE 678 ---
If the bases of the branches die, they are invaded by boring sponges and other organisms and quickly loose strength and are likely to break.

--- SENTENCE 679 ---
In heavy surf, water movement is much improved deep in a colony with tightly spaced branches.

--- SENTENCE 680 ---
So the closely spaced branches both provides surprising strength in heavy waves, and provides better water circulation deep in the colony in the presence of heavy waves.

--- SENTENCE 681 ---
Further, light is very intense in the shallow water, so light levels are still more than sufficient near the base of the branches of these relatively small colonies.

--- SENTENCE 682 ---
So colonies with tightly spaced branches can withstand heavy waves and can grow better there than in calmer or deeper water.

--- SENTENCE 683 ---
Actually lots of *A. nana* branches do get broken, some areas are strewn with broken off branches.

--- SENTENCE 684 ---
Apparently the loss of branches is not so great as to outweigh the other benefits of living on the crest, and most places there aren't large numbers of broken off branches.

--- SENTENCE 685 ---
Short finger *Acropora* such as *Acropora digitifera* may also be common here along with a variety of other *Acropora*.

--- SENTENCE 686 ---
In this area, crustose coralline algae is often very common, covering everything between the corals, giving the reef a light pink or purplish color and a smooth, clean texture.

--- SENTENCE 687 ---
Coralline algae grows well in wave action because waves remove sediment from the algae which cannot clean itself.

--- SENTENCE 688 ---
The reef crest around Tutuila is actually partly protected by the shelf.

--- SENTENCE 689 ---
The shelf reduces the wave power that reaches the crest around Tutuila, because although it is 30-100 m (100-300 feet) deep and thus doesn't reduce the waves much, it is a mile wide so it actually does reduce the waves.

--- SENTENCE 690 ---
The difference can be seen at Rose Atoll and Swains, where there is no bank.

--- SENTENCE 691 ---
At those two, the reef crest gets hit harder, and there are no corals on the reef crest.

--- SENTENCE 692 ---
Only coralline algae can survive the waves there because it is smooth and solid, and as a result the crest there is completely made of pink coralline algae, and looks very pink from a distance.

--- SENTENCE 693 ---
Because waves break directly on sharp coral around Tututila, it is difficult or impossible to get safely over the reef crest in most locations.

--- SENTENCE 694 ---
If the waves are large, it is extremely dangerous.

--- SENTENCE 695 ---
This makes it very hard to access the reef slope without

a boat.

--- SENTENCE 696 ---
One place where it is possible to safely access the reef slope by snorkeling is in Alofau, where waves do not break most days on a section of the reef crest that faces westward away from the prevailing incoming waves which come from the southeast.

--- SENTENCE 697 ---
In Fagatele Bay, it is possible to get over the reef crest, especially at high tide, but this is a risky procedure unless the person is an experienced snorkeler and a good swimmer.

--- SENTENCE 698 ---
It's a bit easier at Vatia but you need high tide to get over the reef flat with a tank.

--- SENTENCE 699 ---
Beyond the reef crest is the reef slope or forereef.

--- SENTENCE 700 ---
Here the reef slopes down into deeper water.

--- SENTENCE 701 ---
In most areas, the bottom slopes very gradually from the crest downward, gradually getting steeper in deeper water until reaching a near vertical slope.

--- SENTENCE 702 ---
In a few places like Alofau the reef crest is a flat area, and the reef slope descends vertically from that reef crest for at least 10 or 20 feet (3-7 m).

--- SENTENCE 703 ---
Below that the reef may descend at a slope of roughly a 45 degree angle.

--- SENTENCE 704 ---
Some of the reef slopes have large ridges running down the slope, separated by deep grooves.

--- SENTENCE 705 ---
These can be called spurs and grooves or buttresses and sand channels.

--- SENTENCE 706 ---
These are usually quite irregular in shape in Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 707 ---
The grooves are usually narrow, and some of them are quite narrow in shallow water, about 1- 3 feet (0.3-1 m) wide.

--- SENTENCE 708 ---
These small grooves have rounded sides, are U-shaped near the bottom, and have some large pieces of rounded rubble on their bottom.

--- SENTENCE 709 ---
In storms, heavy surge moves the rubble back and forth, grinding the groove smooth and deeper.

--- SENTENCE 710 ---
Typically, these grooves are prominent in shallow water on the slope, but less so in deeper water, because the surge is more powerful in shallow water and thus can move the blocks more, grinding the groove down.

--- SENTENCE 711 ---
The reef slope extends down to a depth of about 60-100 feet (20-30 m), where it meets a flat or gently sloping bottom of white calcareous rubble and/or sand.

--- SENTENCE 712 ---
This is the beginning of the shelf.

--- SENTENCE 713 ---
On the slope, the water is relatively clear (often 75-100 foot visibility averaging about 85 feet or 26 meters) with low nutrients and little plankton.

--- SENTENCE 714 ---
In addition, waves produce considerable surge, which sweeps the water back and forth across the reef.

--- SENTENCE 715 ---
The wave surge appears to resuspend any sediment coming from off of land, until it is swept off the reef, so terrestrial sediment is not visible on the reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 716 ---
There is a small amount of suspended sediment in the water on the reef slope giving it a haze.

--- SENTENCE 717 ---
Water on the outer banks is truly oceanic and crystal clear.

--- SENTENCE 718 ---
Crustose coralline algae is the most common organism on the upper reef slope, probably because the clean water and constant surge remove any sediment, and herbivorous fish like surgeonfish and parrotfish remove algae.

--- SENTENCE 719 ---
Close observation reveals that fairly often, fine turf algae grows on the coralline algae.

--- SENTENCE 720 ---
Without heavy herbivory, that filamentous algae would grow dense, trap sediment, and smother the coralline algae.

--- SENTENCE 721 ---
Some surfaces in deeper water are covered with a fine grey fuzzy looking layer.

--- SENTENCE 722 ---
This is turf (filamentous) algae with large amounts of fine sediment trapped it in, just the thing that can smother coralline algae.

--- SENTENCE 723 ---
Although wave surge is ubiquitous, actual currents are usually quite slow and not noticeable by a diver.

--- SENTENCE 724 ---
One place with currents is the channel between Aunu'u and Tutuila- at times it appears to be moving fast not far from the Aunu'u harbor, and some current can be felt on the reef west of the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 725 ---
East of the harbor the currents can be very strong.

--- SENTENCE 726 ---
Another place is near the southwest point of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 727 ---
There is a wide diversity of corals on reef slopes, though the most common corals are encrusting species of *Montipora*, plus *Porites rus* (which forms plates and irregular columns) and *Pavona varians* (which is encrusting).

--- SENTENCE 728 ---
There are many rounded or massive corals as well as the encrusting species and some branching or plate corals.

--- SENTENCE 729 ---
Crustose coralline algae and encrusting corals dominate the upper reef slope, and then on some reefs there is a relatively sharp boundary below which the green alga *Halimeda* and/or plate corals such as *Mycedium* dominate.

--- SENTENCE 730 ---
Table corals (*Acropora hyacinthus*), which grow up on a short stalk like a pedestal, are usually most common in shallow water and dominate the western side of Fagatelle Bay.

--- SENTENCE 731 ---
There is also quite a bit on the tops of ridges in Leone, though the tsunami broke and killed most in the grooves between the ridges.

--- SENTENCE 732 ---
The encrusting coral *Isopora crateriformis* often has plate-like edges, and dominates the shallow reef slope in the area from Leone to Sili in the southwest of the island.

--- SENTENCE 733 ---
It used to be referred to as *Acropora crateriformis*, but it has more features in common with *Isopora*.

--- SENTENCE 734 ---
In bays, wave action may be less, and currents do not carry sediments away.

--- SENTENCE 735 ---
In some, like Vatia, the coral assemblage appears quite different due to the high sediment loading and reduced surge.

--- SENTENCE 736 ---
Thus, in Vatia, *Porites rus*, massive *Porites*, and finger coral (*Porites cylindrica*) are most common and there is sand and fine silt between coral colonies.

--- SENTENCE 737 ---
*Porites cylindrica* is common only in protected locations like Vatia and backreef pools.

--- SENTENCE 738 ---
In Fagasa, everything on the deeper part of the reef is covered with fine gray silt and there are few corals, not even many dead corals.

!

--- SENTENCE 739 ---
[](_page_32_Picture_1.jpeg)

Backreef pools, reef flat, crest (surf line) and spur and groove on the upper slope of the south side of Ofu.

!

--- SENTENCE 740 ---
A spot on the upper reef slope at Vatia with high coral cover before the tsunami.

!

--- SENTENCE 741 ---
A spot with high coral cover in the backreef pool at Alofau.

!

--- SENTENCE 742 ---
[](_page_32_Picture_7.jpeg)

A spot on the mid-depth slope at Aunu'u.

--- SENTENCE 743 ---
Turbidity and silt are especially heavy in the harbor, and many reef areas there have been dredged or killed by sediment.

--- SENTENCE 744 ---
The reefs are somewhat different on the north and south sides of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 745 ---
The north side has reef only in bays, while reef is more continuous on the south side as noted before.

--- SENTENCE 746 ---
Points on the north side are volcanic basalt rock.

--- SENTENCE 747 ---
On the south side, encrusting coralline algae predominates, while on the north side filamentous algae is more common.

--- SENTENCE 748 ---
This is probably because the southeast tradewinds produce more wave surge on the south than the north for much of the year.

--- SENTENCE 749 ---
There is coralline algae at the top of the slope on most north side reefs, but it does not extend very far down the slope (about 12 feet or 4 meters on one reef) compared to the south side, where it extends deeper (about 40 feet or 12 meters deep may be fairly typical, but at Fagatele Bay it dominates to a depth of at least 60 feet or 18 meters; Fagatele Bay experiences strong wave surge most of the time, because it is out at the edge of the shelf and has no shelf to reduce wave power).

--- SENTENCE 750 ---
Wave surge that is sufficient for coralline algae probably extends deeper on the south side than in bays on the north.

--- SENTENCE 751 ---
*Acropora* are more common on the south side than the north side, though exceptional areas exist.

--- SENTENCE 752 ---
The north has more *Porites*, while the south has more *Pocillopora*.

--- SENTENCE 753 ---
The south side has more parrotfish than the north.

--- SENTENCE 754 ---
There are some distinctive coral communities on some spots on the reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 755 ---
For instance, there is a reef near the mouth of Vatia Bay on the northeast which is especially rich in a variety of species of staghorns and tables (*Acropora*).

--- SENTENCE 756 ---
In contrast, the reef slope near Auto and Amaua on the southeast is dominated in medium depths by large fields of *Lobophyllia hemprichii*.

--- SENTENCE 757 ---
In Larson's Bay, there are large fields of *Merulina ampliata* at about 10 m depth, but a diversity of corals in both shallower and deeper areas.

--- SENTENCE 758 ---
In parts of Fagatele Bay, the shallowest depths are dominated by *Galaxea fascicularis*, and shallow to medium depths are heavily dominated by table corals, mostly *Acropora hyacinthus*.

--- SENTENCE 759 ---
At another spot in the bay, there is a huge mound of *Pachyseris rugosa* in medium depths, the largest the author has ever seen.

--- SENTENCE 760 ---
The deeper slopes in Fagatele Bay and several other sites have large numbers of mushroom corals (fungiids).

--- SENTENCE 761 ---
The soft coral genus *Cladiella* is common on some reef slopes, but gorgonians are quite rare, as are black corals and azooxanthellate soft corals like *Dendronephthya*.

--- SENTENCE 762 ---
Black corals are only known to be common at one spot at about 200 feet (60 m) deep.

--- SENTENCE 763 ---
The corallimorph, *Rhodactis*, forms large monospecific patches at Tafeu, but is rare elsewhere on reef slopes, though it is fairly common in the lagoon at Nuuuli and used to have significant patches in Vatia Bay that were overgrowing corals.

--- SENTENCE 764 ---
A green *Zoanthus* sp. appearing similar to *Zoanthus sociatus* is common in most lagoons, but rare on the reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 765 ---
Most coral species on the reef slopes are not in the backreef pools.

--- SENTENCE 766 ---
The most dominant staghorn species in the pools, *Acropora muricata* (*=formosa*), and another less common lagoon staghorn (*Acropora pulchra*) are not found on outer reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 767 ---
The other dominant species in the lagoons, *Porites cylindrica*, is rare on most reef slopes though it is common in the protected and silty waters of Vatia Bay.

--- SENTENCE 768 ---
Deeper parts of some areas of the reef slope are covered with the green calcarious algae of the genus *Halimeda*, usually along with plating corals such as *Mycedium*.

--- SENTENCE 769 ---
The banks that form a broken chain of drowned barrier reef on the shelf have areas of high coral cover with abundant table corals (*Acropora clathrata*) and a diversity of other corals.

--- SENTENCE 770 ---
Other areas, such as much of the top of Taema Banks, are dominated by coralline algae with little coral, and some areas are dominated by rubble such as the north slope of Taema Banks.

--- SENTENCE 771 ---
Mapping the banks for coral has just begun.

--- SENTENCE 772 ---
Many coral reefs go through repeated cycles of disturbance and recovery, as has been documented on the Great Barrier Reef (Connell et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 773 ---
The coral reefs of American Samoa have been subjected to a variety of disturbances in recent decades.

--- SENTENCE 774 ---
Hurricanes struck in 1973, 1981, 1987, 1990, 1991, 2004, 2005 and 2010.

--- SENTENCE 775 ---
Hurricanes can cause a great deal of mechanical breakage of corals and subsequent death of fragments.

--- SENTENCE 776 ---
Damage depends on how close the eye of the hurricane comes to the island, how powerful the hurricane is, how quickly it moves over the island, and which side of the island is exposed to the wind-generated waves.

--- SENTENCE 777 ---
One section of reef may be badly damaged and another may completely escape damage.

--- SENTENCE 778 ---
The reef slope at Fagaalu at about 30 feet (9 meters) deep is covered with coral rubble composed of pieces of branching coral that was probably staghorn (*Acropora*) which may have been killed by the hurricanes in 1990 and/or

--- SENTENCE 779 ---
1991(or by disease or bleaching).

--- SENTENCE 780 ---
As stated before, Tutuila has probably been struck by hurricanes about 300,000 times since it was formed.

--- SENTENCE 781 ---
Coral reefs have evolved to deal with hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 782 ---
Further, there is nothing humans can do to stop hurricanes or reduce their effects on coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 783 ---
After a hurricane, damaged reefs recover in the following years.

--- SENTENCE 784 ---
The reefs repeatedly cycle through damage and recovery.

--- SENTENCE 785 ---
They recover if humans are not chronically damaging the reef.

--- SENTENCE 786 ---
The coral reefs of American Samoa were severely damaged by an outbreak of crownof-thorns starfish (*Acanthaster planci*) in the late 1970's, around 1978.

--- SENTENCE 787 ---
Much of the coral community was eaten and killed by these starfish, leaving only dead skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 788 ---
However, in the ensuing years, the starfish disappeared and the coral populations largely recovered.

--- SENTENCE 789 ---
A visual estimate by R. Wass of coral cover before the crown-of-thorns outbreak indicated over 60% live coral cover, though an estimate of coral cover in Fagatele Bay at the time by C. Birkeland was 30-50%.

--- SENTENCE 790 ---
Now live coral cover averages about 30% around Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 791 ---
60% is very high coral cover, and is likely to occur only when there has been an unusually long period without a major disturbance.

--- SENTENCE 792 ---
Further, the sites the 60% was based on may have been selected for having good coral cover, and accurately estimating coral cover is not easy.

--- SENTENCE 793 ---
So 60% coral cover is likely to be unusually high for American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 794 ---
30% coral cover is actually higher than the average for the South Pacific and the whole Pacific currently, though it is somewhat less than the Pacific had in 1983 (Fenner 2007;

--- SENTENCE 795 ---
Fenner et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 796 ---
Another disturbance on these reefs has been mass coral bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 797 ---
Mass coral bleaching occurred in 1994 and 2003, with a less intense bleaching event in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 798 ---
Bleaching in 1994 produced some coral mortality on the reef slopes (Goreau and Hayes, 1994).

--- SENTENCE 799 ---
Bleaching was documented on the reef slopes in 2002 (Green, 2002) and 2003, but the extent of bleaching and mortality was not recorded in 2003.

--- SENTENCE 800 ---
About half of all staghorns in the deeper backreef pools were dead by the beginning of 2004, and appeared to have been dead for several years.

--- SENTENCE 801 ---
Thus, they may have been killed before the mass bleaching in 2002 and 2003.

--- SENTENCE 802 ---
Mass bleaching tends to kill some corals more than others, and *Acropora* is one of the most sensitive genera, along with the fire coral, *Millepora*.

--- SENTENCE 803 ---
In the Ofu lagoon pools there are quite a few clumps of dead branches that had no live coral visible in late 2003.

--- SENTENCE 804 ---
However, by 2006, many of those clumps were spouting branches of *Millepora dichotoma*.

--- SENTENCE 805 ---
Apparently the bleaching in 2002 and 2003 killed them, but tiny spots that couldn't be seen survived, and regrew in the following years, Phoenix-like.

--- SENTENCE 806 ---
Coral disease has killed much of the coral in the Caribbean, particularly the two species of staghorn that dominated many reefs, and was the primary cause of the reduction of the coral cover there from about 55% in 1977 to about 8% now (Gardiner et al. 2003).

--- SENTENCE 807 ---
Coral disesases are present in American Samoa but not common now (Aeby et al, 2006).

--- SENTENCE 808 ---
However, following the periods of mass coral bleaching in 2002 and 2003, there were outbreaks of coral disease, particularly white syndrome and *Pocillopora* white band (P. Craig, personal comm.).

--- SENTENCE 809 ---
White syndrome usually kills the table coral it infects.

--- SENTENCE 810 ---
The 2005 mass bleaching event in the eastern Caribbean also had a disease outbreak associated with it (Wilkinson and Souter, 2008), so this is not the only time this has happened (Selig et al. 2006;

--- SENTENCE 811 ---
Bruno et al. 2007).

--- SENTENCE 812 ---
Such disease outbreaks associated with mass bleaching events act to multiply the mortality that the bleaching event causes, and so is a cause for significant concern.

--- SENTENCE 813 ---
Coral populations on the reef flats are periodically killed by unusually low tides, as explained earlier.

--- SENTENCE 814 ---
The coral populations have recovered from all these discrete events.

--- SENTENCE 815 ---
This may be because the outer reef slopes are in relatively clear oceanic water and are not impacted by chronic human (anthropogenic) disturbance.

--- SENTENCE 816 ---
In the harbor, however, hard coral populations have declined and some soft coral populations have disappeared since the first measurements of them in 1917.

--- SENTENCE 817 ---
That is, in the areas in which the reefs have not been completely destroyed by being dredged or filled.

--- SENTENCE 818 ---
Sedimentation, high nutrients, and chemical pollution may be some of the chronic disturbances produced by human activities which have had caused these declines.

--- SENTENCE 819 ---
The genus *Acropora* is probably the most important single genus on coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 820 ---
It has the most species (up to 165 species are currently recognized: Veron, 2000).

--- SENTENCE 821 ---
Further, they are abundant on many reefs, and dominate many reefs.

--- SENTENCE 822 ---
The "staghorn" shaped branching species are also the fastest growing corals in the world, able to grow up to about 10-20 cm (4-8 inches) in length per year.

--- SENTENCE 823 ---
On the other hand, they are among the most vulnerable to disease, with table corals having the highest rates of "tumors" (growth anomalies) and "white syndrome" of any corals in American Samoa (Aeby et al, 2006).

--- SENTENCE 824 ---
White syndrome is among the most lethal coral diseases, moving very fast across a colony and almost always fatal.

--- SENTENCE 825 ---
Further, most *Acropora* have branching shapes that are relatively delicate and thus easily broken and killed by hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 826 ---
Also, *Acropora* is among the most vulnerable corals to mass coral bleaching, and thus the first killed by mass bleaching events.

--- SENTENCE 827 ---
*Acropora* is even among the favorite foods of the crown-ofthorns starfish.

--- SENTENCE 828 ---
Table corals are particularly strong competitors for other corals, because they can grow above other corals shading them and thus making it hard for them to grow, and with their table top above the other corals, it is hard for the other corals to reach them with stingers (nematocysts) on their tentacles, even if the tentacles are the specially elongated "sweeper tentacles" used to try to sting other corals in the battle for space.

--- SENTENCE 829 ---
So in the absence of disturbances like hurricanes, crown-of-thorns outbreaks, mass coral bleaching, and disease outbreaks, *Acropora* and particularly table corals may be able to outcompete rival coral species and come to dominate reefs.

--- SENTENCE 830 ---
Descriptions of reefs in American Samoa before crown-of-thorns ate most of the corals indicate that indeed tables and staghorns were common (C. Birkeland, personal comm.)

--- SENTENCE 831 ---
All of these things together suggest that the coral communities are dynamic, unstable features of these reefs.

--- SENTENCE 832 ---
Disturbances tend to kill the very species that come to dominate in the absence of disturbance.

--- SENTENCE 833 ---
Periodic disturbances probably keep most of the reefs in the process of

recovery, and allow other coral species to be common, even though they are less able to compete with tables and staghorn.

--- SENTENCE 834 ---
Soft corals are a minor part of the fauna on American Samoan reefs, though at least 10 genera are found here.

--- SENTENCE 835 ---
Surveys have shown that their populations dropped precipitously on reef flats in the harbor in recent decades (Craig et al. 2005).

--- SENTENCE 836 ---
Drilling of reefs in the harbor revealed these reefs are mostly made of the spicules of soft corals in the genus *Sinularia* (Birkeland et al. 2008), yet they are now rare in the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 837 ---
Soft corals are moderately common at some sites on reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 838 ---
All the soft corals on the reef flats and slopes are zooxanthellate, they contain the same sort of single-cell algae that the hard corals have (dinoflagellates called zooxanthellae).

--- SENTENCE 839 ---
The most common genus on the reef slopes is *Cladiella*.

--- SENTENCE 840 ---
Zooxanthellate soft corals are typically dull browns or greens, much like hard corals.

--- SENTENCE 841 ---
Azooxanthellate soft corals are often brilliant colors such as reds, oranges, yellows or purples, but they are very rare most places in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 842 ---
One exception is a vertical basalt wall near the southwest corner of Tutuila, in the village of Amanave.

--- SENTENCE 843 ---
This wall is reported to have many purple, yellow, and white soft corals, probably in genus *Dendronephthya*.

--- SENTENCE 844 ---
Azooxanthellate soft corals are most common on shaded surfaces such as vertical walls.

--- SENTENCE 845 ---
There are also a few species of zoanthids and corallimorphs, which are groups that have polyps but no skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 846 ---
There are a variety of other non-cryptic (exposed) macroinvertebrates, though they are not common on American Samoa reefs.

--- SENTENCE 847 ---
Giant clams (faisua) are uncommon as they are highly prized for food by the Samoan people and likely overfished.

--- SENTENCE 848 ---
One species (*Hippopus hippopus*) is known only from a few sub-fossil old shells.

--- SENTENCE 849 ---
It may have been driven to local extinction long ago, and since has been reintroduced, but now it has not been seen for some time and might once again be locally extinct.

--- SENTENCE 850 ---
The most common invertebrates are a little black sea urchin (*Echinostrephus molaris*) that lives in holes it makes in the reef rock, an encrusting grey-blue sponge (*Dysidea*) and a small orange sponge, *Styllissa massa* or *S. flabelliformis*.

--- SENTENCE 851 ---
Very few large urchins or sea cucumbers live on the reef slopes, but sea cucumbers are common in some areas of the reef flat pools.

--- SENTENCE 852 ---
Fairly high densities of the black urchin *Echinothrix diadema* live in a band just inside the reef crest at some locations.

--- SENTENCE 853 ---
Crown-of-thorns seastars (*Acanthaster planci*) are rare on Tutuila, but a small persistent population lives on Ofu-Olosega.

--- SENTENCE 854 ---
Adults eat the live tissue off of hard corals.

--- SENTENCE 855 ---
Juveniles live in holes in the reef and eat coralline algae, so small crown-of-thorns are hard to find and it is hard to predict when a new outbreak will occur.

--- SENTENCE 856 ---
Recently (2012) groups of crown-of-thorns have been found a few places on Tutuila and Upolu, indicating populations are now increasing.

--- SENTENCE 857 ---
The Triton shell (*Charonis tritonis*), which eats starfish including crown-of-thorns, is also rare.

--- SENTENCE 858 ---
Spiny lobsters live mostly just seaward of the reef crest, and slipper lobsters live at all depths.

--- SENTENCE 859 ---
Both are hard to find during the day but come out at night.

--- SENTENCE 860 ---
Filter feeders such as sponges, boring clams, feather duster worms, crinoids, black corals, azooxanthellate soft corals and ascidians are rare, small and/or cryptic, except in the harbor where small oysters near the intertidal are common and sponges are common in deeper water, on the inner reef flat of Leone (which is nearly totally covered by an encrusting grey sponge) and on a vertical basalt wall near the SW corner of the island (in Amanave) which has many soft corals.

--- SENTENCE 861 ---
The Coral Reef Ecosystem Division of NOAA in Hawaii has gathered data which suggests that there may be upwelling in the southwestern area of the island, which may provide nutrients for plankton growth.

--- SENTENCE 862 ---
The rarity of filter feeders most places in American Samoa is likely

because water on the reef slopes is clear and has little plankton for them to eat, while the harbor, the Leone inner reef flat, and the southwestern upwelling area have more nutrients and turbid water with plankton.

--- SENTENCE 863 ---
However, if there is any increase in nutrients in plankton on the southwestern side of the island, it is a relatively small amount, particularly compared to the dense plankton making the harbor water look a thick green.

--- SENTENCE 864 ---
There are planktivorous fish on the reef slopes such as fusiliers, small damsels and black triggers (which eat jellies), but anthias are relatively rare.

--- SENTENCE 865 ---
There is some suggestion that planktivorous fish may be more common in the southwestern part of the island.

--- SENTENCE 866 ---
There are many species of invertebrates on the reefs, including over 700 species of mollusks now known.

--- SENTENCE 867 ---
A total of 2705 reef species, including fish, corals, other invertebrates, and algae are now known from American Samoa (Fenner et al, 2008), but this is a truly tiny fraction of the total that is here.

--- SENTENCE 868 ---
The actual total is likely to be in the 100's of thousands.

--- SENTENCE 869 ---
The total is not known from any reef anywhere in the world yet; the highest number known for any one location is over 8000 in New Caledonia (Payri and Richer de Forges, 2006).

--- SENTENCE 870 ---
The amount of work and expertise needed to find and identify all of them would be staggering.

--- SENTENCE 871 ---
A good guidebook to shallow water invertebrates in American Samoa is "Field guide of shallow water marine invertebrates of American Samoa," by Madrigal (1999).

--- SENTENCE 872 ---
One invertebrate, the Palolo worm (*Eunice viridis*), has a spectacular spawning event every year.

--- SENTENCE 873 ---
About 7 days after full moon in October or November, millions of these worms spawn on one night, at about 1 am.

--- SENTENCE 874 ---
In the previous months the worms, which live in holes in the reef, grow a reproductive section on their tail end.

--- SENTENCE 875 ---
On spawning night, they back out of their burrows and the reproductive tail end breaks off.

--- SENTENCE 876 ---
The tail end then swims toward the surface.

--- SENTENCE 877 ---
Male worm tails are brown and females green.

--- SENTENCE 878 ---
Before dawn the tail sections explode, releasing eggs and sperm.

--- SENTENCE 879 ---
The fertilized eggs then float off in the sea to develop into tiny worms which will then settle on the reef.

--- SENTENCE 880 ---
Samoans scoop up the worms when they come out of the reef and eat them.

--- SENTENCE 881 ---
Palolo worms are considered quite a delicacy.

--- SENTENCE 882 ---
Spawning occurs on the same night in the Manu'a and independent Samoa, but at different times.

--- SENTENCE 883 ---
It also occurs in Fiji and probably elsewhere.

--- SENTENCE 884 ---
As stated before, crustose coralline algae dominates upper reef slopes of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 885 ---
They probably dominate the reef slopes because the strong wave surge cleans any sediment off their surfaces, and because there are still good populations of the herbivorous fish, such as surgeonfish and parrotfish.

--- SENTENCE 886 ---
Coral larvae are attracted to settle on some coralline algae by a chemical released by bacteria that live on the surface of the algae.

--- SENTENCE 887 ---
So a reef dominated by coralline algae is basically a healthy, clean reef.

--- SENTENCE 888 ---
There are also species of coralline algae that form bushy or leafy colonies, and these are also common.

--- SENTENCE 889 ---
One genus of green algae, *Halimeda*, produces strings of green plates and often forms small bushes or lawns.

--- SENTENCE 890 ---
The green plates contain calcium which remain as flakes after the alga dies and can form sand.

--- SENTENCE 891 ---
A few sites are nearly covered by *Halimeda*, but it is not known to be a problem.

--- SENTENCE 892 ---
Other kinds of algae that are large enough to easily see (called 'macroalgae') have been rare on most reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 893 ---
In 2008, the brown alga *Dictyota* was found to be common on the slopes of the east side of Vatia bay, where it was absent in previous years.

--- SENTENCE 894 ---
It was growing over living corals as well as *Halimeda* and sand.

--- SENTENCE 895 ---
It was also reported in the Nuuuli backreef pool, appearing around January, 2008, and washed up on the beach in Aoa.

--- SENTENCE 896 ---
*Dictyota* is one of the brown algae that has grown over dead coral in the Caribbean, helping to turn coral reefs into algae beds, so it is a

cause for concern and needs to be monitored.

--- SENTENCE 897 ---
In some of the reef flat pools, there are areas where algae bloom periodically.

--- SENTENCE 898 ---
At the head of the harbor there have been nontoxic red tide blooms that turn the water red.

--- SENTENCE 899 ---
These are likely to be places of higher nutrient levels from runoff from villages, piggeries, and/or fields.

--- SENTENCE 900 ---
The higher levels probably occur mainly because there is little flushing at the head of a narrow bay, so whatever washes into the head of the bay builds up instead of being dispersed as it is along the outer coast.

--- SENTENCE 901 ---
The biodiversity of macroalgae on American Samoa reefs is fairly high, with over 237 species known.

--- SENTENCE 902 ---
Another type of algae is turf or 'filamentous' algae, which are composed of fine hairlike filaments.

--- SENTENCE 903 ---
These algae grow on any dead surface, and grow very rapidly.

--- SENTENCE 904 ---
They do not produce any heavy defenses, like calcium, cellulose, or chemicals, unlike the macroalgae.

--- SENTENCE 905 ---
Producing those defenses slows the growth of the macroalgae, but protects them from the many herbivorous fish like surgeonfish and parrotfish, and the many other herbivores like sea urchins and snails.

--- SENTENCE 906 ---
Herbivores usually prefer to eat filamentous algae because it is not defended.

--- SENTENCE 907 ---
Because the filamentous algae grow fast and are quickly eaten, they form a low, fine 'turf' on dead surfaces which is not very obvious.

--- SENTENCE 908 ---
However, because they grow rapidly and are eaten quickly, they produce a large amount of food and are the base of a large part of the food chain.

--- SENTENCE 909 ---
Some of the most common fish on American Samoa reefs are surgeonfish and parrotfish, most of which are herbivores that eat filamentous algae.

--- SENTENCE 910 ---
Herbivorous fish are likely to be more abundant where there is more filamentous algae and less coral.

--- SENTENCE 911 ---
A last group of algae is the blue-green algae or cyanobacteria.

--- SENTENCE 912 ---
These are actually bacteria, but are photosynthetic.

--- SENTENCE 913 ---
They form fine filaments formed of strings of cells, and often look like clumps of fine stringy hair which easily comes apart.

--- SENTENCE 914 ---
Cyanobacteria lack some of the ability of other algae to use low concentrations of nutrients, so they are not very common unless nutrient levels are elevated.

--- SENTENCE 915 ---
When nutrient levels are high, though, they can grow especially rapidly and accumulate.

--- SENTENCE 916 ---
Occasionally, accumulations of cyanobacteria can be seen on our reefs.

--- SENTENCE 917 ---
Cyanobacteria are often toxic, so although they are soft and easily eaten, they often form large streamers or globs, because the fish won't eat them.

--- SENTENCE 918 ---
The reef slopes as well as the reef flats and lagoons have remarkably low amounts of brown macroalgae on them.

--- SENTENCE 919 ---
The reef flats of Fiji's Coral Coast are now covered with brown macroalgae such as *Sargassum* and *Turbinaria*.

--- SENTENCE 920 ---
Nutrient levels in the water on the reef flats are elevated above natural levels for coral reefs (Mosley and Aalbersberg, 2003), and fish abundances are far below levels remembered by villagers.

--- SENTENCE 921 ---
The combination of increased nutrients and decreased herbivorous fish stimulates algal growth and reduces consumption of the algae, with the result that the brown algae bloom, covering the reef.

--- SENTENCE 922 ---
The fact that this has not yet happened in American Samoa is very good.

--- SENTENCE 923 ---
The experience of Fiji is a lesson for American Samoa to not overfish the herbivorous fish and not release so many nutrients that algae is fertilized and blooms.

--- SENTENCE 924 ---
In American Samoa, it is important that surgeonfish and parrotfish (which are both mostly herbivorous families of fish) remain abundant.

--- SENTENCE 925 ---
The abundance of coralline algae is also a good sign for American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 926 ---
Coralline algae grows best when there is little sediment and there is strong herbivory, so neither sediment nor algae cover them.

--- SENTENCE 927 ---
These are just the conditions that corals thrive in.

--- SENTENCE 928 ---
This may be the reason why coral larvae are stimulated to settle by coralline algae (the coralline algae indicates a good place to settle).

--- SENTENCE 929 ---
When corals are

killed by a disturbance in American Samoa, crustose coralline algae are quick to cover the dead coral skeletons, and cement them together to make a stable substrate, which corals need to survive.

--- SENTENCE 930 ---
The coralline algae then make an attractive substrate for coral larvae to settle on, and so encourage the growth of new corals and the recovery of the reef.

--- SENTENCE 931 ---
The fringing reefs of Tutuila and Ofu-Olosega all end at their deep end in either sand or rubble (except in the harbor where they end in silty mud), anywhere from around 45 feet deep to about 100 feet deep (15 to 30 meters), but more commonly closer to 60 feet deep.

--- SENTENCE 932 ---
The sand or rubble plain may be at a slight slope or appear to be very flat.

--- SENTENCE 933 ---
The sand and rubble are the beginning of a large underwater shelf around Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 934 ---
There are some features on this shelf, such at Taema Banks at the mouth of the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 935 ---
Some of these banks are elongated parallel to the shore and rise to within 30 feet of the surface.

--- SENTENCE 936 ---
Some have a flat bare top, and a slight slope on their seaward edge that has corals growing on it.

--- SENTENCE 937 ---
The corals end at about 100-130 feet (30-40 m) deep in a flat sand plain.

--- SENTENCE 938 ---
NOAA has used multibeam sonar to map the shallow waters around American Samoa, including the shelf around Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 939 ---
The resulting map shows many details not known before (page 9; Fenner et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 940 ---
The author assisted in a research submarine exploration by Dawn Wright and the Hawaii Undersea Research Laboratory (NOAA) of the bottom seaward of Taema Banks in 2005.

--- SENTENCE 941 ---
The sand plane has a very slight slope, and across the width of the plain the shelf increases in depth to about 330 feet (100 m) deep.

--- SENTENCE 942 ---
The shallow half of the plain has fields of green algae (*Halimeda*) on it.

--- SENTENCE 943 ---
When the algae die, they leave behind little plates of calcium, and much of the sand the algae grow on is composed of these plates.

--- SENTENCE 944 ---
The deeper half of the shelf has a species of single-celled animal called a foram or foramaniferan that forms hard, thin coin-like discs, which are abundant there.

--- SENTENCE 945 ---
The discs are up to about 2 inches (5 cm) in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 946 ---
They are among the world's largest forams and the world's largest single animal cells.

--- SENTENCE 947 ---
The genus is *Cycloclypeus*, and it is restricted to deep water where there is no surge to break the thin fragile discs (Song et al, 1994).

--- SENTENCE 948 ---
The cell that builds the disc contains algal symbionts, usually diatoms, so it must have light.

--- SENTENCE 949 ---
The diatoms must be able to use relatively dim light, since they are at about 70-90 m deep on the shelf where light is fairly dim.

--- SENTENCE 950 ---
At about 330 feet (100 m) deep there is a roll off to a near vertical cliff.

--- SENTENCE 951 ---
On the roll off there are gorgonian sea fans with feather stars on them, and mounds of rubble made by small sand tilefish digging their burrows.

--- SENTENCE 952 ---
On the cliff, layers of limestone produced by ancient reefs can be seen.

--- SENTENCE 953 ---
The cliff extends down to a depth of over 1000 feet (350 m), and below that a talus slope of sand and pieces of limestone descends at an angle.

--- SENTENCE 954 ---
No hard corals were seen on the shelf, cliff, or slope.

--- SENTENCE 955 ---
At Fagatele Bay, there is little shelf, and the cliff is dissected by grooves and ridges.

--- SENTENCE 956 ---
At Swains Island, a small shelf extends a few tens of meters from the reef crest where waves smash on encrusting coralline algae, to a depth of about 5 meters, where a 45 degree slope extends down well below scuba depths.

--- SENTENCE 957 ---
There is no deep shelf at Swains or Rose Atoll.

--- SENTENCE 958 ---
A ridge with coral on it connects Olosega to Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 959 ---
The seamount 40 miles south of Tutuila called "South Bank" by the fishermen, has a very flat top in about 80+ feet of water, with a slight hint of an atoll ring.

--- SENTENCE 960 ---
The surface is covered with small rubble and some sand.

--- SENTENCE 961 ---
There are a few lumps of coral rock with a few corals on them, and that is all.

--- SENTENCE 962 ---
It appears to be a drowned atoll that is now dead.

#### **

--- SENTENCE 963 ---
**

 That's not very obvious.

--- SENTENCE 964 ---
In some cultures corals are called "colored rocks."

--- SENTENCE 965 ---
It seemed to early scientists that they had features of both plants and animals, so they called them "zoophytes" (zoo = animal, phyte = plant).

--- SENTENCE 966 ---
In time, it became clear to the scientists that they were animals, related to jellyfish and sea anemones.

--- SENTENCE 967 ---
They are actually very similar to sea anemones, with a couple major differences.

--- SENTENCE 968 ---
A sea anemone is a "polyp," which is a body shaped like a grocery bag, with tentacles around the opening.

--- SENTENCE 969 ---
The opening is the mouth.

--- SENTENCE 970 ---
The bottom of the bag is commonly glued onto something hard.

--- SENTENCE 971 ---
The wall of the sea anemone is made up of just two layers of cells, with connective tissue between them.

--- SENTENCE 972 ---
They are very simple animals, and have no organs.

--- SENTENCE 973 ---
A jellyfish is called a "medusa" and is sort of a polyp turned upside-down with the tentacles and mouth facing downward.

--- SENTENCE 974 ---
It's also not attached except in rare instances.

--- SENTENCE 975 ---
Coral, jellyfish, and anemones are all in a large group called "Cnidaria."

--- SENTENCE 976 ---
That word means "stinging nettles," and of course jellyfish are well known to be able to sting.

--- SENTENCE 977 ---
Actually, anemones and corals are also able to sting.

--- SENTENCE 978 ---
The stingers (called nematocysts) are microscopic, located inside cells in the skin of the tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 979 ---
When something touches them they expode, firing a tiny tube that works like a hypodermic needle full of powerful venom into the victim.

--- SENTENCE 980 ---
There is a jellyfish in Australia that can kill a child in two minutes!

--- SENTENCE 981 ---
Anemones commonly sting, kill, and eat fish and other animals.

--- SENTENCE 982 ---
They may look like a flower (an anemone is a garden flower)

--- SENTENCE 983 ---
but they are ferocious like a lion.

--- SENTENCE 984 ---
They use their tentacles to pull their prey to their mouth and swallow.

--- SENTENCE 985 ---
The inside of the bag acts as a stomach.

--- SENTENCE 986 ---
When they finish digesting, they expel undigested remains out their mouth.

--- SENTENCE 987 ---
The similarity of corals to anemones can be seen in a few corals called "mushroom corals."

--- SENTENCE 988 ---
They are a polyp, and one species has large tentacles that look just like an anemone.

--- SENTENCE 989 ---
Others have short tentacles, and most have such tiny tentacles you can't see them.

--- SENTENCE 990 ---
In the middle, they have a slit that is their mouth.

--- SENTENCE 991 ---
There are two major differences between anemones and corals, however.

--- SENTENCE 992 ---
One is that corals don't leave finding a hard thing to attach to, to chance, they build their own, in the form of what we call a "skeleton."

--- SENTENCE 993 ---
This is a hard thing, made of white calcium carbonate (CaCO3).

--- SENTENCE 994 ---
It is underneath the polyp, and so outside the living part of the coral in most instances.

--- SENTENCE 995 ---
The skeleton is itself not alive, which is actually different from our skeleton, which has living cells in it and blood vessels to supply those cells, etc.

--- SENTENCE 996 ---
Our skeleton is also internal, and it has many separate pieces that have joints so they can move.

--- SENTENCE 997 ---
But corals have just one piece of skeleton, it is dead, and it has no joints-

--- SENTENCE 998 ---
so it can't move.

--- SENTENCE 999 ---
The coral skeleton is the rock which the coral adds to the reef, the bricks that the reef is made of, one on top of another.

--- SENTENCE 1000 ---
The second difference is that corals have more than one polyp.

--- SENTENCE 1001 ---
An anemone is all one polyp, with one ring of tentacles around one mouth.

--- SENTENCE 1002 ---
When an anemone grows, it can get to a size where it is ready to reproduce.

--- SENTENCE 1003 ---
One way it can reproduce is by simply dividing into two!

--- SENTENCE 1004 ---
You want to have kids, just split down the middle?

--- SENTENCE 1005 ---
Anyhow, dividing is called fission and anemones can do it often.

--- SENTENCE 1006 ---
Corals start out as a single tiny polyp, and as it grows, it starts to divide into two.

--- SENTENCE 1007 ---
But it doesn't finish the job, and the two polyps stay attached to each other.

--- SENTENCE 1008 ---
Then the two partway divide into four, four into eight and so on until a single coral can have many polyps- a large coral that has tiny

!

--- SENTENCE 1009 ---
The coral *Heliofungia* (not found here) which has tentacles as large as an anemone.

--- SENTENCE 1010 ---
This coral has a skeleton under tentacles that is nearly identical to that in the following picture.

!

--- SENTENCE 1011 ---
A coral closely related to the coral shown above (*Heliofungia*), named *Fungia fungites*, photographed here.

--- SENTENCE 1012 ---
The tentacles are much smaller and the shape including the mouth slit and the radiating septa (ridges) all show the shape of the skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 1013 ---
polyps can have millions of polyps.

--- SENTENCE 1014 ---
Because the polyps are all attached to each other, in some ways a coral acts like a single individual, and we call it a "colony.

--- SENTENCE 1015 ---
"

 One last important feature of reef corals and some anemones is that they supplement their diet.

--- SENTENCE 1016 ---
They have tiny single-celled plants inside the cells that line the inside of their body.

--- SENTENCE 1017 ---
These cells are a kind of algae (dinoflagellates) usually called zooxanthellae (which just means "brown algae that lives inside an animal").

--- SENTENCE 1018 ---
When sunlight strikes them, they can do photosynthesis and make food.

--- SENTENCE 1019 ---
Some of the food leaks out and nourishes the coral animal.

--- SENTENCE 1020 ---
This is why reef-building corals can grow only in shallow sun-lit water: so their algae can make food.

--- SENTENCE 1021 ---
So now that you know what corals are, are they animal, vegetable, or mineral?

--- SENTENCE 1022 ---
How about all three?

--- SENTENCE 1023 ---
An animal, with a plant inside it, making a mineral.

--- SENTENCE 1024 ---
### **Coral Identification and Taxonomy**

 Coral identification and taxonomy are almost entirely based on the skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1025 ---
In the days of wooden sailing ships, a ship would return to Europe or American after being at sea for a year or more, and would sometimes be carrying some odds and ends picked up along the way by the crew or ship naturalist, like Charles Darwin.

--- SENTENCE 1026 ---
If you take a coral out of the water, it dies, and the thin tissue rots off (especially in a damp ship hold, I bet).

--- SENTENCE 1027 ---
Long before the ship reached home port, any corals consisted only of the skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 1028 ---
Further, the skeleton is rigid and holds all the shape and tiny spines and details, while the soft tissues that are highly elastic are easy to deform into all sorts of shapes.

--- SENTENCE 1029 ---
Just preserving them in alcohol or formaldehyde will cause them to scrunch up and be highly distorted.

--- SENTENCE 1030 ---
Even if you have the tissue, there are a lot more fine details you can see in the skeleton, and they are easier to study and more reliable.

--- SENTENCE 1031 ---
There is now actually one genus (*Euphyllia*) which has large tentacles that are so different that a few species are distinguished based on their tentacles alone even though their skeleton is identical.

--- SENTENCE 1032 ---
Scientists are now working on the genetics of corals.

--- SENTENCE 1033 ---
Corals aren't easy to do genetics on compared to other organisms (hard to get "primers").

--- SENTENCE 1034 ---
Further, some of the results coming out now do not correspond very well with the traditional taxonomy based on the skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1035 ---
Part of that may be because they are an old group, and very elastic and deformable over evolutionary time, and there are often very few characters to use even with the skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1036 ---
If you look at vertebrate skeletons, there are a myriad of bones, all fitting together in very regular patterns, and changes in evolution are relatively easy to figure out.

--- SENTENCE 1037 ---
Not so with corals.

--- SENTENCE 1038 ---
It is particularly difficult to try to figure out the evolution of corals at the family level, there have been a few very different proposals.

--- SENTENCE 1039 ---
Another reason genetics may not correspond well is that the genetics that is being done is of very small parts of the total genome- other parts might produce different results.

--- SENTENCE 1040 ---
The basic structure of coral skeletons reflects the colonial polyps that construct the skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 1041 ---
So for each polyp there is a cup-like structure called a "corallite" that the polyp is situated in.

--- SENTENCE 1042 ---
Actually, it's a little like a fort- a refuge the polyp can contract into if something threatens it.

--- SENTENCE 1043 ---
Corallites often protrude, and the word "corallite" refers to all of the structure including the protruding part and the outer slopes of the volcano-shaped structure.

--- SENTENCE 1044 ---
The inside of the cup by itself is called the "calice," another name for cup.

--- SENTENCE 1045 ---
Occassionally someone mistakenly refers to it as a calyx- that's a term in plants and soft corals.

--- SENTENCE 1046 ---
Coral polyps build ridges or plates on the inside walls of the calice.

--- SENTENCE 1047 ---
These ridges or plates are called "septa" or "septum" for singular.

--- SENTENCE 1048 ---
They are most easily visible on mushroom corals where they are quite large.

--- SENTENCE 1049 ---
Septa often have teeth on their edge, granules on their sides and/or edges and can produce larger projections called "palli" as well.

--- SENTENCE 1050 ---
Septa often come in different numbers and different sizes, with the first set of six being largest, the second set of six between those may be smaller, the next set of 12 between those 12 smaller still, and so on.

--- SENTENCE 1051 ---
Septa commonly extend up and over the walls of the calice to the outside slopes of the corallite, where they are called costae.

--- SENTENCE 1052 ---
The spaces between corallites are called "coenosteum" and often are ornamented with spines.

--- SENTENCE 1053 ---
In the center of a corallite there is usually a spongy structure called a "columella."

--- SENTENCE 1054 ---
All of these things have many variations.

--- SENTENCE 1055 ---
In addition, the shape of the colony is a cue that helps distinguish some species.

--- SENTENCE 1056 ---
Colors are usually not helpful, but on rare occasions they can be distinctive for a species.

--- SENTENCE 1057 ---
A few species have large fleshy polyps, and then the shape of the polyps and their tentacles can help distinguish species.

--- SENTENCE 1058 ---
But the things that you use to distinguish coral species in the water are often not those that are used to distinguish them in taxonomy- you often can't see the finer details of the skeleton through the live tissue.

--- SENTENCE 1059 ---
On the other hand, you can see the shape of the whole colony, while skeleton samples in collections are often just pieces of colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1060 ---
Futher, in the water you can see many

colonies (unless it is rare!)

--- SENTENCE 1061 ---
and thus much of the variation.

--- SENTENCE 1062 ---
Variation is what kills coral taxonomists.

--- SENTENCE 1063 ---
There is huge variation within species and small differences between species, making things very difficult.

--- SENTENCE 1064 ---
When you go to a new location, you discover things you think are the same species you saw elsewhere, but are somewhat different, and you can't be sure it is the same species.

--- SENTENCE 1065 ---
Other things you see may look different from anything you've seen before.

--- SENTENCE 1066 ---
Describing and studying that variation is important, which is one reason why a detailed description of Samoan corals is important.

--- SENTENCE 1067 ---
It is necessary to study the skeletons at any new location to confirm the identification of the live corals.

--- SENTENCE 1068 ---
A guidebook to the corals of the Samoan archipelago will hopefully help all those who need to study or work with the reef, as well as encourage a knowledge and appreciation of the corals by the people who live here.

--- SENTENCE 1069 ---
For more details on the skeletons which the identifications are based on, see Veron (2000), Wallace (1999) and Hoeksema (1989) and the upcoming coral taxonomy monograph by Fenner (in press).

#### **Coral Diversity and Biogeography**

 Coral reefs are sometimes said to have the highest diversity of any marine ecosystem, and some of them do indeed.

--- SENTENCE 1070 ---
However, coral reefs vary greatly in diversity.

--- SENTENCE 1071 ---
Some of the lowest diversity coral reefs are the small, widely scattered reefs on the Pacific coast of the Americas.

--- SENTENCE 1072 ---
These reefs are commonly dominated by a single species of coral, and have a total of about 3-10 species of coral on them.

--- SENTENCE 1073 ---
The world's most diverse coral reefs are in the area sometimes called the "Coral Triangle", encompassing the Philippines, eastern Indonesia, New Guinea and the Solomon Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1074 ---
Coral diversity decreases in all directions from this center- north, south, east and west.

--- SENTENCE 1075 ---
Nearly every group of coral reef organisms shows the same pattern of gradients, such as corals, fish, mollusks, echinoderms and sea grasses.

--- SENTENCE 1076 ---
The Samoan archipelago is located midway in the Pacific between the depauperate reefs of the Pacific Americas and the Coral Triangle between the Pacific and Indian Oceans.

--- SENTENCE 1077 ---
As a result, the diversity of corals is not as high as in the Coral Triangle, but is much higher than in the Eastern Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 1078 ---
About 600 species of coral are currently known from the Coral Triangle, and the number known continues to grow.

--- SENTENCE 1079 ---
Only about 30 species are known from all the Eastern Pacific put together.

--- SENTENCE 1080 ---
Hawai'i has about 65 species.

--- SENTENCE 1081 ---
A total of 165 species of hard corals are presented in this book from the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 1082 ---
The total number eventually found here will likely be considerably more.

--- SENTENCE 1083 ---
The total can be estimated by two methods.

--- SENTENCE 1084 ---
One method is to look at the known ranges of corals shown in Corals of the World (Veron, 2000).

--- SENTENCE 1085 ---
The proportion of the corals which Samoa is in range of indicates what proportion of the total for Samoa is currently known.

--- SENTENCE 1086 ---
Another method depends on the fact that coral species ranges can extend both east and west, or can extend only west, but almost never extend only east in the Pacific (because almost all ranges extend west to the center of diversity in the Philippines-Indonesia-New Guinea area).

--- SENTENCE 1087 ---
Thus, of the non-endemic species in Hawai'i, all species are likely to be in Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1088 ---
Again, the proportion so far found in Samoa indicates the proportion of the total fauna so far found.

--- SENTENCE 1089 ---
These two methods produce very similar results.

--- SENTENCE 1090 ---
Both indicate that the total number of coral species that will eventually be found here may be about 440 species.

--- SENTENCE 1091 ---
Increases in searching time, effort

and area searched increases the number of species found in a very regular fashion, such that doubling the amount of effort produces a fixed number of additional species.

--- SENTENCE 1092 ---
Continuing effort produces diminishing returns.

--- SENTENCE 1093 ---
Calculations suggest that about 2000 additional dives in different locations may be required to find all of the remaining coral species.

--- SENTENCE 1094 ---
Another method for measuring coral diversity also shows that coral diversity in American Samoa is quite high.

--- SENTENCE 1095 ---
The author has collected data on dives of about one hour duration, during which he searches the reef from bottom to top looking for as many coral species as can be found.

--- SENTENCE 1096 ---
In American Samoa, an average of 71 species per dive were found, while in the Coral Triangle, an average of 94 species per dive were found.

--- SENTENCE 1097 ---
Thus, American Samoa had a local coral diversity 75.5% as high as was found in the Coral Triangle area of highest diversity.

--- SENTENCE 1098 ---
This is very close to the 73% which 440 species would represent of the 600 species in the Coral Triangle.

--- SENTENCE 1099 ---
However, more species will certainly be found in the Coral Triangle beyond 600, and 440 species have not yet been found in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1100 ---
Another aspect is that the Coral Triangle is vastly larger than American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1101 ---
The number of species increases with increasing area, which fits with the observation that different reefs and nearby islands have somewhat different species lists.

--- SENTENCE 1102 ---
American Samoa may have about ¾ of the coral diversity of a similar sized island in the Coral Triangle.

--- SENTENCE 1103 ---
It should be noted that in brief work in (independent) Samoa, all the species seen there had previously been seen by the author in American Samoa except three (*Seriatopora hystrix*, *S. aculeata*, and *Leptoseris incrustans*).

--- SENTENCE 1104 ---
These islands are close enough together, that almost all marine species that are in one are likely to be in the other as well.

--- SENTENCE 1105 ---
The only limitation might be that if one habitat did not occur on an island, then species that are strictly limited to that habitat might not be found there.

--- SENTENCE 1106 ---
Most or nearly all of the coral species in this book should be found on all the high islands of the archipelago, though Swains clearly has a different reef community.

--- SENTENCE 1107 ---
In high diversity ecosystems it is said that most species are rare.

--- SENTENCE 1108 ---
That is certainly true of the corals in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1109 ---
A few species, *Porites cylindrica*, *Acropora muricata*, *Montipora grisea*, *Pavona varians*, *Porites rus*, *Lobophyllia hemprichii, Isopora crateriformis* and *Acropora hyacinthus* are abundant, but most other corals are much less abundant quickly tapering off towards rare, which most of the species are.

--- SENTENCE 1110 ---
This property of the ecosystem makes finding all the species a near-impossible task, and means that guidebooks and taxonomic studies can be more complete than their predecessors and a step forward, but unlikely to ever have all of the species.

--- SENTENCE 1111 ---
The taxonomist has the choice between doing something useful now or spending the rest of their life trying to make it complete.

--- SENTENCE 1112 ---
No study can be complete or final, only an advance in the field and the best possible in a finite period of time.

--- SENTENCE 1113 ---
#### Some global perspectives on our coral reefs

 The islands and reefs of American Samoa are quite small.

--- SENTENCE 1114 ---
However, the reef area of the Pacific is the largest of any ocean in the world.

--- SENTENCE 1115 ---
If you turn a globe the right way, almost all you can see is the Pacific Ocean (and part of the Southern Ocean which has no land dividing it from the Pacific).

--- SENTENCE 1116 ---
Although the Pacific and that part of the Southern Ocean together do not cover half the planet, they cover around 40% of the planet.

--- SENTENCE 1117 ---
The Pacific Ocean has about 4 times as much coral reef area as the Indian Ocean (including

the Red Sea and Arabian Gulf), and nearly 10 times the coral reef area of the Atlantic.

--- SENTENCE 1118 ---
The Indo-Pacific biogeographic area is the largest area of coral reefs with a unified set of organisms in the world, by far, encompassing all of the Pacific except the East Pacific, plus the Indian Ocean, Red Sea, and Arabian Gulf.

--- SENTENCE 1119 ---
The Western Atlantic is very small in comparison, composed of the Caribbean, Florida, part of the Gulf of Mexico, and Bermuda.

--- SENTENCE 1120 ---
The largest part of the Western Atlantic reef area, the Caribbean, is smaller than French Polynesia alone, which has an EEZ as large as all of Europe.

--- SENTENCE 1121 ---
French Polynesia is small compared to the Pacific Ocean, let alone the Indo-Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 1122 ---
The greatest concentration of reefs is in the Coral Triangle-Australia region.

--- SENTENCE 1123 ---
Australia and Indonesia compete for the title of country with the most coral reefs, and they are virtually tied.

--- SENTENCE 1124 ---
The Philippines has the third largest coral reef area of any country in the world, and no other country comes close to those top three countries.

--- SENTENCE 1125 ---
The Philippines lies entirely in the Pacific, as does the Great Barrier Reef, and reefs along the north side of Indonesia.

--- SENTENCE 1126 ---
Ningaloo Reef on the western coast of Australia (the world's largest fringing reef) is in the Indian Ocean, as are the reefs along the south side of Indonesia.

--- SENTENCE 1127 ---
The Coral Triangle has the highest coral and fish species diversity, and high or the highest diversity of most other kinds of coral reef organisms as well.

--- SENTENCE 1128 ---
The richest zone of the Great Barrier Reef is about as diverse as sites in the Coral Triangle.

--- SENTENCE 1129 ---
American Samoa reefs are not as diverse, but do pretty well.

--- SENTENCE 1130 ---
The total number of coral reef species known in American Samoa is about 2700, but that is a truly tiny fraction of the total species present here, and we don't even have an estimate of the total here.

--- SENTENCE 1131 ---
