The most coral reef species that have been identified anywhere is 8500 in New Caledonia (a listing of the names alone of the species there fills a book an inch thick).

--- SENTENCE 1132 ---
But the latest estimate is that the world's coral reefs all together have a total of about a million species of all kinds.

--- SENTENCE 1133 ---
Of course, no one spot has all of those species.

--- SENTENCE 1134 ---
To find and identify the species on coral reefs is very labor-intensive, and requires taxonomic experts.

--- SENTENCE 1135 ---
A majority of the coral reef species have not even been named so far, and a large part have not even been found, let alone named.

--- SENTENCE 1136 ---
The number of species found so far at a location is almost completely a function of how much effort has been expended there by taxonomic experts.

--- SENTENCE 1137 ---
You have to have different taxonomic experts for each group of organisms.

--- SENTENCE 1138 ---
Fish are probably the best known of any group of organisms on coral reefs, followed by corals.

--- SENTENCE 1139 ---
We think of reef corals as diverse, but really they aren't, with only 800 or so known in the whole world.

--- SENTENCE 1140 ---
There are something like 30,000 species of fish known from all over the world (a large part of which are fresh water species and most fish are not coral reef species).

--- SENTENCE 1141 ---
There are about 40,000 orchids in the world, and several million species of insects.

--- SENTENCE 1142 ---
The groups of organisms that have the largest number of species have individuals that are small, and they are the least well known.

--- SENTENCE 1143 ---
In any case, American Samoa has just under 1000 species of reef fish, compared to perhaps 2500 in the Philippines or Indonesia and about 600 in Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 1144 ---
But the only way to make anything close to an accurate comparison between areas is to equate for effort and area.

--- SENTENCE 1145 ---
I can do that looking at corals, doing a standard one hour search dive.

--- SENTENCE 1146 ---
On the average, I find about 94 coral species in one such dive in the Coral Triangle, about 71 species in American Samoa, and about 14 in Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 1147 ---
This is the most accurate comparison for the diversity of a standard size area searched with standard effort available, and it shows that American Samoa does pretty well.

--- SENTENCE 1148 ---
American Samoa is the eastern limit of the biogeographic range of blue coral (*Heliopora coerulea*), seagrass, and mangroves (there is one small spot in French Polynesia with one species of seagrass, and

another such spot with one species of mangrove; mangroves have been introduced into Hawaii).

--- SENTENCE 1149 ---
Likely American Samoa is the eastern limit of the ranges of some other species as well, though that is not as well documented.

--- SENTENCE 1150 ---
Because diversity is higher to the west and lower to the east, other places in the Pacific are also likely to be the eastern limit for some species, though which species will differ from location to location.

--- SENTENCE 1151 ---
Independent Samoa is the eastern limit of large seagrass species.

--- SENTENCE 1152 ---
The gap to the west of us is small and the gap to the east of us is large, so (independent) Samoa is likely to be the eastern limit of the range of fewer species than American Samoa, because the barrier to dispersal is greater with greater distances.

--- SENTENCE 1153 ---
The total number of species for each area is also affected by the size of the area.

--- SENTENCE 1154 ---
For instance, American Samoa has 6 islands (Ofu and Olosega are a single island as far as the reefs are concerned, the reefs are continuous between them), while the Great Barrier reef has about 2500 reefs, the Philippines has about 3000 islands, and Indonesia has about 13,000 islands.

--- SENTENCE 1155 ---
Two nearby islands will have most of their corals in common, but there will be some corals on one of the islands but not on the other.

--- SENTENCE 1156 ---
So even if the number of species in the area of one dive is the same, if one area is larger it will have a larger total number of species.

--- SENTENCE 1157 ---
The theory of Island Biogeography provides predictions of how much the number of species should increase with increasing area.

--- SENTENCE 1158 ---
It was developed based on the land area of islands, and has rarely been applied to reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1159 ---
But the general trend is clear, the larger the area the more species (though the relationship is not linear, rather the increases are proportional to something like the power function of the area, such that if you add a fixed size area, the rate of increase of species slows down as the area gets larger).

--- SENTENCE 1160 ---
So the Coral Triangle has more species partly because it has more species in a small fixed area than elsewhere, and partly because the area of reefs in the Coral Triangle is so large.

--- SENTENCE 1161 ---
The Caribbean is not only relatively small, but it also has lower diversity than the Indo-Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 1162 ---
The areas of reef in the Eastern Pacific (the Pacific coast of the Americas and a few small islands) and Brazil are each much smaller than the Caribbean, and both areas have much lower diversity than the Caribbean, they are the lowest diversity reef zones in the world, along with the Arabian Gulf, which has extreme conditions.

--- SENTENCE 1163 ---
The eastern Atlantic and Mediterranean each have a few zooxanthellate corals, but no reefs (carbonate build up below the living corals).

--- SENTENCE 1164 ---
The species in each of these major areas, Indo-Pacific, Caribbean, Eastern Pacific, Brazil, east Atlantic, and Mediterranean, are each almost completely different from the others.

--- SENTENCE 1165 ---
So even though the areas with lower diversity have fewer species, they are still valuable because their species are different, and interesting because the whole ecosystem is based on different species, yet it has many of the same processes.

--- SENTENCE 1166 ---
There are a few exceptions, such as the corals of the Eastern Pacific, which are almost all Indo-Pacific species (though most of the fish there are not).

--- SENTENCE 1167 ---
Also, some of the corals of Brazil and the eastern Atlantic are the same as in the Caribbean.

--- SENTENCE 1168 ---
Another perspective on reefs is provided by recent estimates of the area of reef flats and slopes on the world's coral reefs (Vesci, 2004).

--- SENTENCE 1169 ---
Reef flats are very shallow, so they are easily seen from space and can be mapped and measured in satellite photos.

--- SENTENCE 1170 ---
Reef slopes, however, go down into deeper water, and quickly become hard or impossible to see from space with increasing depth.

--- SENTENCE 1171 ---
So estimates of the world's coral reef area have until recently been based entirely on reef flats.

--- SENTENCE 1172 ---
Vesci (2004) uses typical slope angles and depths to make a range of estimates of reef slope areas in the world.

--- SENTENCE 1173 ---
He estimates that the world's reef flats are 4.7-14.7 times as large as reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1174 ---
Early coral reef

scientists had no scuba or snorkeling gear, and could only access the reef flats, by walking around on them (with the exception of a little bit of information on deeper areas coming from depth measurements from lowering a weight on a line).

--- SENTENCE 1175 ---
So the first studies of coral reefs were all studies of reef flats.

--- SENTENCE 1176 ---
It was only relatively recently, starting around the 1960's, that the first reef scientists began to explore the reef slopes with SCUBA.

--- SENTENCE 1177 ---
Now, reef science is mainly done on reef slopes (probably in part because there is more coral and other life there than on many reef flats), and many scientists seem to have largely forgotten the reef flats.

--- SENTENCE 1178 ---
Reef flats are quite different from reef slopes, and you can't find out what is happening on most of the world's coral reef area by studying reef slopes!!

--- SENTENCE 1179 ---
Reef flats have access advantages and problems.

--- SENTENCE 1180 ---
On fringing reefs reef flats are closer to shore so easy to access, but wave action on them can be severe and make study impossible at times.

#### **Human Impacts on Reefs and Conservation**

 Coral reefs can be damaged by many human activities, and the reefs in Pago Pago Harbor have suffered many of these, and most are now dead.

--- SENTENCE 1181 ---
Originally, living reefs extended about as far into the harbor as the canneries and the marina.

--- SENTENCE 1182 ---
Many were dredged, others filled and built on top of.

--- SENTENCE 1183 ---
Today on the north side the waves can spash right onto the road, eroding it, so the volcanic rock cladding protecting the road is being moved several feet out onto the reef and fill placed behind it.

--- SENTENCE 1184 ---
However, the reef there is long dead, except at the very outer edge of the reef flat, where live corals persist.

--- SENTENCE 1185 ---
For decades the tuna canneries released their effluent directly into the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 1186 ---
The farther you go into the harbor, the less flushing there is, and so nutrients put in can accumulate.

--- SENTENCE 1187 ---
Nutrients dropped dramatically when the liquid outfall pipe was extended to near the harbor mouth, and solid wastes were shipped 5 miles out of the harbor (Fenner, 2008).

--- SENTENCE 1188 ---
Meantime, some industrial wastes continue to leak into the harbor, and area streams have so much plastic trash wash out during rain storms that the harbor is dotted with white Styrofoam.

--- SENTENCE 1189 ---
In those same rainstorms, brown mud washes out of those same streams into the harbor turning the surface of large areas brown.

--- SENTENCE 1190 ---
Nutrients from people, piggeries, lawn fertilizer, and/or laundary detergent probably goes into the harbor as well as the reefs all around our islands.

--- SENTENCE 1191 ---
In 2007-2009 there were non-toxic Red Tide blooms in the head of the harbor, turning the water red from millions of red colored algae feeding on the nutrients (Morton et al. 2011).

--- SENTENCE 1192 ---
Even when there are no red tide blooms, the water at the head of the harbor is murky green from green phytoplankton that bloom when nutrients are plentiful.

--- SENTENCE 1193 ---
On the other hand, if you go near to the mouth of the harbor, the water is clear and blue, and there are reef flats that are solid with healthy looking coral, 60% cover in one small patch, which is as high as is recorded anywhere on the island.

--- SENTENCE 1194 ---
There have been brown algae blooms in Vatia Bay and along Coconut Point, indicating that nutrients in those areas are higher than they should be.

--- SENTENCE 1195 ---
Sediment runoff is a problem several places around the island.

--- SENTENCE 1196 ---
The worst is probably at Fagaalu, where in a heavy rainstorm a thick brown plume enters the bay and is carried out the ava.

--- SENTENCE 1197 ---
But most reef flats look clean- the waves and swift currents swiftly carry any new brown sediment out the ava into the ocean.

--- SENTENCE 1198 ---
The reef slopes also look clean, and

coralline algae and corals seem to be thriving there.

--- SENTENCE 1199 ---
The water looks relatively clear over the slopes, but you can see a haze in the distance.

--- SENTENCE 1200 ---
This is likely sediment which the heavy wave surge continually suspends up off the slope and eventually currents carry away into the ocean.

--- SENTENCE 1201 ---
If you go a mile or more out into the ocean you will see really clear water.

--- SENTENCE 1202 ---
The islands in American Samoa are very steep, and still mostly covered with dense forest.

--- SENTENCE 1203 ---
If that forest were ever to be cut, the sediment runoff would be devastating to the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1204 ---
Luckily, there appears to be no prospect of that.

--- SENTENCE 1205 ---
But people do clear areas on steep slopes for growing taro, and when the rains come, the soil washes away.

--- SENTENCE 1206 ---
Good farmers use lots of mulch to slow that as much as possible, and don't plant on steep slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1207 ---
But there is so little room and the population is growing, where can fields be expanded?

--- SENTENCE 1208 ---
Fishing is another impact that people have on coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1209 ---
The challenges in managing fisheries are many and difficult (Fenner, 2012).

--- SENTENCE 1210 ---
Several articles have discussed coral reef fish and fisheries in American Samoa (e.g., Wass, 1984;

--- SENTENCE 1211 ---
Craig et al. 1993; Craig, 1996; Craig et al. 1997; Craig, 1998; Page, 1998; Green, 2002; Craig and Green 2005; Craig et al. 2005; Zeller et al. 2007; Sabater and Tofaeono, 2007; Brainard et al. 2008; Craig et al. 2008; Fenner et al. 2008; Sabater and Carroll, 2009; Allen et al. 2010; Ochavillo et al. 2010; Kilarksy and Evanson, 2011).

--- SENTENCE 1212 ---
Some people walk out on the reef flat to collect little things to eat in what is called gleaning.

--- SENTENCE 1213 ---
Usually women and children do this.

--- SENTENCE 1214 ---
Also fishermen walk on the reef flat to catch fish with throw nets, and also to cast a line over the edge into the deeper water of the slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1215 ---
Walking can damage coral, but there are not a large number of people doing this, and the coral shows little signs of trampling.

--- SENTENCE 1216 ---
Scientists have found that the world is warming up.

--- SENTENCE 1217 ---
This is called climate change or global warming.

--- SENTENCE 1218 ---
When ocean temperatures get too warm, the corals become sick and expel the tiny algae cells that can make sugar in the presence of sunlight.

--- SENTENCE 1219 ---
The algae have much of the color the coral has, and when they leave the live coral tissue turns clear, and you can easily see the white skeleton gleaming in the bright sunlight.

--- SENTENCE 1220 ---
The coral is still alive, but sick, and now has less to eat.

--- SENTENCE 1221 ---
If it doesn't get any hotter, and then cools off, the coral can get the algae back, get its color back, and survive and start growing again.

--- SENTENCE 1222 ---
It has lost so much energy it can't reproduce for a year.

--- SENTENCE 1223 ---
But if the water gets any hotter, the coral dies, and then fuzzy algae start to grow on the dead white skeleton, quickly turning it light green, then dark green and even black.

--- SENTENCE 1224 ---
We have had three mass bleaching events on the reef slopes we know about, in 1994, 2002, and 2003.

--- SENTENCE 1225 ---
1994 and 2002 were worse than 2003.

--- SENTENCE 1226 ---
Some corals died but not huge numbers.

--- SENTENCE 1227 ---
Since then it has not happened again, but it will sooner or later.

--- SENTENCE 1228 ---
Meantime, in the backreef pools, the water gets hotter on a sunny summer day at low tide, because little water flows through the pool and it heats up in the sun.

--- SENTENCE 1229 ---
It gets several degrees hotter than the reef slope, and now the staghorns in the backreef pools bleach every summer, and a little bit of them dies some summers.

--- SENTENCE 1230 ---
With global temperatures slowly but steadily rising, bleaching events are predicted to become more common and more intense.

--- SENTENCE 1231 ---
Most likely in a few decades, summer bleaching on the slopes may be common, and coral death from bleaching will be common.

--- SENTENCE 1232 ---
The prospects are looking pretty grim in the long term.

--- SENTENCE 1233 ---
The cause is humans all over the world burning too much gasoline, diesel, natural gas, coal, and forests.

--- SENTENCE 1234 ---
The burning puts carbon dioxide in the atmosphere, which holds heat in like a greenhouse, which is why it is sometimes called the greenhouse effect.

--- SENTENCE 1235 ---
We can reduce consumption while not hurting our lifestylethe average Frenchman lives just as luxurious a life as the average American, but produces only one third of the amount of carbon dioxide.

--- SENTENCE 1236 ---
We must take action, because this looks like a global emergency in many ways, not just bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 1237 ---
Please help save the planet and use less fossil fuel any way you can.

--- SENTENCE 1238 ---
The amount of harm most harmful human activities do depends on how many people there are, and how much harm each person does.

--- SENTENCE 1239 ---
If the population stays steady and people reduce the harmful activities, the harm to the reef diminishes.

--- SENTENCE 1240 ---
If everyone keeps doing the same things, and the population doubles, the damage to the reef grows likewise.

--- SENTENCE 1241 ---
If the population doubles and people manage to reduce the harm each does by half, the damage to the reef stays the same.

--- SENTENCE 1242 ---
So population is a multiplier that exacerbates all the problems people cause with reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1243 ---
The population in American Samoa is growing very rapidly, like in most developing countries.

--- SENTENCE 1244 ---
It would grow even faster if American Samoans couldn't easily move to the states (but slower if people in independent Samoa and Tonga couldn't move here).

--- SENTENCE 1245 ---
There are more American Samoans in Carson, California than in American Samoa, lots in Hawaii, and many elsewhere in the states.

--- SENTENCE 1246 ---
That's a pressure relief system for American Samoa, but rapid population growth in the states can cause problems there as well.

--- SENTENCE 1247 ---
The other side of the coin is that many people from independent Samoa come to American Samoa for jobs, and there are now about as many in the child-raising ages as American Samoans here.

--- SENTENCE 1248 ---
Family size here has come down from an average of about six children to an average of about four children, but the population has a very large number of young people in it, who have not completed their families, and so they are now having many children and the territory continues to have many more children than is sustainable.

--- SENTENCE 1249 ---
Rapid economic development often leads to smaller families- people are better educated, and children become expensive.

--- SENTENCE 1250 ---
But rapid economic development appears unlikely here, as there are almost no natural resources (other than the islands' beauty) and it is located very far from any markets, and the people did not inherit a long tradition and knowledge of modern economic ways.

--- SENTENCE 1251 ---
The best hope might be for something like a "call center" providing customer service.

--- SENTENCE 1252 ---
Only a good fiber optic connection is needed for that.

#### **Coral Taxonomy**

 The taxonomy of corals is very complicated, with a long history (a few coral species were described by Linneaus in 1758, when he set up the system of identification that is still in use today).

--- SENTENCE 1253 ---
There are many details which many users of a field guide do not need to learn to identify corals.

--- SENTENCE 1254 ---
However, a field guide really should be based on examination of skeleton samples, since that is what the species identification is really based on.

--- SENTENCE 1255 ---
There are a variety of field guides to living Indo-Pacific coral species available, such as Veron (1986, 2000), Randall and Myers (1983), Nishihira and Veron (1995), Carpenter et al (1997), Pillay et al (2002), Fenner (2005), Erhardt and Knop (2005), Latypov (2006, 2010) and Licuanan (2009).

--- SENTENCE 1256 ---
There are also invertebrate guidebooks that have coral sections, such as Allen and Steene (1994), Colin and Arneson (1995), and Gosliner et al (1996).

--- SENTENCE 1257 ---
In addition, there are taxonomic works which can help, since some corals have very thin tissue and what you see underwater resembles the skeleton with color added.

--- SENTENCE 1258 ---
They also often have photos of a few living colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1259 ---
Such works would include Scleractinia of East Australia by Veron and colleagues, and also Hoeksema (1989), and

Wallace (1999).

--- SENTENCE 1260 ---
The author finds the quality of the field guides to be quite uneven.

--- SENTENCE 1261 ---
The taxonomic works are the best, followed by Veron (2000) and Fenner (2005).

--- SENTENCE 1262 ---
The quality in others is spotty, with perhaps the worst being Erhardt and Knop (2005) where fully half of the species either cannot be recognized with any certainty since the coral is too small in the photo, or just plain wrong.

--- SENTENCE 1263 ---
One photo (*Cosinaraea macneilli* on page 145 of their book) is actually that of a sponge, oscules are clearly visible.

--- SENTENCE 1264 ---
The authors are aquariasts and excellent underwater photographers, not coral taxonomists.

--- SENTENCE 1265 ---
Good photography does not make a good ID guide book.

--- SENTENCE 1266 ---
It makes a pretty coffee table book.

--- SENTENCE 1267 ---
It is for that reason that the author has delayed releasing his field ID guide until he has checked skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1268 ---
It is an ongoing work, and the author hopes to release expanded, updated versions from time to time, which is facilitated by electronic publishing.

--- SENTENCE 1269 ---
The previous guide, "Benthic identification for coral reef monitoring in American Samoa," (Fenner, 2011) covers the soft corals, black corals, zoanthids, and corallimorphs to the genus level in most cases, because identification to the species level is not yet available.

#### **The Corals**

About 439 names have been applied to the hard corals of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1270 ---
Only two published papers have been based on collected corals, and were published by coral taxonomists (Hoffmeister, 1925; Lamberts, 1983), both based on collected samples which can be examined in museums (Smithsonian and Berkeley, respectively).

--- SENTENCE 1271 ---
The rest are unpublished reports produced largely from visual observations, usually by nontaxonomists.

--- SENTENCE 1272 ---
Some of the names used are junior synonyms of other names, and others were likely incorrectly applied.

--- SENTENCE 1273 ---
There are no coral collections from these studies that can be used to verify those species names, and they will remain doubtful until samples can be collected.

--- SENTENCE 1274 ---
The author has a table of all of these names and which studies they come from which is available upon request.

--- SENTENCE 1275 ---
The present study involved photographing live corals, studying corals in the DMWR collection and collecting samples of as many additional species as possible.

--- SENTENCE 1276 ---
Also, Hoffmeister's collection in the Smithsonian Institution and Lamberts' collection in the Berkeley Museum were studied.

--- SENTENCE 1277 ---
Richard Randall has collected a large number of corals from American Samoa and will hopefully be publishing a study of those.

--- SENTENCE 1278 ---
However, his American Samoa corals are intermixed with a very large collection of other corals he has in Guam, which are in wooden crates and not catalogued.

--- SENTENCE 1279 ---
They are in practice inaccessible, and so have not been studied.

--- SENTENCE 1280 ---
A companion work on the skeletal samples will be published, documenting each of the species illustrated here.

--- SENTENCE 1281 ---
The coral families are presented in a conventional taxonomic order, but within a genus similar-appearing species are grouped together as much as possible.

--- SENTENCE 1282 ---
The author suggests learning the most common corals first, or maybe some lifeforms.

--- SENTENCE 1283 ---
Then maybe learn some of the corals you see most commonly or remember easiest.

--- SENTENCE 1284 ---
Looking at the book before getting in the water and then after getting out is a good idea.

--- SENTENCE 1285 ---
Each description gives a rough estimate of how common a coral is.

--- SENTENCE 1286 ---
If you see a coral, it is most likely to be one of the most common, less likely to be uncommon, and very unlikely to be rare.

--- SENTENCE 1287 ---
So if you concentrate on learning common corals first, it will make it much easier.

--- SENTENCE 1288 ---
Learning corals is not easy, don't get discouraged, keep feeling good about the

ones you have learned and forget how many others there are left to learn.

--- SENTENCE 1289 ---
It's the only way to stay sane and keep learning.

--- SENTENCE 1290 ---
Remember, you won't have to learn many to know more than other people!

--- SENTENCE 1291 ---
There is a "Directory to Coral Genera" that shows a thumbnail photo of a common species in each genus, and gives the page number for the genus.

--- SENTENCE 1292 ---
If you pick the one most like what you see, hopefully you will find out what it is quickly.

--- SENTENCE 1293 ---
Just looking at every species in the book will take quite a while, though if you are familiar with what they look like you can probably learn to identify them faster.

--- SENTENCE 1294 ---
If you know the species name, just look in the directory to coral species on pages 3-5, or the index to find the page.

--- SENTENCE 1295 ---
In the descriptions, the most common color is listed first, and an attempt was made to show species in the most common colors as well as pretty colors (which may be rare).

#### **Lifeforms**

Corals have some shapes that are easily recognized by beginners.

--- SENTENCE 1296 ---
Many are referred to in the species descriptions.

--- SENTENCE 1297 ---
Here are some of them.

--- SENTENCE 1298 ---
Encrusting = a thin layer on the bottom like a coat of paint.

--- SENTENCE 1299 ---
Branching = has branches.

--- SENTENCE 1300 ---
(Now, that was easy, wasn't it!

--- SENTENCE 1301 ---
You're off and running!)

--- SENTENCE 1302 ---
Massive = rounded, dome shaped, spherical, but any size (here, massive doesn't mean heavy).

--- SENTENCE 1303 ---
Plate = like a plate with a top and bottom surface above the substrate.

--- SENTENCE 1304 ---
Columnar = has columns that go nearly vertically, and don't branch much.

--- SENTENCE 1305 ---
Staghorn = looks like deer antlers, can form huge thickets, always *Acropora*.

Table

--- SENTENCE 1306 ---
= looks like a circular table, has a pedestal in the middle or side, may have more than one tier, usually has little branchlets on the upper surface, always *Acropora*.

--- SENTENCE 1307 ---
Bushy = lots of branches going in all directions, much like a bush.

--- SENTENCE 1308 ---
Digitate = branches are finger-like and short, and do not branch further, always *Acropora*.

--- SENTENCE 1309 ---
Sub-massive = looks massive at first, then you realize there are thin cracks between the polyps, and find out it is actually branching.

--- SENTENCE 1310 ---
Usually *Lobophyllia* or *Caulastrea*.

### **

--- SENTENCE 1311 ---
The Most Common Corals**

By learning just eight corals, you can recognize many of the corals on our reefs, and luckily the eight most common corals are very easy to distinguish from each other.

!

--- SENTENCE 1312 ---
[](_page_53_Picture_2.jpeg)

*Pocillopora cylindrica* – Finger Coral forms masses of finger shaped branches in the backreef pools.

--- SENTENCE 1313 ---
[](_page_53_Picture_4.jpeg)

*Montipora grisea* forms brown encrusting patches all over the reef slopes and is the most common coral on the reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1314 ---
[](_page_53_Picture_6.jpeg)

*Acropora muricata* – Staghorn Coral forms thickets of deer-antler shaped branches with blue tips in the backreef pools.

--- SENTENCE 1315 ---
This is the most common staghorn, but there are others.

--- SENTENCE 1316 ---
[](_page_53_Picture_8.jpeg)

*Porites rus* forms masses of irregular columns growing up from thin plates on reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1317 ---
Smaller individual columns and plates are common as well.

--- SENTENCE 1318 ---
[](_page_54_Picture_0.jpeg)

*Pavona varians* forms small brown masses or encrusting colonies covered with small ridges.

--- SENTENCE 1319 ---
[](_page_54_Picture_2.jpeg)

*Lobophyllia hemprichii* forms large mounds of many large polyps- polyps are about 1-2 inches (3-5 cm) diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1320 ---
SE Tutuila mid slope.

--- SENTENCE 1321 ---
*Acropora hyacinthus* forms "table corals" and is most common on the upper-mid reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 1322 ---
[](_page_54_Picture_6.jpeg)

*Isopora crateriformis* is encrusting, often with lifted lower plate edges.

--- SENTENCE 1323 ---
Most common on upper reef slopes on the SW of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1324 ---
P. 99

## **Directory to Coral Genera**

!

--- SENTENCE 1330 ---
If a description does not state where a coral is located, then it is found on the reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 1331 ---
Pronunciation guides are given for each genus, derived from Veron (1986).

--- SENTENCE 1332 ---
The accent is placed on the underlined syllables, and is usually on the first syllable and "por".

### **Order Hexacorallia**

 The hexacorals have six tentacles or multiples of six.

--- SENTENCE 1333 ---
If they have lots of tentacles then it is often not an exact multiple of six.

--- SENTENCE 1334 ---
Hexacorallia includes the Scleractinia (which literally means "hard anemones") which are most of what we call "hard corals."

--- SENTENCE 1335 ---
It also includes anemones, black corals, corallimorphs, zoanthids and Ceranthids (burrowing anemones).

--- SENTENCE 1336 ---
See the "Benthic identification for reef monitoring in American Samoa" for the anemones, black corals, zoanthids and corallimorphs.

--- SENTENCE 1337 ---
**Scleractinia** are hexacorallia with hard skeletons made of calcium carbonate in the form of aragonite.

--- SENTENCE 1338 ---
Some are solitary, meaning they have just one polyp and corallite, others are colonial and have several to many polyps and corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1339 ---
Some have zooxanthellae (single celled algae in the group called dinoflagellates) and live in the light and grow large enough to contribute to reefs, and others do not have zooxanthellae and generally live in the dark in deeper water or shaded parts of shallow reefs, and most of them produce small skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1340 ---
Almost all reef-building corals have zooxanthellae and most are colonial, while a majority of azooxanthellate corals are solitary.

--- SENTENCE 1341 ---
**Family Astrocoeniidae** (pronounced ass-tro-see-nee-id-ee) are scleractinia with small, simple corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1342 ---
### *Stylocoeniella* (pronounced sty-lo-see-nee-el-la)

*Stylocoeniella* is an uncommon genus that has just two species in the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 1343 ---
They tend to be encrusting but can have small bumps, and have very tiny polyps (a half millimeter in diameter!).

--- SENTENCE 1344 ---
The distinctive feature is tiny spines on the colony surface, usually one per polyp.

--- SENTENCE 1345 ---
The "style" in the genus name refers to the spines.

#### 1.

--- SENTENCE 1346 ---
*Stylocoeniella armata* (Ehrenberg, 1834)

--- SENTENCE 1347 ---
This coral forms small encrusting colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1348 ---
Colonies are brown with cream highlights and the spines are relatively easily visible.

--- SENTENCE 1349 ---
Found on reef slopes, rare.

!

--- SENTENCE 1350 ---
[](_page_61_Picture_5.jpeg)

A closeup *of Stylocoeniella armata*.

#### 2.

--- SENTENCE 1351 ---
*Stylocoeniella guentheri* Bassett-Smith, 1890

--- SENTENCE 1352 ---
This coral forms larger encrusting colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1353 ---
Colonies commonly have uniform rounded lumps about 1 cm in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1354 ---
Colonies are commonly red, orange, or tan, and may have white dots which are the polyps.

--- SENTENCE 1355 ---
The spines are not easily seen but can be easily felt.

--- SENTENCE 1356 ---
Found on reef slopes, uncommon.

!

--- SENTENCE 1357 ---
[](_page_62_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Stylocoeniella guentheri*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1358 ---
[](_page_63_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Stylocoeniella guentheri*.

--- SENTENCE 1359 ---
**Family Pocilloporidae** (pronounced po-sil-oh-por-id-ee) has small corallites that are are fairly simple.

--- SENTENCE 1360 ---
Colonies are usually branching.

### *Pocillopora* (pronounced po–sil-oh-por-a)

--- SENTENCE 1361 ---
*Pocillopora* is branching, and has small bumps all over the surface called "verrucae."

--- SENTENCE 1362 ---
The corallites are small, only about 1 mm diameter, so hard to see.

--- SENTENCE 1363 ---
The bumps are larger than the corallites, and easy to see, in fact the corallites are all over the bumps as well as between the bumps.

--- SENTENCE 1364 ---
Branches can be cylindrical or flattened or thin enough that it is hard to tell a bump from a short branch.

--- SENTENCE 1365 ---
Colonies commonly are up to a foot in diameter, but a few can get to be 2-3 feet in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1366 ---
Has rounded bumps like some *Montipora*, but corallites are all over the bumps.

--- SENTENCE 1367 ---
Some have branch shapes like *Stylophora*, but have bumps instead of spines.

#### 3*.

--- SENTENCE 1368 ---
Pocillopora damicornis* (Linnaeus, 1758)

--- SENTENCE 1369 ---
This coral forms small bushes.

--- SENTENCE 1370 ---
Branches sub-branch such that there is no clear distinction between branches and verrucae.

--- SENTENCE 1371 ---
Branches are usually close together and very knobby, but can on occasion be farther apart and straighter.

--- SENTENCE 1372 ---
Branches are smaller than on any other species.

--- SENTENCE 1373 ---
Branch tips are always round.

--- SENTENCE 1374 ---
Brown, rarely pink, often with lighter branch tips, common in back reef pools and reef crest.

!

--- SENTENCE 1375 ---
[](_page_64_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Pocillopora damicornis*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1376 ---
[](_page_65_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Pocillopora damicornis*.

--- SENTENCE 1377 ---
The tiny circles are corallites.

#### 4.

--- SENTENCE 1378 ---
*Pocillopora eydouxi* Milne Edwards & Haime, 1860

--- SENTENCE 1379 ---
This coral forms small to medium sized colonies made of branches.

--- SENTENCE 1380 ---
Branches are large and farther apart than on other species.

--- SENTENCE 1381 ---
Branch ends are commonly flattened and may be curved or even meandroid.

--- SENTENCE 1382 ---
Commonly brown but can be pink, purple or green.

--- SENTENCE 1383 ---
Common on reef slopes, also present on reef flats.

!

--- SENTENCE 1384 ---
A colony of *Pocillopora eydouxi* 

!

--- SENTENCE 1385 ---
[](_page_67_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Pocillopora eydouxi*.

--- SENTENCE 1386 ---
*Pocillopora ligulata* Dana, 1846

--- SENTENCE 1387 ---
This coral forms small branching colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1388 ---
Most branches are flattened like *P. meandrina* and *P. eydouxi*, but are thinner.

--- SENTENCE 1389 ---
Branches can be irregular, and look rough as the verrucae are as large as on *P. meandrina*.

--- SENTENCE 1390 ---
Prefers shallow water of reef flats.

--- SENTENCE 1391 ---
A colony of *Pocillopora ligulata.* 

!

--- SENTENCE 1392 ---
[](_page_69_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Pocillopora ligulata*.

--- SENTENCE 1393 ---
*Pocillopora meandrina* Dana, 1846 Cauliflower Coral

This coral forms small bushes.

--- SENTENCE 1394 ---
Branches are medium sized for *Pocillopora*, and most are flattened and curved on their ends.

--- SENTENCE 1395 ---
The verrucae are normal size.

--- SENTENCE 1396 ---
Can be brown, green or perhaps pink, uncommon.

--- SENTENCE 1397 ---
See taxonomic note.

!

--- SENTENCE 1398 ---
A colony of *Pocillopora meandrina.* 

!

--- SENTENCE 1399 ---
A closeup of *Pocillopora meandrina*.

#### 7.

--- SENTENCE 1400 ---
*Pocillopora setichelli* Hoffmeister, 1929

This coral forms small bushes.

--- SENTENCE 1401 ---
Branches are medium sized for *Pocillopora*, but are closer together than for other similar species.

--- SENTENCE 1402 ---
Branch tips are commonly flattened instead of rounded, making the colony surface smooth with narrow uniform cracks between branches.

--- SENTENCE 1403 ---
Branches are usually flattened and meandroid.

--- SENTENCE 1404 ---
Usually brown but can be pink, restricted to the reef crest area, but can be common there.

--- SENTENCE 1405 ---
See taxonomic note.

!

--- SENTENCE 1406 ---
[](_page_72_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Pocillopora setichelli.* 

!

--- SENTENCE 1407 ---

#### 8*.

--- SENTENCE 1408 ---
Pocillopora verrucosa* (Ellis & Solander, 1786)

--- SENTENCE 1409 ---
This coral forms small bushes.

--- SENTENCE 1410 ---
Branches are medium sized for *Pocillopora*, but are not flattened or curved on their ends.

--- SENTENCE 1411 ---
Brown, green or pink, common, particularly on the reef crest.

!

--- SENTENCE 1412 ---
A colony of *Pocillopora verrucosa.

--- SENTENCE 1414 ---
### *Stylophora* (pronounced sty-loh-for-a)

*Stylophora* is a genus represented by just one species in the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 1415 ---
It forms forms branching colonies, usually less than a foot in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1416 ---
Corallites are small, only about 1 mm diameter, and each corallite has a tiny sharp hood partway over it, giving the branch a fine spiny appearance and feel.

--- SENTENCE 1417 ---
Species differ in the diameter of their branches.

#### 9.

--- SENTENCE 1418 ---
*Stylophora pistillata* Esper, 1797

This coral forms small bushes.

--- SENTENCE 1419 ---
Branches are medium sized and commonly flattened and curved.

--- SENTENCE 1420 ---
Branches are covered with tiny spines and lack the larger verrucae of *Pocillopora.*

--- SENTENCE 1421 ---
Each spine is actually a sharp hood over a corallite.

--- SENTENCE 1422 ---
Tan, uncommon, on reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1423 ---
A colony of *Stylophora pistillata*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1424 ---
A closeup of the branches of *Stylophora pistillata* showing the spines.

--- SENTENCE 1425 ---
### *Seriatopora* (pronounced see-ree-at-oh-por-a)

*Seriatopora* has only been seen by the author in independent Samoa so far, but may well be in American Samoa as well, but would surely have to be rare.

--- SENTENCE 1426 ---
*Seriatopora* forms thin branches with rows of small corallites about 1 mm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1427 ---
Colonies are usually under a foot in diameter, and are commonly a light cream color.

--- SENTENCE 1428 ---
*Seriatopora stellata* Quelch, 1886

This coral forms small bushes of thin branches that abrupty taper to sharp points.

--- SENTENCE 1429 ---
Corallites are in raised rows.

--- SENTENCE 1430 ---
Branches are about 3-5 mm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1431 ---
Colonies are commonly cream colored, but may have pink coloration particularly out near the ends of branches.

--- SENTENCE 1432 ---
Not yet found in American Samoa, found in a backreef pool on Upolu (Palolo Deep), rare.

!

--- SENTENCE 1433 ---
A colony of *Seriatopora stellata*, photo taken in (independent) Samoa.

!

--- SENTENCE 1434 ---
[](_page_79_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Seriatopora stellata*, photo taken in (independent) Samoa.

#### 11*.

--- SENTENCE 1435 ---
Seriatopora caliendrum* Ehrenberg, 1834

--- SENTENCE 1436 ---
This coral forms small bushes of thin branches that have rounded tips.

--- SENTENCE 1437 ---
Branches are about 3-5 mm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1438 ---
Colonies are commonly cream colored, but may have pink coloration particularly out near the ends of branches.

--- SENTENCE 1439 ---
Not yet found in American Samoa, found in a backreef pool on Upolu (Palolo Deep), rare.

!

--- SENTENCE 1440 ---
[](_page_80_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Seriatopora caliendrum*.

--- SENTENCE 1441 ---
photo taken in (independent) Samoa

**Family Acroporidae** (pronounced akro-por-id-ee) contains the largest two genera, *Acropora* and *Montipora*, plus several smaller genera.

--- SENTENCE 1442 ---
They all have small corallites, and can be encrusting, massive, or branching.

### *Montipora* (pronounced mon-tee-por-a)

*Montipora* is the most common genus in Tutuila, and is almost always encusting here.

--- SENTENCE 1443 ---
It is the second largest genus in terms of number of species following *Acropora*.

--- SENTENCE 1444 ---
It usually

has very small corallites that are hard to see underwater, and a forest of tiny spines that look and feel like the surface of sandpaper.

--- SENTENCE 1445 ---
There are a few less common species that have larger corallites that can be seen as holes, or the spines are larger and look like little bumps.

--- SENTENCE 1446 ---
If the spines are smaller than the corallites they are called "papillae," if they are about the same size as corallites or larger they are called "tuberculae," and if they are larger than the corallites and uniform and rounded they are called "verrucae."

--- SENTENCE 1447 ---
Most colonies are some shade of brown, but a few are bright orange or purple, and a few have green polyps on a brown background.

--- SENTENCE 1448 ---
When *Monitpora* has verrucae, which is rare here, the verrucae are smooth not covered with corallites like in *Pocillopora*, and the colony is not branching.

--- SENTENCE 1449 ---
In rare instances a colony can have recessed larger polyps and no spines, and then it is very difficult to distinguish from one or two species of *Porites* without knowing what the species is.

!

--- SENTENCE 1450 ---
A closeup showing *Montipora* sp. with the tiny polyps extended.

--- SENTENCE 1451 ---
The species is not known for this colony.

#### 12.

--- SENTENCE 1452 ---
*Montipora grisea*

This coral forms thin encrusting sheets.

--- SENTENCE 1453 ---
The surface has many tiny spines that are so small they are hard to see.

--- SENTENCE 1454 ---
Brown, sometimes green, most abundant of all corals on reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1455 ---
[](_page_82_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Montipora grisea*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1456 ---
A closeup of *Montipora grisea*, showing the tiny circles of papillae around polyps, which are elevated to different degrees.

--- SENTENCE 1457 ---
*Montipora informis* Bernard, 1897

--- SENTENCE 1458 ---
This coral forms thin encrusting sheets covered with a dense cover of uniform tiny spines called papillae.

--- SENTENCE 1459 ---
The spines have rounded tips but are so small this is hard to see.

--- SENTENCE 1460 ---
Brown or grey, and spine tips are usually white, uncommon to rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1461 ---
A colony of *Montipora informis.

--- SENTENCE 1462 ---
[](_page_85_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Montipora informis*.

--- SENTENCE 1463 ---
This coral forms encrusting colonies covered with small bumps called tuberculae which are about the same size as the polyps.

--- SENTENCE 1464 ---
The tuberculae are much larger than the spines on *Montipora grisea*, but smaller than on *Montipora capitata*.

--- SENTENCE 1465 ---
A colony of *Montipora tuberculosa.

--- SENTENCE 1466 ---
A closeup of *Montipora tuberculosa*.

--- SENTENCE 1467 ---
Tiny rings of polyp tentacles can be seen between the tuberculae.

#### 15.

--- SENTENCE 1468 ---
This coral forms encrusting colonies with small verrucae between the polyps.

--- SENTENCE 1469 ---
The verrucae are slightly larger than the size of the polyps, and tend to be taller than wide.

--- SENTENCE 1470 ---
Generally there is as much or more space between the bumps than the width of the bumps.

--- SENTENCE 1471 ---
Usually brown, rare, reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1472 ---
This species is common in Hawaii, where it also forms columns on colonies, and edges can be plates.

!

--- SENTENCE 1473 ---
A colony of *Montipora capitata.

--- SENTENCE 1474 ---
[](_page_89_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Montipora capitata*.

--- SENTENCE 1475 ---
*Montipora turgescens* Bernard, 1897

--- SENTENCE 1476 ---
This coral forms encrusting or massive colonies that are covered with small irregular lumps.

--- SENTENCE 1477 ---
The lumps are irregular in size, most around 3-5 mm diameter, and have polyps on them as well as between them.

--- SENTENCE 1478 ---
They look like lumps of the colony surface.

--- SENTENCE 1479 ---
Purple or brown, uncommon.

--- SENTENCE 1480 ---
See taxonomic note.

!

--- SENTENCE 1481 ---
A colony of *Montipora turgescens.* 

!

--- SENTENCE 1482 ---
A closeup of *Montipora turgescens*.

#### 17*.

--- SENTENCE 1483 ---
Montipora caliculata* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 1484 ---
This coral forms encrusting colonies which have thin sharp ridges between the polyps, and no bumps or spines.

--- SENTENCE 1485 ---
The thin ridges completely surround some polyps, but only partially surround others, and in some areas there may be no thin ridges.

--- SENTENCE 1486 ---
It differs from *M. venosa* by not having the ridges around all polyps.

--- SENTENCE 1487 ---
Usually brown, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1488 ---
[](_page_92_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Montiopra caliculata.

--- SENTENCE 1489 ---
A closeup of *Montipora caliculata*.

#### 18.

--- SENTENCE 1490 ---
*Montipora venosa* (Ehrenberg, 1834)

--- SENTENCE 1491 ---
This coral forms encrusting colonies with ridges between polyps, and no bumps or spines.

--- SENTENCE 1492 ---
The corallites are slightly recessed between the ridges.

--- SENTENCE 1493 ---
The ridges completely surround all polyps, unlike on *M. caliculata* or *M. vaughani*.

--- SENTENCE 1494 ---
The ridges are thicker than on *M. caliculata*.

--- SENTENCE 1495 ---
[](_page_94_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Montipora venosa*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1496 ---
A closeup of *Montipora venosa.* 

#### 19.

--- SENTENCE 1497 ---
*Montipora vaughani* Hoffmeister, 1925

This coral forms thick encrusting colonies that have thick rounded ridges between polyps, but several polyp cups are commonly in a row in a single valley surrounded by the ridges.

--- SENTENCE 1498 ---
Ridges are thicker than on *M. venosa* and *M. caliculata*, and often surround more than one polyp, ridges are not broken into sections as in *M. caliculata*.

--- SENTENCE 1499 ---
Brown, rare, reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1500 ---
See taxonomic note.

!

--- SENTENCE 1501 ---
[](_page_96_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Montipora vaughani*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1502 ---
A closeup of *Montipora vaughani*.

#### 20.

--- SENTENCE 1503 ---
*Montipora incrassita* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 1504 ---
This coral forms colonies with thick columns on its upper surface.

--- SENTENCE 1505 ---
Columns are about 3- 4 cm diameter, and around 5-15 cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 1506 ---
The edges of the colony may be extended in a short plate about 2-5 cm wide.

--- SENTENCE 1507 ---
Surfaces are irregularly bumpy, and the tops of columns may be white and are rounded, with recessed polyps.

--- SENTENCE 1508 ---
Colonies are brown or grey-green, so far only found on the outer slope on Rose Atoll.

--- SENTENCE 1509 ---
Colonies are exactly like those in Hawai'i, and the exact same type of colonies have also been seen by the author in New Caledonia and Papua New Guinea.

!

--- SENTENCE 1510 ---
[](_page_98_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Montipora incrassita*, showing both plate and columns.

!

--- SENTENCE 1511 ---
[](_page_99_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Montipora incrassita*, showing the tops of columns.

--- SENTENCE 1512 ---
### *Isopora* (pronounced eye-so-por-a)

*Isopora* used to be considered a sub-genus of *Acropora*, but the evidence mounted that they are actually a separate genus (Wallace et al. 2007), though closely related to *Acropora*.

--- SENTENCE 1513 ---
They do not have a single axial corallite and polyp.

--- SENTENCE 1514 ---
If they have a branch, there will be many corallites on the end.

--- SENTENCE 1515 ---
Several other features of their biology, such as brooding larvae and releasing them unlike *Acropora* which broadcasts eggs and sperm, indicate that this is a separate genus.

--- SENTENCE 1516 ---
One species here is encrusting, and another forms very thick branches, about as thick as a wrist, and the branches have very rounded, blunt ends, not sharp ends.

--- SENTENCE 1517 ---
The encrusting species dominates the upper reef slopes in some areas of the SW of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1518 ---
The branching form is uncommon most places.

--- SENTENCE 1519 ---
Colonies are light brown or reddish-brown.

--- SENTENCE 1520 ---
Encrusting colonies have smaller and smoother cylindrical projecting corallites than *Astreopora*, which is usually massive as well.

21.

--- SENTENCE 1521 ---
(Gardiner, 1898) (previously referred to as *Acropora* (*Isopora*) *crateriformis*)

--- SENTENCE 1522 ---
This coral forms encrusting sheets often with the lower edge raised as a plate with thin edges.

--- SENTENCE 1523 ---
The surface often has an irregular network of thin ridges.

--- SENTENCE 1524 ---
The surface is covered with small round tubular corallites close together.

--- SENTENCE 1525 ---
Corallites are smaller than on *I. palifera* and no branches are formed.

--- SENTENCE 1526 ---
Tan to reddish-brown sometimes with green polyps, most common on upper reef slopes, particularly on the southwest section of Tutuila, where it dominates at Leone.

!

--- SENTENCE 1527 ---
[](_page_100_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Isopora crateriformis*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1528 ---
[](_page_101_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Isopora crateriformis*.

22.

--- SENTENCE 1529 ---
*Isopora palifera* (Lamarck, 1816) (previously referred to as *Acropora* (*Isopora*) *palifera*)

--- SENTENCE 1530 ---
This coral forms clumps of very thick radiating branches about the diameter of a wrist and up to about a foot and a half long (many are shorter).

--- SENTENCE 1531 ---
Branches have rounded tips and no one axial corallite.

--- SENTENCE 1532 ---
Corallites are large and thick-walled.

--- SENTENCE 1533 ---
Brown, uncommon on most upper reef slopes and common on the outer reef flat and upper slope at Alofau.

!

--- SENTENCE 1534 ---
[](_page_102_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Isopora palifera*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1535 ---
[](_page_103_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of the side of a branch of an *Isopora palifera* colony, showing the corallites.

!

--- SENTENCE 1536 ---
A closeup of the ends of branches of *Isopora palifera*, showing the corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1537 ---
*Acropora* is the largest genus of hard corals with 165 species known, and it dominates many reefs around the world, making it very important.

--- SENTENCE 1538 ---
Some of the *Acropora* are the fasted growing corals on earth, and can grow 10 cm in a year.

--- SENTENCE 1539 ---
*Acropora* are also among the most easily damaged corals in the world.

--- SENTENCE 1540 ---
Many are relatively delicate branching forms which are easily broken in hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 1541 ---
They are also among the most sensitive to bleaching and easily killed that way.

--- SENTENCE 1542 ---
They are favorite food for crown-of-thorns starfish, and they are among the most susceptible to disease.

--- SENTENCE 1543 ---
There are only two coral species which the U.S. has declared as endangered, and both are *Acropora* and in Florida and the Caribbean.

--- SENTENCE 1544 ---
The thing that reduced them from dominating many reefs there to being uncommon was a disease.

--- SENTENCE 1545 ---
So their populations may be fairly unstable, growing fast and then being killed off, and repeating those cycles.

--- SENTENCE 1546 ---
Much of our rubble here consists of rounded sticks that came from staghorns, and pieces of table corals.

--- SENTENCE 1547 ---
*Acropora* is **always** branching, and branch tips usually look pretty sharp.

--- SENTENCE 1548 ---
They have small polyps about 1 mm diameter, and all have a single polyp at the end of a branch, which distinguishes them from all other corals exept one.

--- SENTENCE 1549 ---
The corallite on the tip of the branch is called the "axial" corallite, because it is like an axis on a wheel in that it is in the center of a circular structure, since the branch has a circular surface.

--- SENTENCE 1550 ---
The axial corallite is often larger than the radial corallites that are on the sides of the branches, though not always.

--- SENTENCE 1551 ---
Radial corallites are named that because they radiate on the circular branch surface.

--- SENTENCE 1552 ---
Common colony shapes include staghorns that look like deer antlers, tables that have a flat (but rough) upper surface, colonies with short branches that look like fingers and are called "digitate," bushy colonies of a wide variety of shapes, and everything in between.

--- SENTENCE 1553 ---
On table corals, the upper surface and edge of the colony have small branchlets, which have the axial polyp on the end.

--- SENTENCE 1554 ---
Colonies are often some shade of brown, but can be grey, or bright green, or other colors.

--- SENTENCE 1555 ---
No other genus has a corallite at the end of each branch.

--- SENTENCE 1556 ---
We will begin with staghorn species, then digitate, bushy, and tables.

--- SENTENCE 1557 ---
*Acropora muricata* (Linnaeus, 1758) "staghorn" (Previously referred to as *A. formosa*)

--- SENTENCE 1558 ---
This coral forms staghorn colonies in the backreef pools.

--- SENTENCE 1559 ---
Branches can be widely spaced or clustered tightly, and are about 1-1.5 cm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1560 ---
Radial corallites are small and tubular with the lower wall extending some.

--- SENTENCE 1561 ---
Thinner branches than *A. nobilis*, rougher branch surfaces than *A. pulchra*, only staghorn with blue tips.

--- SENTENCE 1562 ---
Brown with blue branch tips which are diagnostic here, co-dominates backreef pools, not found on slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1563 ---
[](_page_106_Picture_3.jpeg)

A thicket of *Acropora muricata*.

--- SENTENCE 1564 ---
Digital cameras do not capture the blue on branch tips well.

!

--- SENTENCE 1565 ---
[](_page_107_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora muricata*.

--- SENTENCE 1566 ---
*Acropora pulchra* (Brook, 1891) "staghorn"

This coral forms staghorn corals made of branches about 1-1.5 cm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1567 ---
Branches usually appear smooth, due to many short tentacles that cover most of the radial corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1568 ---
The larger radial corallites have an extended lower lip, and a few extend through the tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 1569 ---
Sometimes the tentacles are retracted making the corallites easily visible.

--- SENTENCE 1570 ---
Brown with white tips, common in backreef pools.

!

--- SENTENCE 1571 ---
*Acropora pulchra,* with tentacles extended, making it looks smooth with corallites hard to see.

--- SENTENCE 1572 ---
This is the more common appearance.

!

--- SENTENCE 1573 ---
[](_page_109_Picture_0.jpeg)

*Acropora pulchra* with the tentacles retracted.

--- SENTENCE 1574 ---
This is a less common appearance, but the different sizes of leafy corallites can be more easily seen.

#### 25.

--- SENTENCE 1575 ---
*Acropora intermedia* (Brook, 1891) (often referred to as *A. nobilis*)

--- SENTENCE 1576 ---
This coral forms staghorn colonies that have thicker branches back from the tips than either *A. muricata* or *A. pulchra*.

--- SENTENCE 1577 ---
Radial corallites near the branch tip are uniform, with openings at an angle pointing toward the tip, and farther down on the branch they become irregular in size and shape.

--- SENTENCE 1578 ---
Light brown to brown with white tips, uncommon in back reef pools and reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1579 ---
See taxonomic note.

!

--- SENTENCE 1580 ---
[](_page_110_Picture_3.jpeg)

Branches of *Acropora intermedia*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1581 ---
A closeup of *Acropora intermedia*.

#### 26.

--- SENTENCE 1582 ---
*Acropora aspera* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 1583 ---
This coral forms bushy colonies on reef flats.

--- SENTENCE 1584 ---
Branches are about 1 cm diameter and often radiate from the center of the colony or grow vertically.

--- SENTENCE 1585 ---
Radial corallites have a large lower "lip" or wall.

--- SENTENCE 1586 ---
If tentacles are extended they may obscure the smaller such lips.

--- SENTENCE 1587 ---
Brown, common on some reef flats, can form large patches, but easily killed by extra low tides.

!

--- SENTENCE 1588 ---
A colony of *Acropora aspera*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1589 ---
[](_page_113_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora aspera*.

--- SENTENCE 1590 ---
*Acropora austera* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 1591 ---
This coral forms branching staghorn-like colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1592 ---
Branches are closer together, more irregular and fused than most staghorns, and radial corallites are tubular and have thick walls.

--- SENTENCE 1593 ---
Brown or purple, uncommon, slopes and reef flats.

!

--- SENTENCE 1594 ---
[](_page_114_Picture_2.jpeg)

Two colonies of *Acropora austera*, one purple and the other brown.

!

--- SENTENCE 1595 ---
[](_page_115_Picture_0.jpeg)

Closeup of branches of *Acropora austera*, showing the corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1596 ---
*Acropora robusta* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 1597 ---
This coral forms large staghorn branches that can be quite thick at the base (hence the name).

--- SENTENCE 1598 ---
Branches are fairly widely spaced, very unform and regular, and may radiate nearly horizontal and then curve up.

--- SENTENCE 1599 ---
Radial corallites are small and the opening is at an angle pointing toward the tip.

--- SENTENCE 1600 ---
A few longer more tubular corallites may be at the tip.

--- SENTENCE 1601 ---
Brown, grey, or bright green, uncommon, prefers near the reef crest.

--- SENTENCE 1602 ---
On reef flats the branches may be short, stubby and vertical.

!

--- SENTENCE 1603 ---
[](_page_116_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora robusta*.

--- SENTENCE 1604 ---
Notice the upward curving branches with the even tapering.

!

--- SENTENCE 1605 ---
[](_page_117_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora robusta*.

--- SENTENCE 1606 ---
Notice the leaning corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1607 ---
*Acropora palmerae* Wells, 1954

--- SENTENCE 1608 ---
This coral forms thin encrusting colonies which may have a few bumps or stubby branches.

--- SENTENCE 1609 ---
The colony is covered with little radial corallites that are short and tubular and have angled openings that point in various directions.

--- SENTENCE 1610 ---
Brown or bright green, rare, on reef flat or upper reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 1611 ---
See taxonomic note.

!

--- SENTENCE 1612 ---
A colony of *Acropora palmerae*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1613 ---
[](_page_119_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora palmerae*.

--- SENTENCE 1614 ---
*Acropora abrotanoides* (Lamarck, 1816)

--- SENTENCE 1615 ---
This coral forms large colonies of sturdy staghorn-like branches, colonies are often 1-2 m diameter and branches 3-4 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1616 ---
Most branches extend either near vertically or near horizontally, and often curve irregularly.

--- SENTENCE 1617 ---
Horizontal branches often end in a horizontal fan of branch tips.

--- SENTENCE 1618 ---
Corallites near the branch tips are long and tubular and inclined toward the point.

--- SENTENCE 1619 ---
Grey-brown, fairly common above 10 m depth on slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1620 ---
[](_page_120_Picture_2.jpeg)

Part of a colony of *Acropora abrotanoides* showing the vertical branches and the horizontal branches that tend to fuse and produce fans at their ends.

!

--- SENTENCE 1621 ---
[](_page_121_Picture_0.jpeg)

A fan of branch tips produced at the end of a horizontal branch on *A. abrotanoides*.

--- SENTENCE 1622 ---
Notice the relatively long tubular radial corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1623 ---
*Acropora globiceps* (Dana 1846)

(Probably referred to as *A. humilis* previously, which it resembles closely except *A. humilis* has a large, dome-shaped axial corallite.)

--- SENTENCE 1624 ---
This coral forms digitate colonies with finger shaped radiating branches.

--- SENTENCE 1625 ---
The axial corallite is a small short raised tube.

--- SENTENCE 1626 ---
Radial corallites are tubular, may have upward facing openings, are nearly uniform in size, and are often in rows.

--- SENTENCE 1627 ---
Brown or fluorescent green, common, upper reef slope.

!

--- SENTENCE 1628 ---
[](_page_122_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora globiceps*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1629 ---
[](_page_123_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora globiceps*.

--- SENTENCE 1630 ---
Notice the small tubular axial corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1631 ---
*Acropora gemmifera* (Brook, 1892)

--- SENTENCE 1632 ---
This coral forms digitate colonies with moderate length branches that taper.

--- SENTENCE 1633 ---
Radial corallites are in rows and have large lower lips and increase in size down the branch.

--- SENTENCE 1634 ---
Smaller corallites may be seen between the larger ones.

--- SENTENCE 1635 ---
Brown, rare, at the Rainmaker on the crest and backreef pool.

!

--- SENTENCE 1636 ---
[](_page_124_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora gemmifera*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1637 ---
[](_page_125_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora gemmifera*.

--- SENTENCE 1638 ---
*Acropora monticulosa* (Brüggemann, 1879)

--- SENTENCE 1639 ---
This coral forms flat digitate colonies with short branches.

--- SENTENCE 1640 ---
Branches very strongly taper and form short cones unlike other digitate species.

--- SENTENCE 1641 ---
The axial corallite is small, as are the radials.

--- SENTENCE 1642 ---
Radials are tubular with a projecting lower lip.

--- SENTENCE 1643 ---
Brown to yellow, uncommon.

!

--- SENTENCE 1644 ---
[](_page_126_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora monticulosa*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1645 ---
[](_page_127_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora monticulosa*.

#### 34.

--- SENTENCE 1646 ---
*Acropora digitifera* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 1647 ---
This coral forms digitate colonies with short branches, near the reef crest.

--- SENTENCE 1648 ---
Branches taper slightly but have a strongly tapered tip with a small tubular axial.

--- SENTENCE 1649 ---
Radial corallites appear to have large lower lips from above, and in some colonies a black dot is in each radial.

--- SENTENCE 1650 ---
Branches are shorter than most digitate species, and do not taper evenly like a cone as in *A. monticulosa*.

--- SENTENCE 1651 ---
Yellow-brown and may have purple tints, common on reef crests only.

!

--- SENTENCE 1652 ---
[](_page_128_Picture_2.jpeg)

Three colonies of *Acropora digitifera*.

--- SENTENCE 1653 ---
Usually they are closer to the substrate than these colonies.

!

--- SENTENCE 1654 ---
[](_page_129_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora digitifera*.

--- SENTENCE 1655 ---
*Acropora cophodactyla* (Brook, 1842)

--- SENTENCE 1656 ---
This coral forms digitate colonies on the reef flat near the crest.

--- SENTENCE 1657 ---
Branches radiate, are finger-shaped, taper, and have a large tapering axial corallite that is bare for a short ways on its side.

--- SENTENCE 1658 ---
Reddish-brown, brown or tan, uncommon most places, mostly reef flats.

!

--- SENTENCE 1659 ---
[](_page_130_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora cophodactyla*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1660 ---
[](_page_131_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora cophodactyla*.

--- SENTENCE 1661 ---
Acropora aculeus* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 1662 ---
This coral forms small low cushions, about a foot (30 cm) in diameter, and a couple inches (4 cm) tall.

--- SENTENCE 1663 ---
Branchlets are relatively thin and tapering, and are vertical except near the edge of the colony where they point outward.

--- SENTENCE 1664 ---
Corallites on the sides of branches are short and pressed against the side of the branch pointing toward the tip.

--- SENTENCE 1665 ---
Light pinkpurple, rare, in pools.

!

--- SENTENCE 1666 ---
[](_page_132_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora aculeus*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1667 ---
A closeup of *Acropora aculeus*.

#### 37.

--- SENTENCE 1668 ---
*Acropora cerealis* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 1669 ---
This coral forms small bushes of upright branches.

--- SENTENCE 1670 ---
Branches are thin but have many long tubular corallites, so the corallites make up most of the effective diameter of the branch.

--- SENTENCE 1671 ---
Brown with light branch tips, uncommon.

!

--- SENTENCE 1672 ---
[](_page_134_Picture_2.jpeg)

--- SENTENCE 1673 ---

#### 38.

--- SENTENCE 1674 ---
*Acropora chesterfieldensis* Veron and Wallace, 1985

--- SENTENCE 1675 ---
This coral is digitate with upright branches.

--- SENTENCE 1676 ---
Branches are long and thin, and parallel with not much side branching.

--- SENTENCE 1677 ---
Corallites on the branch sides are uniform, appressed, and some are in rows.

--- SENTENCE 1678 ---
Found in Ofu backreef pools.

!

--- SENTENCE 1679 ---
[](_page_136_Picture_2.jpeg)

--- SENTENCE 1680 ---

--- SENTENCE 1681 ---
*Acropora insignis* Nemenzo, 1967

--- SENTENCE 1682 ---
This coral forms small bushy colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1683 ---
The radial corallites on branch sides are tubular and slanted strongly toward the end of the branch.

--- SENTENCE 1684 ---
The radial corallites are colored and contrast strongly with the white of the branch.

--- SENTENCE 1685 ---
Brown or green, rare.

!

--- SENTENCE 1686 ---
A colony of *Acropora insignis*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1687 ---
[](_page_139_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora insignis*.

--- SENTENCE 1688 ---
*Acropora nana* (Studer, 1878)

--- SENTENCE 1689 ---
This coral forms small bushes of thin branches on and near the reef crest.

--- SENTENCE 1690 ---
Branches are about pencil diameter, and usually are vertical or radiate from the center.

--- SENTENCE 1691 ---
Most radial corallites are fused to the branch (appressed) and point toward the end, though some are tubular and project.

--- SENTENCE 1692 ---
Reddish-brown, rarely with light green polyps, usually dominates the reef crest, rare elsewhere.

!

--- SENTENCE 1693 ---
[](_page_140_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora nana*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1694 ---
[](_page_141_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora nana*.

--- SENTENCE 1695 ---
*Acropora pagoensis* Hoffmeister, 1925

--- SENTENCE 1696 ---
This coral forms dense scraggly branching colonies with branches about 1 cm thick, and has many side branches.

--- SENTENCE 1697 ---
Radial corallites have sharp edges and most are short and have an opening slanted toward the branch tip.

--- SENTENCE 1698 ---
Some are longer and tubular and becoming axial corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1699 ---
Usually light reddish brown, uncommon on reef slopes and common on the reef flat at Alofau.

--- SENTENCE 1700 ---
I believe these are the first color photos of living colonies ever shown in a guide.

--- SENTENCE 1701 ---
This species is not recognized in any modern taxonomic works.

--- SENTENCE 1702 ---
See taxonomic note.

!

--- SENTENCE 1703 ---
[](_page_142_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora pagoensis*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1704 ---
[](_page_143_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora pagoensis*.

--- SENTENCE 1705 ---
*Acropora speciosa* (Quelch, 1886) sensu Wallace (1999)

--- SENTENCE 1706 ---
This coral forms small flat-topped colonies with scraggly branchlets and many long smooth incipient axials and axials.

--- SENTENCE 1707 ---
Many radials are similar.

--- SENTENCE 1708 ---
All have blunt ends with thick walls.

--- SENTENCE 1709 ---
Axials and incipient axials are similar to *A. aculeus* but thinner.

--- SENTENCE 1710 ---
Usually rust colored, uncommon to rare, lower reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1711 ---
[](_page_144_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora speciosa*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1712 ---
[](_page_145_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora speciosa*.

--- SENTENCE 1713 ---
*Acropora jacquelineae* Wallace, 1994

--- SENTENCE 1714 ---
This coral forms small flat-topped colonies with many thin long axial and incipient axial corallites growing upward.

--- SENTENCE 1715 ---
Axials and incipient axials have thin walls and are only about 1 mm thick.

--- SENTENCE 1716 ---
This species is similar to *A. speciosa*, but the axials and incipient axials are thinner.

--- SENTENCE 1717 ---
Found on lower reef slopes, rare.

--- SENTENCE 1718 ---
This species was proposed for listing as an endangered species.

!

--- SENTENCE 1719 ---
[](_page_146_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora jacquelineae*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1720 ---
[](_page_147_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup photo of *Acropora jacquelineae*.

--- SENTENCE 1721 ---
*Acropora verweyi* Veron & Wallace, 1984

--- SENTENCE 1722 ---
This coral forms small bushy colonies with radiating pencil-diameter branches.

--- SENTENCE 1723 ---
The axial corallites are very large and flat ended, having very thick walls and a small opening.

--- SENTENCE 1724 ---
Radial corallites are very uniform, short, thick walled, and pointing toward the branch tip.

--- SENTENCE 1725 ---
Brown with a yellow branch tip and white centers to radial corallites, rare except in the Ofu pools.

!

--- SENTENCE 1726 ---
[](_page_148_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora verweyi*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1727 ---
[](_page_149_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora verweyi*.

--- SENTENCE 1728 ---
*Acropora surculosa* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 1729 ---
This coral forms small cushions about 10-20 cm diameter, composed of vertical branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 1730 ---
Branchlets are close together, taper from a diameter of up to 1 cm near the base to a sharp tip only about 1 mm diameter, have little branching and little to no incipient axials, but can divide into two or more vertical branchlets that look like fused branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 1731 ---
Lower parts of branchlets have long thin tentacles out in the day.

--- SENTENCE 1732 ---
Green or reddish brown, uncommon to rare, upper reef slopes and sometimes reef flats or pools.

!

--- SENTENCE 1733 ---
[](_page_150_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Acropora surculosa*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1734 ---
A closeup of *Acropora surculosa*.

--- SENTENCE 1735 ---
This coral forms tables that can have a second smaller tier.

--- SENTENCE 1736 ---
The upper surface is composed of small vertical branchlets which taper strongly and which have radial corallites with projecting lower lips that can look like rose petals from above.

--- SENTENCE 1737 ---
Tentacles may be extended.

--- SENTENCE 1738 ---
Colonies in backreef pools may have longer branchlets that partly separate near the edge of the table.

--- SENTENCE 1739 ---
Reddish-brown, rarely green, common, dominates the center-west section of Fagatele Bay, most common on upper reef slope.

!

--- SENTENCE 1740 ---
[](_page_152_Picture_3.jpeg)

Colonies of *Acropora hyacinthus*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1741 ---
[](_page_153_Picture_0.jpeg)

A young colony of *Acropora hyacinthus*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1742 ---
[](_page_154_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Acropora hyacinthus*.

--- SENTENCE 1743 ---
This coral forms large tables which can sometimes have a smaller second tier.

--- SENTENCE 1744 ---
The upper surface is covered with a carpet of very small tubular vertical axial tips of small branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 1745 ---
Radials on the branchlets are elongated and point toward the tip, not flattened leaves as on *A. hyacinthus*.

--- SENTENCE 1746 ---
Grey-brown, uncommon, slopes and reef flats.

!

--- SENTENCE 1747 ---
A large colony of *Acropora cytherea*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1748 ---
[](_page_156_Picture_0.jpeg)

!

--- SENTENCE 1749 ---
[](_page_157_Picture_0.jpeg)

An extreme closeup of *Acropora cytherea*.

--- SENTENCE 1750 ---
This coral forms large tables, usually with just one tier.

--- SENTENCE 1751 ---
Branchlets are usually short and have many radiating long tubular corallites which are axials or incipient axials.

--- SENTENCE 1752 ---
Branchlet tips may point outward and upward or curve upward.

--- SENTENCE 1753 ---
Corallites are radiating and bushy instead of all vertical and close together as on *A. cytherea*.

--- SENTENCE 1754 ---
Brown, more common in deeper water.

--- SENTENCE 1757 ---
*Acropora clathrata* (Brook, 1891)

--- SENTENCE 1758 ---
"table coral"

This coral forms large table corals formed of branchlets that grow nearly horizontally, with their tips just above the plane of the table pointing outward.

--- SENTENCE 1759 ---
Branchlets retain a uniform diameter of about 1 cm over the entire table, and there are no small vertical branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 1760 ---
Grey, common especially on some offshore banks, present on slopes and a few are in pools.

### *Astreopora* (pronounced ass-tree-oh-por-a)

*Astreopora* is a medium size genus that usually has massive colonies or sometimes encrusting colonies, with small corallites that look a bit like little volcanoes.

--- SENTENCE 1764 ---
It is uncommon most places.

--- SENTENCE 1765 ---
Corallites are larger and spinier than on encrusting *Isopora* and it is usually massive not encrusting.

#### 50.

--- SENTENCE 1766 ---
This coral forms massive colonies with corallites that project and are uniform in size, with all projecting outward from the surface.

--- SENTENCE 1767 ---
Browns, greys, purples, uncommon, reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1770 ---
*Astreopora gracilis* Bernard, 1896

--- SENTENCE 1771 ---
This coral forms massive colonies with corallites that project different amounts and which are pointed in different directions, giving a rough, disorderly surface appearance.

--- SENTENCE 1772 ---
Brown to reddish brown, uncommon to rare.

This coral forms massive or encrusting colonies, and can have raised plate edges.

--- SENTENCE 1776 ---
Corallites are uniform in size, and most or all corallites are strongly tilted or inclined downward.

--- SENTENCE 1777 ---
Reddish color, uncommon.

--- SENTENCE 1798 ---
**Family Poritiidae** (pronounced por-eye-tid-ee) has several genera, some of which have very small corallites (*Porites* and *Stylaraea*), and others (*Alveopora* and *Goniopora*) which have corallites ranging from very small to just small.

--- SENTENCE 1799 ---
In the genera with very small corallites the polyps are very small and not extended, while in those with small corallites the polyps are very long thin columns which along with the tentacles can make the polyps look like daisies.

--- SENTENCE 1800 ---
In *Porites* and *Goniopora*, the septa fuse near the center of the corallite in distinctive patterns.

--- SENTENCE 1801 ---
### *Porites* (pronounced por-eye-tees)

*Porites* is the third largest genus in number of species following *Montipora*, and it produces the largest coral colonies in the world, including the giant at Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 1802 ---
These giants have records of the climate of the past hundreds of years in their skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1803 ---
Some species are massive, by which we mean rounded hemispheres, other species are branching or columnar, some form plates and some have both plates and branches or columns.

--- SENTENCE 1804 ---
Finger coral which co-dominates back reef pools is a *Porites* species.

--- SENTENCE 1805 ---
The second most common species on the slopes is a *Porites* species as well and has columns and plates.

--- SENTENCE 1806 ---
Some species are very difficult to tell apart, particularly the massive species.

--- SENTENCE 1807 ---
Corallites are tiny, about 1-2 mm diameter, and may give the surface a "cellular" look when examined closely.

--- SENTENCE 1808 ---
Polyps are tiny as well, and often aren't extended, though sometimes the tips of the tentacles give the surface a slight fuzzy look.

--- SENTENCE 1809 ---
*Porites* never has spines like *Montipora* and very few species have recessed corallites that can look like *Monitpora*.

--- SENTENCE 1810 ---
Polyps are smaller than *Goniopora* and *Alveopora* and not extended.

--- SENTENCE 1811 ---
We begin with branching species, then columnar species with basal plates, then plates, and end with massive (boulder) species.

--- SENTENCE 1812 ---
*Porites* are among the hardest of all corals to identify to species.

#### 56.

--- SENTENCE 1813 ---
*Porites cylindrica* Dana, 1846 "finger coral"

This coral forms colonies composed of finger-like branches with rounded tips.

--- SENTENCE 1814 ---
Colonies can be very large.

--- SENTENCE 1815 ---
Branches may be fuzy with tiny tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 1816 ---
Tan or yellow-green, codominates backreef pools, common on slopes in Vatia Bay, rare on outer slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1817 ---
[](_page_174_Picture_4.jpeg)

--- SENTENCE 1819 ---
*Porites annae* Crossland*,* 1952

--- SENTENCE 1820 ---
This coral forms small colonies with many lumpy irregular columns or branches.

--- SENTENCE 1821 ---
The upper ends of some or most columns are flattened and white.

--- SENTENCE 1822 ---
The sides of columns are covered with an even covering of light dots which are tufts of tiny tentacles- each dot is the center of a polyp.

--- SENTENCE 1823 ---
Dark brown, uncommon in lagoons.

!

--- SENTENCE 1824 ---

--- SENTENCE 1826 ---
*Porites lichen* Dana, 1846

--- SENTENCE 1827 ---
This coral on reef slopes forms thin plates with rounded knobs that can grow into fingerlike columns, which usually are not fused and typically are different heights.

--- SENTENCE 1828 ---
The surface is fuzzy with tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 1829 ---
In backreef pools, the knobs and columns are close together or fused together with just a small plate at the lower edge.

--- SENTENCE 1830 ---
Some corallites are in rows, especially on plates.

--- SENTENCE 1831 ---
Grey or brown on slopes, green or brown in pools, common only on the east side of Olosega and Ofu pools.

Brown with white column tops, white ridges, and tiny white dots which are polyps, less common green, rarely blue, abundant many places on reef slopes.
