### *Stylaraea* (pronounced sty-la-ree-a)

*Stylaraea* is a genus with only one species, which is so small and hidden, few people have ever seen it.

--- SENTENCE 1884 ---
It forms the smallest colonial, zooxanthellate (having algae) colonies of any coral in the world.

--- SENTENCE 1885 ---
Many colonies are only about 5 mm diameter, and the largest colonies anywhere are probably no more than 2 cm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1886 ---
They are very cryptic (=hidden or hard to see).

--- SENTENCE 1887 ---
They only live on reef flats on rubble.

--- SENTENCE 1888 ---
You really have to know what you are looking for to find them, and it may take a lot of patience looking at rubble to find them.

--- SENTENCE 1889 ---
They look like tiny *Porites* colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1890 ---
The genus was first described by Linneus in 1758, which is when species were first described in the system we use now.

--- SENTENCE 1891 ---
But no one knew how to find them reliably until Richard Randall discovered how in Guam.

--- SENTENCE 1892 ---
Later, he came here to American Samoa and found them, and taught Chuck Birkeland how to find them, who taught me.

--- SENTENCE 1893 ---
If you learn to find them, you will be one of only a handful of people in the world who know how.

--- SENTENCE 1894 ---
American Samoa is the easternmost place they are known in the world, but if someone who knows how to find

them goes farther east, they may find them.

--- SENTENCE 1895 ---
I recently found them on Ofu, the easternmost finding yet.

--- SENTENCE 1896 ---
*Stylaraea punctata* (Linneaus, 1758)

--- SENTENCE 1897 ---
This coral forms tiny colonies on rubble on reef flats.

--- SENTENCE 1898 ---
Colonies are about 3-10 mm diameter, and are cushion-shaped, and look just like *Porites*.

--- SENTENCE 1899 ---
Tan, may have green polyp centers, rare, found so far only on the reef flats at Aua and Alofau on Tutuila, and Vaoto pool on Ofu.

--- SENTENCE 1900 ---
You must search persistently to find this coral.

!

--- SENTENCE 1901 ---
[](_page_197_Picture_3.jpeg)

A closeup of a colony of *Stylaraea punctata*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1902 ---
[](_page_198_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of a colony of *Styaraea puncata* with green polyp centers.

--- SENTENCE 1903 ---
### *Alveopora* (pronounced al-vee-oh-por-a)

--- SENTENCE 1904 ---
*Alveopora* looks much like *Goniopora*, with polyps that look a bit like daisies.

--- SENTENCE 1905 ---
However, the skeleton is soft because it is made of very thin tiny rods fused together.

--- SENTENCE 1906 ---
So a thumbnail sinks easily into the skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 1907 ---
Also, it has 12 tentacles instead of the 24 tentacles on *Goniopora*, though it is very hard to tell the difference underwater since it just looks like a lot of tentacles on both.

--- SENTENCE 1908 ---
There is only one common species in American Samoa, which forms clusters of small grey or brown lumps, usually in backreef pools.

--- SENTENCE 1909 ---
*Alveopora tizardi*

This coral forms clusters of knobs or single knobs, about 2-5 cm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1910 ---
Colonies are covered with a fuzz of polyp tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 1911 ---
The polyp column under the tentacles may be seen sometimes.

--- SENTENCE 1912 ---
Grey, common in backreef pools but not on slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1913 ---
[](_page_199_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Alveopora tizardi* or *A. exelsa*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1914 ---
[](_page_200_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Alveopora tizardi/exelsa*.

--- SENTENCE 1915 ---
### *Goniopora* (pronounced go-nee-oh-por-a)

*Goniopora* has tall, daisy-like polyps that are usually extended during the day.

--- SENTENCE 1916 ---
It has 24 tentacles while *

--- SENTENCE 1917 ---
Alveopora* has just 12 tentacles, and it has a solid hard skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 1918 ---
Corallites are usually about 2-4 mm diameter, and polyps a similar diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1919 ---
In some species the polyps can extend as far as about 10 cm, but in others they may only extend 2-5 mm, and in one they don't extend at all.

--- SENTENCE 1920 ---
The polyps look a bit like daisies, with a tall thin stem (better called a column) and a ring of tentacles at the top like the petals of the flower.

--- SENTENCE 1921 ---
Colonies can be encrusting, massive, or columns.

--- SENTENCE 1922 ---
The skeleton is relatively hard, if you try to push your thumbnail into it, it won't go into it, unlike *Alveopora*, which can otherwise look similar.

--- SENTENCE 1923 ---
*Goniopora* always has exactly 24 tentacles, but you can't count that many tentacles underwater, it is even hard in a photo.

--- SENTENCE 1924 ---
*Goniopora columna* Dana, 1846

--- SENTENCE 1925 ---
This coral forms large thick columns close together that may appear to be a solid colony several feet tall and wide.

--- SENTENCE 1926 ---
Polyps are daisy-like and the column or "stem" is easily seen, tentacles are very short and fat.

--- SENTENCE 1927 ---
Grey with white polyp centers, rare, seen only at Utelei.

!

--- SENTENCE 1928 ---
[](_page_201_Picture_4.jpeg)

Colonies of *Goniopora columna*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1929 ---
[](_page_202_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of the polyps of *Goniopora columna*.

--- SENTENCE 1930 ---
*Goniopora fruiticosa* Saville-Kent, 1893

This coral forms large encrusting sheets which may have some lumps which may be fused to various degrees.

--- SENTENCE 1931 ---
Polyps are small and short.

--- SENTENCE 1932 ---
Reddish-brown with white tentacles, uncommon on slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1933 ---
[](_page_203_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Goniopora fruiticosa*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1934 ---
[](_page_204_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Goniopora fruiticosa* polyps.

--- SENTENCE 1935 ---
**Family Siderastraeadae** (sigh-der-ass-tree-id-ee) has two genera in the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 1936 ---
They have small corallites and the septa fuse near the center of the corallite.

### *Psammocora* (pronounced sam-oh-kor-a)

*Psammocora* forms colonies that can be encrusting, small massive colonies, or branching.

--- SENTENCE 1937 ---
In some species the corallites are too tiny to be seen underwater.

--- SENTENCE 1938 ---
In the small massive species the surface is commonly covered with ridges that come to a sharp upper edge and which enclose a funnel-shaped corallite which has a black dot in the center.

--- SENTENCE 1939 ---
In the encrusting species there are rounded winding ridges that usually don't intersect, and no sign of septa or corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1940 ---
In the branching species, the branch surfaces appear smooth with no corallites visible.

--- SENTENCE 1941 ---
Uncommon most places, the branching species can be abundant on some reef flats.

--- SENTENCE 1942 ---
Encrusting colonies with ridges can look like one of the species of *Pavona* (*Pavona varians*) which is common, but the *Psammocora* has a smooth looking surface while the *Pavona* has little septa that can be seen on ridge sides.

#### 70.

--- SENTENCE 1943 ---
*Psammocora contigua* (Esper, 1797)

--- SENTENCE 1944 ---
This coral forms small branching or lumpy colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1945 ---
Branches or lumps are about 2 cm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1946 ---
Branches or columns are highly irregular and lumpy.

--- SENTENCE 1947 ---
Surfaces are nearly smooth.

--- SENTENCE 1948 ---
Brown, sometimes green, common in some back-reef pool areas, rare elsewhere.

!

--- SENTENCE 1949 ---
[](_page_205_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Psammocora contigua*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1950 ---
[](_page_206_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Psammocora contingua*.

--- SENTENCE 1951 ---
*Psammocora haimeana* Milne Edwards & Haime, 1851

--- SENTENCE 1952 ---
This coral forms small encrusting, massive or lumpy colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1953 ---
The surface has high thin ridges between corallites or groups of corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1954 ---
Corallite centers are hard to see.

--- SENTENCE 1955 ---
Brown, rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1956 ---
[](_page_207_Picture_2.jpeg)

A colony of *Psammocora haimeana*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1957 ---
[](_page_208_Picture_0.jpeg)

A colony of *Psammocora haimeana*.

--- SENTENCE 1958 ---
In some colonies, the surface ridges between corallites or groups of corallites are not as tall and thin as shown above, and the corallite centers are easily seen black dots.

--- SENTENCE 1959 ---
This has been called *Psammocora profundacella* Gardiner, 1898, but a recent study (Stefani et al. 2008) has concluded that it is not a separate species.

--- SENTENCE 1960 ---
The name *Psammocora haimeana* is older, so it has prescedence.

--- SENTENCE 1961 ---
Photos of colonies with lower ridges and more distinct colony centers are shown below.

--- SENTENCE 1965 ---
This coral forms encrusting colonies covered with small irregular winding ridges or bumps.

--- SENTENCE 1966 ---
Usually no finer features can be seen, unlike on *Pavona varians*.

--- SENTENCE 1967 ---
Green, brown, grey, uncommon, reef slopes.

### *Coscinaraea* (pronounced ko-sin-a-ree-a)

*Coscinaraea* forms encrusting or massive colonies with quite small winding ridges that enclose spaces.

--- SENTENCE 1970 ---
The ridges are somewhat square-topped.

--- SENTENCE 1971 ---
Colonies are always brown.

--- SENTENCE 1972 ---
Corallites are deep in the valleys and can't be seen.

--- SENTENCE 1973 ---
*Coscinaraea* might look a bit like *Favites*, but the ridges and valleys are smaller.

#### 73.

--- SENTENCE 1974 ---
*Coscinaraea columna* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 1975 ---
This coral forms massive or encrusting colonies covered with small winding ridges.

--- SENTENCE 1976 ---
The ridges may enclose single corallites or several corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1977 ---
Small spines may be visible on the ridges.

--- SENTENCE 1978 ---
Brown, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1979 ---
[](_page_213_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Coscinaraea columna*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1980 ---
[](_page_214_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of the surface of *Coscinaraea columna*.

--- SENTENCE 1981 ---
**Family Agariciidae** (pronounced ag-a-ree-see-id-ee) has several medium-sized genera plus smaller genera.

--- SENTENCE 1982 ---
Many but not all species lack walls to the corallites, that is, the corallite is a flat surface which extends between corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1983 ---
As a result, septa in the center of the corallite extend out from the corallite center across the flat surface as costae to become septa in the adjacent corallite's center, and so they are called "septo-costae."

--- SENTENCE 1984 ---
Colonies are commonly plates or laterally flattened massives.

### *Gardinoseris* (pronounced gar-din-ner-oh-seer-is)

--- SENTENCE 1985 ---
*Gardinoseris* is a genus with only one species.

--- SENTENCE 1986 ---
It forms massive colonies that have small corallites about 1/8 inch diameter that are separated by a very sharp ridge and no groove.

--- SENTENCE 1987 ---
Septa inside the corallite are so small and close together and all the same size so that they appear to be a very smooth surface.

--- SENTENCE 1988 ---
Redish-brown to brown, rare.

--- SENTENCE 1989 ---
Smaller corallites and sharper, smoother ridges than *Favites* or *Goniastrea*.

#### 74.

--- SENTENCE 1990 ---
*Gardineroseris planulata* Dana, 1846

--- SENTENCE 1991 ---
This coral forms encrusting or massive colonies, the surface of which is covered with tall sharp ridges that enclose corallites in a honey-comb like fashion.

--- SENTENCE 1992 ---
Septa are too small to be seen underwater, any ridges visible inside corallites are where the corallites are dividing.

--- SENTENCE 1993 ---
Brown, uncommon to rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 1994 ---
[](_page_215_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Gardineroseris planulata*.

!

--- SENTENCE 1995 ---
[](_page_216_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Gardineroseris planulata*.

--- SENTENCE 1996 ---
### *Leptoseris* (pronounced lep-toe-seer-is)

--- SENTENCE 1997 ---
*Leptoseris* usually forms thin plates, but some species are encrusting.

--- SENTENCE 1998 ---
They tend to be yellow-brown, and to be in lower-light conditions such as deeper water.

--- SENTENCE 1999 ---
Corallites in some cases are raised as round cushions, and may point outwards.

--- SENTENCE 2000 ---
In one of the most common species corallites are recessed and can't be seen, with winding ridges between the corallites giving a very wrinkled appearance.

--- SENTENCE 2001 ---
The septae continue as costae like in *Pavona*.

--- SENTENCE 2002 ---
Browns, light yellows to cream.

--- SENTENCE 2003 ---
The wrinkled colonies have smooth surfaces on the wrinkles and the wrinkles are thinner than ridges on *Favites*.

--- SENTENCE 2004 ---
On colonies with outward leaning cushion-shapped corallites, the plates are smoother and corallites smaller than on *Mycedium*.

#### 75.

--- SENTENCE 2005 ---
*Leptoseris scabra* Vaughan, 1907

--- SENTENCE 2006 ---
This coral forms small thin plates deeper on reef slopes in shaded locations.

--- SENTENCE 2007 ---
Corallites are small raised lumps that are tilted and point toward the edge of the plate.

--- SENTENCE 2008 ---
The plate has many tiny radiating ridges that are hard to see.

--- SENTENCE 2009 ---
Yellow to brown, fairly common on lower reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2010 ---
[](_page_217_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Leptoseris scabra*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2011 ---
A closeup of *Leptoseris scabra*.

#### 76.

--- SENTENCE 2012 ---
*Leptoseris explanata* Yabe & Sugiyama, 1941

This coral forms small encrusting colonies with plate edges.

--- SENTENCE 2013 ---
Larger colonies form plates.

--- SENTENCE 2014 ---
The surface is covered with small obvious ridges that radiate from corallite centers.

--- SENTENCE 2015 ---
The ridges are more obvious than on other similar *Leptoseris*.

--- SENTENCE 2016 ---
Reddish-brown, rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2017 ---

!

--- SENTENCE 2018 ---
*Leptoseris foliosa* Dineson, 1980

This coral forms small encrusting colonies that may have plate edges.

--- SENTENCE 2019 ---
The surface is covered with smooth rounded ridges that are a variety of sizes.

--- SENTENCE 2020 ---
Ridges are separated by little black holes which are the corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2021 ---
Yellow to brown, rare, reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2024 ---

--- SENTENCE 2025 ---
This coral forms small encrusting colonies covered with small irregular bumps.

--- SENTENCE 2026 ---
Some bumps may form partial circles, surrounding corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2027 ---
Brown to green, rare, reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 2030 ---
This coral forms encrusting colonies covered with winding intersecting ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2031 ---
Ridges wind and intersect in a chaotic fashion.

--- SENTENCE 2032 ---
The corallites are between the ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2033 ---
The ridges are larger on some colonies than on others.

--- SENTENCE 2034 ---
Brown or yellow, sometimes with green polyps, uncommon to common, reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2037 ---

--- SENTENCE 2038 ---
This coral forms plates that may be in tiers.

--- SENTENCE 2039 ---
The upper surfaces of the plates are covered with intersecting ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2040 ---
Near the edge of plates the largest ridges radiate and other ridges run at right angles forming rectangles.

--- SENTENCE 2045 ---
### *Pavona* (pronounced pa-voh-na)

--- SENTENCE 2046 ---
*Pavona* has a variety of colony forms.

--- SENTENCE 2047 ---
Some are encrusting, some form ridges the shape of pork chops, some have thick branches, some have lumps or columns, and some are plates.

--- SENTENCE 2048 ---
Among the plate colonies, a couple species have vertical plates which intersect, and the intersections between plates may be far apart so the plates are obvious, or very close together making it a crinkly mass.

--- SENTENCE 2049 ---
*Pavona* usually has corallites that are nearly flush with the surface, and the septa radiating from one tiny mouth run to the neighboring corallite and in to its mouth.

--- SENTENCE 2050 ---
So there is no wall on the corallite, and the septa continue as costae with no boundary between the two, so they are called septo-costae.

--- SENTENCE 2051 ---
Corallites and septae are usually small, and can be tiny so they are hard to see.

--- SENTENCE 2052 ---
In one species they are covered with little white tentacles so you can't see them.

--- SENTENCE 2053 ---
It is somewhat similar to *Leptoseris* but rarely forms thin plates.

--- SENTENCE 2054 ---
*Pavona duerdeni* Vaughan, 1907

--- SENTENCE 2055 ---
This coral forms thick vertical plates that look a little like pork chops on edge, though small colonies may be a lump.

--- SENTENCE 2056 ---
Surfaces are nearly smooth, with tiny star-like corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2057 ---
Tan, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2058 ---
[](_page_229_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Pavoa duerdeni*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2059 ---
[](_page_230_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closup of Pavona duerdeni.

--- SENTENCE 2060 ---
*Pavona diffluens* (Lamarck, 1816)

--- SENTENCE 2061 ---
This coral forms small porkchop-shaped colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2062 ---
Corallites are relatively large, much larger than *P. durdeni*, but tentacles are not extended as on *P. gigantea*, and colonies are smaller than both.

--- SENTENCE 2063 ---
Uncommon on Rose Atoll, rare on Tutuila, reef slopes grey or rust color.

--- SENTENCE 2064 ---
Thought to exist only in the Red Sea, it was discovered here by Richard Randall, a long ways from the Red Sea.

!

--- SENTENCE 2065 ---
[](_page_231_Picture_2.jpeg)

--- SENTENCE 2066 ---

--- SENTENCE 2067 ---
*Pavona gigantea* Verrill, 1896

--- SENTENCE 2068 ---
This coral forms clusters of stout columns and thick plates.

--- SENTENCE 2069 ---
Clusters are usually just a half meter or less in diameter, but rarely can reach two meters or more in diameter and height.

--- SENTENCE 2070 ---
The surface is often obscured by tentacles, which may be in little rings.

--- SENTENCE 2071 ---
Corallites are crowded together.

--- SENTENCE 2072 ---
Grey with white tentacles, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2076 ---
This coral forms thin encrusting sheets or plates.

--- SENTENCE 2077 ---
Corallites can be seen as a ring of tiny bumps.

--- SENTENCE 2078 ---
Corallites are essentially the same as on lower plate edges of *Pavona gigantea* colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2079 ---
Brown to grey, rare, reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2121 ---
Plates turn and twist in many directions.

--- SENTENCE 2122 ---
Much smaller plates than P. decussata.

--- SENTENCE 2123 ---
Yellow-brown, common on parts of reef flats, especially at Coconut Point.

### *Pachyseris* (pronounced pak-ee-seer-is)

--- SENTENCE 2127 ---
*Pachyseris* forms plates or an encrusting base with flattened columns.

--- SENTENCE 2128 ---
The plates have very regular, smooth ridges that run in parallel concentric circles.

--- SENTENCE 2129 ---
The ridges are around 3 mm wide and have a pyramid-shaped cross section.

--- SENTENCE 2130 ---
Colonies with columns on an encrusting base have similar ridges, but they wind around each other a lot.

--- SENTENCE 2131 ---
Fagatele Bay has a huge colony of the columnar species, by far the largest the author has ever seen.

--- SENTENCE 2132 ---
The plate colonies are grey, the columns may be grey or brown, plates seem to like it a bit deeper than the columns.

--- SENTENCE 2133 ---
*Pachyseris* is the only genus with concentric ridges that are triangular in cross section.

#### 92.

--- SENTENCE 2134 ---
This coral forms encrusting corals with vertical plates and/or paddles on them.

--- SENTENCE 2135 ---
The surface is covered with small ridges, many of which wind around each other.

--- SENTENCE 2136 ---
This is the only *Pachyseris* species that has ridges on both sides of paddles and plates.

--- SENTENCE 2137 ---
Grey or brown, reef slopes, rare except in Fagatele Bay where there is a huge colony that may be one of the largest known anywhere, and many smaller colonies.

!

--- SENTENCE 2138 ---
[](_page_252_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Pachyseris rugosa*.

--- SENTENCE 2139 ---
*Pachyseris speciosa* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 2140 ---
This coral forms thin nearly horizontal plates covered with small concentric ridges which are very uniform in height.

--- SENTENCE 2141 ---
The undersides of plates are smooth.

--- SENTENCE 2142 ---
Grey or brown, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2143 ---
[](_page_253_Picture_2.jpeg)

--- SENTENCE 2144 ---
*Pachyseris gemmae* Nemenzo, 1955

--- SENTENCE 2145 ---
This coral forms thin plates covered with small concentric ridges which vary in height along their length like waves.

--- SENTENCE 2146 ---
Grey or brown, rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2147 ---

--- SENTENCE 2148 ---
**Family Fungiidae** (pronounced fun-dgee-id-ee) are often solitary (having only a single corallite) and unattached, though some are colonial and some are attached.

--- SENTENCE 2149 ---
The visible septa on solitary species make them look like overturned mushroom caps, hence the common name "mushroom corals.

--- SENTENCE 2150 ---
"

### *Cycloseris* (pronounced sigh-klo-seer-is)

--- SENTENCE 2151 ---
*Cycloseris* forms small single (solitary) corallites that are not attached and look like an overturned mushroom cap on the top.

--- SENTENCE 2152 ---
They are nearly smooth on the bottom, with small uniform granules like on sandpaper.

--- SENTENCE 2153 ---
The nearly smooth underside distinguishes it from small (young) individuals of *Fungia*, which have spines on the underside.

95.

--- SENTENCE 2154 ---
*Cycloseris costulata* Ortmann, 1889 "mushroom coral"

This coral forms small discs about 5 cm diameter or less in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2155 ---
The center may be raised some, and the ridges (septa) are thick near the center.

--- SENTENCE 2156 ---
Brown, very rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2157 ---
### *Fungia* (pronounced fun-dgee-a)

*Fungia* is a large genus of corals in which almost all species have just one corallite and polyp, and all species are unattached.

--- SENTENCE 2158 ---
All of ours have just one corallite.

--- SENTENCE 2159 ---
Corals with only one polyp are called "solitary."

--- SENTENCE 2160 ---
They form discs or ovals, up to about a foot in diameter though most commonly about 3-6 inches diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2161 ---
You can easily pick them up since they are not attached.

--- SENTENCE 2162 ---
One surface has a slit in the center which is the mouth, and sharp, thin, radiating ridges from the mouth out to the edge of the coral.

--- SENTENCE 2163 ---
The ridges are septa.

--- SENTENCE 2164 ---
This is the oral surface since the mouth is on that side.

--- SENTENCE 2165 ---
Usually the oral surface is facing up, but not always, and it doesn't much hurt for it to be facing down.

--- SENTENCE 2166 ---
The other surface has rows of radiating spines or granules on it and no mouth-slit.

--- SENTENCE 2167 ---
They are commonly called "mushroom corals" because the oral surface with the radiating ridges looks a bit like the underside of a mushroom cap.

--- SENTENCE 2168 ---
In one species here, soft tentacles may extend for up to about a quarter inch.

--- SENTENCE 2169 ---
*Fungia* are usually brown, but may have a green or yellow edge, or the whole coral may be purple.

--- SENTENCE 2170 ---
Those with tentacles may be yellow with green tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 2171 ---
Uncommon to very abundant.

--- SENTENCE 2172 ---
Most abundant at the bottom of some slopes where they accumulate as waves move them down slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2173 ---
*Fungia* are circular and have just one corallite while most other similar genera are elongated and have multiple polyps.

--- SENTENCE 2174 ---
*Halomitra* is circular but has many small mouths.

--- SENTENCE 2175 ---
This coral forms flat discs about 10 cm in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2176 ---
The radiating ridges (septa) have very fine teeth on their edge and look smooth.

--- SENTENCE 2177 ---
The underside has rows of spines.

--- SENTENCE 2178 ---
Brown, sometimes with a green edge, uncommon to common, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2179 ---
[](_page_257_Picture_3.jpeg)

*Fungia concinna*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2180 ---
This coral forms discs up to at least 20 cm in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2181 ---
Radiating ridges (septa) are close together and have small teeth visible.

--- SENTENCE 2182 ---
The underside has rows of spines.

--- SENTENCE 2183 ---
Small green tentacles are usually extended.

--- SENTENCE 2184 ---
Some colonies have the radiating septa curve together in a unique pattern.

--- SENTENCE 2185 ---
Brown to yellow with green tentacles and may have purple injured areas, common to uncommon, reef slopes and backreef pools.

!

--- SENTENCE 2186 ---
[](_page_259_Picture_3.jpeg)

*Fungia fungites*.

Fungia fungites

This coral forms discs up to about 10 cm in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2190 ---
The underside has an even, dense cover of tiny granules instead of tall spines.

Brown, uncommon, reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2195 ---
Radiating ridges (septa) have very large teeth on their edge.

--- SENTENCE 2196 ---
Mottled brown and white, rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2197 ---
This coral forms discs up to at least 20 cm in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2198 ---
There may be a small hump in the center, or the entire upper surface may be raised as a low cone.

--- SENTENCE 2199 ---
The ridges (septa) have medium size teeth, may be wavy, and a few are taller than the others.

--- SENTENCE 2200 ---
Brown, uncommon to rare, reef slopes.

*Fungia scruposa*.

This coral forms irregularly oval discs up to at least 15 cm in length.

--- SENTENCE 2203 ---
The center has a very pronounced hump.

--- SENTENCE 2204 ---

--- SENTENCE 2205 ---
The underside has a large "attachment scar" in the center.

--- SENTENCE 2206 ---
Grey, rare, reef slopes, seen only so far at Fagasa, on sand.

This coral forms oval discs up to at least 15 cm in length.

--- SENTENCE 2209 ---
There is usually little or no central hump, and the coral is not distorted.

--- SENTENCE 2210 ---

--- SENTENCE 2211 ---
The underside is covered with uniform tiny granules.

--- SENTENCE 2212 ---

--- SENTENCE 2215 ---
There is no central hump.

--- SENTENCE 2216 ---

--- SENTENCE 2217 ---
The underside is covered with fine granules.

--- SENTENCE 2218 ---

--- SENTENCE 2220 ---
### *Ctenactis* (pronounced teen-ac-tis)

*Ctenactis* forms oval or elongated, unattached colonies or single polyps.

--- SENTENCE 2221 ---
The upper surface is usually nearly flat.

--- SENTENCE 2222 ---
There is a central crack running the length of the oral surface.

--- SENTENCE 2223 ---
In some the central crack is all one crack, so it has just one mouth, and the whole coral is just one polyp.

--- SENTENCE 2224 ---
In others, the central crack is divided into sections each of which is a mouth, and the coral is a colony.

--- SENTENCE 2225 ---
From the central crack, rows of thick spines run toward the edge of the colony, these are the septa.

--- SENTENCE 2226 ---
The other side is covered with thick granular spines.

--- SENTENCE 2227 ---
Flat upper surface and rows of spines instead of rounded upper surface and ridges like *Herpolitha*.

#### 104.

--- SENTENCE 2228 ---
*Ctenactis crassa* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 2229 ---
This coral forms elongated mushroom-like corals with very spiny ridges (septa).

--- SENTENCE 2230 ---
There is a very long central furrow which is divided into sections by places where the septa cross the slit.

--- SENTENCE 2231 ---
Brown, uncommon to rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2232 ---
A colony of *Ctenactis crassa.

--- SENTENCE 2234 ---
*Ctenactis echinata* (Pallas, 1766)

--- SENTENCE 2235 ---
This coral forms elongated mushroom-like corals with very spiny ridges (septa).

--- SENTENCE 2236 ---
There is a very long central furrow which is continuous and undivided.

--- SENTENCE 2237 ---
Brown, uncommon to rare, reef slopes.

### *Herpolitha* (pronounced herp-oh-lee-tha)

*Herpolitha* forms elongated, unattached colonies with a central crack running lengthwise on the oral surface.

--- SENTENCE 2240 ---
The upper surface is usually smoothly rounded, so convex.

--- SENTENCE 2241 ---
The central crack is divided into multiple sections, each of which is a separate mouth.

--- SENTENCE 2242 ---
So it is a colony with several polyps.

--- SENTENCE 2243 ---
There are many ridges that go from the central crack towards the edge of the colony, these are septa.

--- SENTENCE 2244 ---
The other side is usually concave and is always covered with granules.

--- SENTENCE 2245 ---
The two ends of the elongated colony usually taper to a point.

--- SENTENCE 2246 ---
They are light brown to brown.

--- SENTENCE 2247 ---
Rounded upper surface with radiating ridges instead of flat upper surface and rows of spines like *Ctenactis*.

#### 106.

--- SENTENCE 2248 ---
*Herpolitha limax* (Houttuyn, 1772)

--- SENTENCE 2249 ---
This coral forms elongated mushroom-like corals with smooth ridges (septa).

--- SENTENCE 2250 ---
The coral may be bent or curved.

--- SENTENCE 2251 ---
The upper surface is often rounded.

--- SENTENCE 2252 ---
The ridges (septa) do not go all the way from the center to the edge, giving it a rough look.

--- SENTENCE 2253 ---
Brown, uncommon to rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2254 ---
[](_page_276_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Herpolitha limax*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2255 ---
[](_page_277_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Herpolitha limax*.

--- SENTENCE 2256 ---
### *Polyphyllia* (pronounced polly-fill-ee-a)

*Polyphyllia* forms unattached, dome-shaped mushroom corals with small extended tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 2257 ---
Colonies can be circular in outline, oval, elongated, or irregular.

--- SENTENCE 2258 ---
There are two species, only one of which is in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2259 ---
The species not found here is always elongated and has a thick heavy skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 2260 ---
This coral forms small to medium dome-shaped colonies that can be circular, oval, or irregular.

--- SENTENCE 2261 ---
Small tentacles are extended and cover the upper surface.

--- SENTENCE 2262 ---
This species has relatively thin skeletons and breaks fairly easily.

--- SENTENCE 2263 ---
It then regrows, forming irregularly shaped colonies in groups.

--- SENTENCE 2264 ---
Dark green, only found in one spot on the slope at Matufau school and may have been destroyed by the tsunami.

!

--- SENTENCE 2265 ---
[](_page_278_Picture_4.jpeg)

Two medium size colonies of *Polyphyllia novohibernae*.

--- SENTENCE 2266 ---
[](_page_279_Picture_0.jpeg)

### *Halomitra* (pronounced hal-oh-my-tra)

*Halomitra* forms circular bowls, which can be up to nearly 2 feet wide, though commonly less than that.

--- SENTENCE 2267 ---
The convex surface has white, star-like appearing spots, which are the mouths, of which there are many.

--- SENTENCE 2268 ---
In between the white mouths are small spiny ridges, which are the septa.

--- SENTENCE 2269 ---
The edge of the colony is always purple.

--- SENTENCE 2270 ---
The concave surface has fine spines and is usually facing down, so the colony looks like a massive colony that is attached, but it is not.

--- SENTENCE 2271 ---
It is not attached and can be picked up.

--- SENTENCE 2272 ---
Most often the oral (convex) side is up.

--- SENTENCE 2273 ---
Individual colonies may differ in how arched they are, most here seem to be fairly low arches.

--- SENTENCE 2274 ---
but there are clusters of them on the slope in some spots on the SE of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 2275 ---
Circular like *Fungia*, but with many mouths and large arched colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2276 ---
Circular and with white mouths, unlike *Sandalolitha*.

#### 108.

--- SENTENCE 2277 ---
*Halomitra pileus* (Linnaeus, 1758)

--- SENTENCE 2278 ---
This coral forms large, thin, circular inverted-bowl shaped colonies that are not attached.

--- SENTENCE 2279 ---
There are many white mouths, and the ridges (septa) have large saw-tooth teeth.

--- SENTENCE 2280 ---
Yellowbrown with a purple edge on the colony, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2281 ---
[](_page_280_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Halomitra pileus*.

--- SENTENCE 2282 ---
[](_page_281_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closup of *Halomitra pileus* showing the sawthooth spines on the edge of the septa radiating from the white mouths.

--- SENTENCE 2283 ---
### *Sandalolitha* (san-da-loh-lee-tha)

*Sandalolitha* forms oval bowls that may be less arched than *Halomitra*, and thicker.

--- SENTENCE 2284 ---
It has many short radiating ridges on the convex surface, which have smaller spines than on *Halomitra.*

--- SENTENCE 2285 ---
The color is usually a uniform brown or grey, and never has white stars at the mouths or a purple edge to the colony.

--- SENTENCE 2286 ---
The concave side is covered with small spines.

--- SENTENCE 2287 ---
Oval not circular like *Halomitra*, and mouths are not white.

#### 109*.

--- SENTENCE 2288 ---
Sandalolitha dentata* Quelch, 1884

--- SENTENCE 2289 ---
This coral forms flat ovals with an irregular outline that is often dumbbell-shaped.

--- SENTENCE 2290 ---
There is a large mouth in the center, and smaller mouths clustered around it, but no mouths near the outer edge of the colony.

--- SENTENCE 2291 ---
Brown, uncommon, reef slopes.

This coral forms domed ovals with an irregular outline.

--- SENTENCE 2295 ---
Small mouths are distributed equally all over the surface.

--- SENTENCE 2296 ---
Brown, rarely grey or green, rare, reef slopes.

### **Family Oculinidae** (pronounced ok-you-line-id-ee) has just one genus here, *Galaxea*.

### *Galaxea* (pronounced gal-aks-ee-a)

*Galaxea* forms small encrusting colonies about 2-6 inches in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2300 ---
The surface is covered with corallites which have septa that end in fairly long spines.

--- SENTENCE 2301 ---
So the surface looks like rings of spines.

--- SENTENCE 2302 ---
The spines also have tentacles on them.

--- SENTENCE 2303 ---
Colonies are usually dark green.

--- SENTENCE 2304 ---
Uncommon most places, but abundant on upper reef slope in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 2305 ---
The rings of spines are more pronounced than on *Favia*.

#### 111.

--- SENTENCE 2306 ---
*Galaxea fascicularis* (Linnaeus, 1767)

This coral forms small flat cushions with spiny corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2307 ---
Corallites are projecting and circular or oval, with septa projecting as spines and tentacles about the same size.

--- SENTENCE 2308 ---
Corallites are about 10 mm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2309 ---
Green or green and brown, reef slopes, uncommon but abundant in Fagatele Bay on the upper reef slope.

!

--- SENTENCE 2310 ---
[](_page_286_Picture_5.jpeg)

A colony of *Galaxea fascicularis*.

--- SENTENCE 2311 ---

--- SENTENCE 2312 ---
**Family Pectiniidae** (pronounced pek-tin-ee-id-ee) has genera here which are very spiny.

--- SENTENCE 2313 ---
"Echino" means spiny.

### *Echinomorpha* (pronounced ee-kine-oh-morf-a)

*Echinomorpha* forms small encrusting corals which usually just have one large polyp.

--- SENTENCE 2314 ---
The center of the polyp is lower than the rest of the coral, and may be a different color from the rest.

--- SENTENCE 2315 ---
The outer part of the coral gently slopes down to the edge of the coral.

--- SENTENCE 2316 ---
The outer surface is covered with uniform small spines.

--- SENTENCE 2317 ---
Not like other genera.

--- SENTENCE 2318 ---
There is only one species, which was previously placed in *Echinophyllia*.

#### 112.

--- SENTENCE 2319 ---
*Echinomorpha nishihirai* (Veron, 1990)

--- SENTENCE 2320 ---
This coral forms small encrusting corals with a central depression in the center of the corallite, and many sharp spines on its surface.

--- SENTENCE 2321 ---
Many are solitary, but there may be about 1-4 corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2322 ---
Grey-green, very rare, reef slopes, seen in Fagatele Bay and on Ofu so far.

!

--- SENTENCE 2325 ---
### *Echinophyllia* (pronounced ee-kine-oh-fill-ee-a)

*Echinophyllia* forms thin plates close to the substrate on deeper slope areas.

--- SENTENCE 2326 ---
The surface is covered with spines.

--- SENTENCE 2327 ---
The genus name means "spine loving."

--- SENTENCE 2328 ---
Corallites are about a quarter inch diameter, and are round raised cushions.

--- SENTENCE 2329 ---
They are often hard to spot among the spines.

--- SENTENCE 2330 ---
Usually grey or brown, rare.

--- SENTENCE 2331 ---
Corallites are not inclined to face the outer edge of the plate, as they are in *Mycedium*, and form mounds more than on *Oxypora*.

#### 113.

--- SENTENCE 2332 ---
*Echinophyllia aspera* (Ellis & Solander, 1788)

--- SENTENCE 2333 ---
This coral forms large thin sheets that are encrusting in the center and a raised thin plate at the edge.

--- SENTENCE 2334 ---
The surface is very spiny and lumpy.

--- SENTENCE 2335 ---
Lumps are irregular and about 1 cm in diameter and are where the polyps are, but the polyps are often hard to recognize.

--- SENTENCE 2336 ---
The lumps are not inclined.

--- SENTENCE 2337 ---
Brown, reddish-brown or grey, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2338 ---
[](_page_290_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Echinophyllia aspera*.

--- SENTENCE 2339 ---

--- SENTENCE 2340 ---
### *Oxypora* (oks-ee-por-a)

*Oxypora* forms thin plates close to the substrate on deeper slope areas.

--- SENTENCE 2341 ---
The surface is covered with spines.

--- SENTENCE 2342 ---
Corallites are about an eighth inch diameter, round but usually not raised.

--- SENTENCE 2343 ---
They are hard to spot among the spines unless they are colored differently from the rest of the colony.

--- SENTENCE 2344 ---
Usually grey or brown, uncommon to rare.

--- SENTENCE 2345 ---
Corallites are smaller than on *Echinophyllia*, and not inclined as on *Mycedium*

#### 114.

--- SENTENCE 2346 ---
*Oxypora lacera* Verrill, 1864

--- SENTENCE 2347 ---
This coral forms thin plates which are covered with fine spines.

--- SENTENCE 2348 ---
If polyp mouths are a contrasting color to the rest of the plate, they are obvious.

--- SENTENCE 2349 ---
Polyp mouths are surrounded by a ring of raised spines.

--- SENTENCE 2350 ---
Brown, grey or green, sometimes with pink or white polyp mouths, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2353 ---
*Oxypora crassispinosa* Nemenzo, 1979

This coral forms thin plates with many thick rounded spines which may be in rows or even fuse into ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2354 ---
Corallites are not obvious.

--- SENTENCE 2355 ---
Brown with white spines, uncommon to rare, reef slopes.

### *Mycedium* (pronounced my-see-dee-um)

*Mycedium* forms thin plate corals that are usually close to the substrate, on deeper reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2359 ---
The surface of the colony is usually very rough with large spines.

--- SENTENCE 2360 ---
If corallites can be distinguished, they lean over, pointing toward the outer edge of the colony.

--- SENTENCE 2361 ---
The leaning corallites are the distinctive and defining feature of this genus.

--- SENTENCE 2362 ---
Less commonly, the spines are much smaller and the corallites are easy to see.

--- SENTENCE 2363 ---
Dominates some deep slopes, common to uncommon elsewhere, rare in shallow.

--- SENTENCE 2364 ---
Mycedium elephantotus* (Pallas, 1766)

--- SENTENCE 2365 ---
This coral forms thin plates which have many raised corallites which are strongly inclined so they point towards the edge of the plate.

--- SENTENCE 2366 ---
Small ridges run radially on the plate.

--- SENTENCE 2367 ---
Grey or tan, rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2368 ---
[](_page_296_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Mycedium elephantotus*

!

--- SENTENCE 2369 ---
[](_page_297_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Mycedium elephantotus*.

--- SENTENCE 2370 ---
*Family Mussidae* (pronounced muss-id-ee) has more soft flesh than other genera, though it is not always obvious.

--- SENTENCE 2371 ---
Polyps and corallites are larger than on many other genera, in most species.

--- SENTENCE 2372 ---
Most species are spiny.

### *Acanthastrea* (pronounced ak-an-thas-tree-a)

*Acanthastrea* is usually encrusting but rarely massive, has medium size polyps, is spiny, and may have some flesh that can be seen.

--- SENTENCE 2373 ---
Corallites can be separated or joined by a single ridge.

--- SENTENCE 2374 ---
It has corallites about 1 cm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2375 ---
Colors can be greens and browns and mottled patterns.

--- SENTENCE 2376 ---
Spinier than *Favia* or *Favites*, which it can otherwise resemble closely.

#### 117.

--- SENTENCE 2377 ---
*Acanthastrea brevis* Milne Edwards & Haime, 1849

--- SENTENCE 2378 ---
This coral forms small encrusting colonies covered with a dense forest of long thin spines.

--- SENTENCE 2379 ---
Corallites are medium size circular depressions with space between them.

--- SENTENCE 2380 ---
Mottled brown with cream spines, rare, reef slopes.

This coral forms small encrusting colonies with an even covering of small short spines.

--- SENTENCE 2385 ---
Corallites are medium size circular depressions with space between them, and there is enough flesh for there to often be circular folds of flesh around the corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2386 ---
Grey, dark or light brown, rarely brilliant green, uncommon to rare, reef slopes.

This coral forms small encrusting colonies with an even covering of small short spines.

There is no space between corallites, they share common walls like *Favites*.

This coral forms hemispherical massive colonies with larger corallites than the other *Acanthastrea*.

Grey, very rare (only one colony found so far), reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 2402 ---
### *Lobophyllia* (pronounced lobo-fill-ee-a)

--- SENTENCE 2403 ---
*Lobophyllia* forms colonies that have what appears to be rounded ridges at first glace.

--- SENTENCE 2404 ---
A closer look will reveal that the rounded ridges are all circular or ovals, so they connect in a circle of some sort.

--- SENTENCE 2405 ---
Each circle or oval is about 1-3 inches across.

--- SENTENCE 2406 ---
There is a very thin crack between ovals.

--- SENTENCE 2407 ---
The crack is actually very deep, because the circles and ovals are individual polps and corallites on the ends of long thick branches.

--- SENTENCE 2408 ---
We call these colonies "semimassive" because they look like a solid massive coral, but actually are not, they are branching with the branches hidden.

--- SENTENCE 2409 ---
*Lobophyllia* thus has large polyps, about 1-3 inches diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2410 ---
Lobophyllia colonies can get to be 5 or even 10 feet in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2411 ---
They are usually brown or grey.

--- SENTENCE 2412 ---
They are so common at medium depths in places in the SE coast of Tutuila that they dominate those areas, but they are uncommon other places.

--- SENTENCE 2413 ---
*Lobophyllia* has larger polyps than most genera, including *Caulastrea*.

--- SENTENCE 2414 ---
What appears to be a double ridge has a very deep crack in the center unlike the ridges of *Symphyllia*, which have no crack, so *Symphyllia* is massive while *Lobophyllia* is branching.

#### 121.

--- SENTENCE 2415 ---
*Lobophyllia hemprichii* (Ehrenberg, 1834)

--- SENTENCE 2416 ---
This coral forms large gently rolling fields of large corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2417 ---
Corallites are about 4-6 cm in diameter, and have a large rounded edge with slight radiating ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2418 ---
Some corallites are circular but many are pinched or distorted to various degrees.

--- SENTENCE 2419 ---
Brown, dominates medium depth slopes of the SE of Tutuila, otherwise uncommon.

!

--- SENTENCE 2420 ---

A whole colony of *Lobophyllia hemprichii*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2422 ---
[](_page_308_Picture_0.jpeg)

--- SENTENCE 2423 ---
The white and yellow areas are disease.

### *Symphyllia* (pronounced sim-fill-ee-a)

*Symphyllia* is massive or encrusting, and has large rounded winding ridges on it.

--- SENTENCE 2424 ---
Ridges are about a quarter inch to a half inch wide.

--- SENTENCE 2425 ---
*Symphyllia* can be called "brain corals" from similarity to the ridges on a brain.

--- SENTENCE 2426 ---
It may be brilliant orange or greens or browns.

--- SENTENCE 2427 ---
The ridges may have a tiny groove down the center, but it is not a crack, and if you feel, there is no crack, unlike *Lobophyllia* which it can otherwise look like.

--- SENTENCE 2428 ---
The ridges are wider than on *Oulophyllia*, and much wider than on *Platygyra*

122.

--- SENTENCE 2429 ---
*Symphyllia agaricia* Milne Edwards & Haime, 1849 "brain coral"

This coral forms massive colonies covered with large rounded ridges that meander over the surface, a "brain coral."

--- SENTENCE 2430 ---
The ridges have a finely bumpy surface.

--- SENTENCE 2431 ---
Brown or red or orange, uncommon to rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2432 ---
[](_page_309_Picture_5.jpeg)

A colony of *Symphyllia agaricia*.

--- SENTENCE 2434 ---
**Family Merulinidae** (pronounced merr-you-line-id-ee) has some genera with small ridges and one genus with bumps.

### *Hydnophora* (pronounced hide-no-for-a)

--- SENTENCE 2435 ---
*Hydnophora* can be massive, or have an encrusting base with branches, or be all branches.

--- SENTENCE 2436 ---
In all cases the surface is covered with fairly sharp bumps.

--- SENTENCE 2437 ---
If the bumps are tiny they are nearly round, larger bumps are usually oval or elongated into short ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2438 ---
The bumps are between corallites and have tiny ridges on their sides that are septa.

--- SENTENCE 2439 ---
The bumps are called "hydnophores" hence the name.

--- SENTENCE 2440 ---
In encrusting and branching colonies, tentacles are often extended, obscuring the bumps.

--- SENTENCE 2441 ---
The bumps are usually larger and are not rounded with corallites on them like on *Pocillopora*, and the bumps are not smooth like on *Montipora* and the tentacles if present are larger.

#### 123.

--- SENTENCE 2442 ---
*Hydnophora microconos* (Lamarck, 1816)

--- SENTENCE 2443 ---
This coral forms encrusting or massive colonies covered with small circular bumps that are smaller than on other *Hydnophora*.

--- SENTENCE 2444 ---
Light tan to brown, uncommon, shallow slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2448 ---
This coral forms encrusting sheets which usually have irregular upward growths on them.

--- SENTENCE 2449 ---
The surface is covered with small oval bumps which are surrounded by small tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 2450 ---
The tentacles may extend far enough to obscure the bumps.

--- SENTENCE 2451 ---
Grey or green, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2455 ---
This coral forms masses of thin branches that sub-branch often.

--- SENTENCE 2456 ---
Branches are about the diameter of a pencil.

--- SENTENCE 2457 ---
The branches have elongated bumps or ridges on them, but the ridges are often obscured by fine tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 2458 ---
Grey, rare, reef slopes, present in Fagatele Bay and Larson's Bay.

--- SENTENCE 2461 ---
### *Merulina* (pronounced merr-you-line-a)

*Merulina* forms thin plates.

--- SENTENCE 2462 ---
The plates have small, irregularly lumpy ridges on them.

--- SENTENCE 2463 ---
The ridges diverge and unite irregularly, but always run toward the edge of the plate.

--- SENTENCE 2464 ---
Often it forms thickets of plates.

--- SENTENCE 2465 ---
Sometimes colonies have bumps or columns growing on them.

--- SENTENCE 2466 ---
Usually brown but can be other colors.

--- SENTENCE 2467 ---
*Merulina* differs from *Pachyseris* in that the ridges run radially not concentrically, and the ridges are bumpy not smooth and don't have a sharp ridge.

--- SENTENCE 2468 ---
The plates have ridges instead of round corallites like *Echinopora*.

--- SENTENCE 2469 ---
Ridges are nearly parallel unlike on *Leptoseris* where they intersect and go all directions.

#### 126.

--- SENTENCE 2470 ---
*Merulina ampliata* (Ellis & Solander, 1786)

--- SENTENCE 2471 ---
This coral forms thin plates which have small rounded radiating ridges on them.

--- SENTENCE 2472 ---
The ridges are rough with small ridges (septa) running sideways on them.

--- SENTENCE 2473 ---
Brown, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2474 ---
*Merulina ampliata* can form large fields of plates growing up at a 45 degree angle.

!

--- SENTENCE 2475 ---
[](_page_318_Picture_0.jpeg)

This colony of *Merulina ampliata* is mostly encrusting and has small columns or knobs on it.

!

--- SENTENCE 2476 ---

*Merulina ampliata* plates showing the radiating ridges.

!

--- SENTENCE 2477 ---
[](_page_320_Picture_0.jpeg)

--- SENTENCE 2478 ---
### *Scapophyllia* (pronounced skap-oh-fill-ee-a)

*Scapophyllia* forms encrusting bases which may have thick oval bumps or columns on them, on reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2479 ---
Here they don't form columns and only sometimes form bumps.

--- SENTENCE 2480 ---
The surface has small irregularly lumpy ridges on them much like *Merulina*.

--- SENTENCE 2481 ---
Usually light yellow or brown or cream.

--- SENTENCE 2482 ---
There is only one species.

--- SENTENCE 2483 ---
Encrusting not plates like in *Merulina*, and light colors instead of dark brown.

#### 127.

--- SENTENCE 2484 ---
*Scapophyllia cylindrica* Milne Edwards & Haime, 1848

This coral forms encrusting colonies which may have some knobby growths on it.

--- SENTENCE 2485 ---
The surface is covered with ragged ridges which fuse so they surround small groups of corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2486 ---
Knobs are more common other places and can grow into columns.

--- SENTENCE 2487 ---
Grey or brown, uncommon to rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2488 ---
[](_page_321_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Scapophyllia cylindrica*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2489 ---
[](_page_322_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Scapophyllia cylindrica*.

--- SENTENCE 2490 ---
**Family Faviidae** (pronounced fav-vee-id-ee) has many genera, most of which are massive or encrusting, with medium size corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2491 ---
### *Caulastrea* (kawl-ass-tree-a)

*Caulastrea* forms colonies of branches, with a corallite on the end of each branch.

--- SENTENCE 2492 ---
Corallites are about a quarter inch to a half inch diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2493 ---
If branch ends are very close together it is semi-massive and may look a little like a small *Lobophyllia*.

--- SENTENCE 2494 ---
But in other colonies the branches are well enough separated you can see the branches.

--- SENTENCE 2495 ---
Brown or green, rare most places except in Fagatele Bay where it is uncommon at medium depths.

--- SENTENCE 2496 ---
(Milne Edwards & Haime, 1849) or *Caulastrea furcata*

This coral forms clumps of branches tightly squeezed together with a corallite at the end of each branch.

--- SENTENCE 2497 ---
Corallites are about 1-1.5 cm diameter, round or pinched, with a rounded rim that has septal ridges radiating.

--- SENTENCE 2498 ---
Brown, may have green polyp centers, uncommon (found in Fagatele Bay), reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2499 ---
See taxonomic note.

!

--- SENTENCE 2500 ---
[](_page_323_Picture_5.jpeg)

A whole colony of *Caulastrea furcata.

--- SENTENCE 2501 ---
[](_page_324_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closup of *Caulastrea echinulata*.

### *Favia* (pronounced fay-vee-a)

*Favia* usually forms massive colonies which have corallites about a quarter to half inch in diameter, though it can also be encrusting.

--- SENTENCE 2502 ---
There is a groove that separates corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2503 ---
Corallites often have a ring of spines on their upper edge that are the ends of septa.

--- SENTENCE 2504 ---
Corallites are not always perfect circles.

--- SENTENCE 2505 ---
Yellows, browns, greens, may be mottled.

--- SENTENCE 2506 ---
Corallites are always separated by a groove or space, unlike on *Favites* where they are united by a common ridge.

--- SENTENCE 2507 ---
Common *Montastrea* here have corallites that are projecting cylinders more than on *Favia.

--- SENTENCE 2508 ---
This coral forms clusters of stout columns, some of which may be oval, about 5-10 cm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2509 ---
Corallites are small (about 3 mm diameter) and volcano-shaped or flat.

--- SENTENCE 2510 ---
Reddish-brown, common, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2511 ---
[](_page_325_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Favia stelligera*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2512 ---
[](_page_326_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of the surface of a colony of *Favia stelligera*.

--- SENTENCE 2513 ---
### *Montastrea* (mont-ass-tree-a)

*Montastrea* forms massive or encrusting colonies that have corallites about a quarter inch or a bit less in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2514 ---
Corallites are circular, projecting cylinders, and quite uniform, and have a groove between corallites separating them.

--- SENTENCE 2515 ---
Massive colonies are usually cream, encrusting colonies usually brown.

--- SENTENCE 2516 ---
Corallites are more projecting than on most *Favia*.

#### 130.

--- SENTENCE 2517 ---
*Montastrea annuligera* (Milne Edwards & Haime, 1849)

--- SENTENCE 2518 ---
This coral forms small encrusting sheets or massive colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2519 ---
The corallites are circular about 5 mm in diameter, and have a narrow groove between them and the next corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2520 ---
A few septa are larger than the rest, and may be white.

--- SENTENCE 2521 ---
Brown or cream, uncommon, reef slopes, crest.

!

--- SENTENCE 2522 ---
[](_page_327_Picture_4.jpeg)

A closeup of a colony of *Montastrea annuligera*.

--- SENTENCE 2523 ---
*Montastrea curta* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 2524 ---
This coral forms small massive colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2525 ---
The corallites are circular about 5 mm in diameter, and have a narrow groove between them and the next corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2526 ---
All septa are the same size and none are a different color.

--- SENTENCE 2527 ---
Cream, common, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2528 ---
A colony of *Montastrea curta*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2529 ---

--- SENTENCE 2530 ---
### *Plesiastrea* (pronounced plees-ee-ass-tree-a)

*Plesiastrea* forms encrusting colonies that have small circular corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2531 ---
Often tentacles are extended in a ring around each corallite.

--- SENTENCE 2532 ---
Corallites are about 3 mm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2533 ---
There is only one species in the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 2534 ---
Greens or browns, rare.

--- SENTENCE 2535 ---
Smaller corallites than *Echinopora*, but larger than *Cyphastrea*, and tentacles are usually extended unlike other genera.

#### 132.

--- SENTENCE 2536 ---
*Plesiastrea versipora* (Lamarck, 1816)

--- SENTENCE 2537 ---
This coral forms encrusting sheets or mounds.

--- SENTENCE 2538 ---
The corallites are small and circular, about 3-4 mm in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2539 ---
Usually, polyps are extended as a ring of tissue and tentacles, obscuring the corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2540 ---
When the polyp is contracted, the corallite is a low cone or flat.

--- SENTENCE 2541 ---
Grey with dark brown tentacles, or green, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

### *Diploastrea* (pronounced diplo-ass-tree-a)

*Diploastrea* forms large encrusting colonies almost always on steep slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2545 ---
Corallites are easily visible as small volcano-like mounds about a quarter inch diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2546 ---
The sides of the corallites have little radiating ridges, which are the costae.

--- SENTENCE 2547 ---
There is only one species.

--- SENTENCE 2548 ---
Usually a dull green-grey color.

--- SENTENCE 2549 ---
Uncommon most places but fairly common others.

--- SENTENCE 2550 ---
No other coral is quite like this one.

--- SENTENCE 2551 ---
This coral forms huge encrusting sheets on steep slopes, with thick overhanging lower edges.

--- SENTENCE 2552 ---
The corallites are about 1 cm in diameter, and resemble volcanoes, with costae (ridges) running down their side.

--- SENTENCE 2553 ---
Dull green, sometimes grey with light centers, uncommon to common, reef slopes.

!

### *Cyphastrea* (pronounced sigh-fass-tree-a)

134.

--- SENTENCE 2557 ---
*Cyphastrea* is encrusting here, and has small corallites that are only 2-3 mm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2558 ---
The corallites project and are round, with a groove between them.

--- SENTENCE 2559 ---
Corallites are smaller than on *Echinopora* and *Plesiastrea*, and tentacles are not extended.

### *Echinopora* (pronounced ek-kine-oh-por-a)

*Echinopora* can be thin plates or encrusting.

--- SENTENCE 2562 ---
Corallites are small, around an eighth inch in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2563 ---
Corallites are always circular, slightly raised as bumps, and have some flat space between them or at least a groove.

--- SENTENCE 2564 ---
Encrusting colonies are covered with small spines and may have some lumpy, irregular, small columns.

--- SENTENCE 2565 ---
Thin plates are usually grey and encrusting colonies brown.

--- SENTENCE 2566 ---
Thin plates can form large masses of horizontal plates up to at least 20 feet across.

--- SENTENCE 2567 ---
Spinier and corallites may be more widely spaced and not project as far as on *Montastrea*.

--- SENTENCE 2568 ---
Corallites larger than on *Plesiastrea* and *Cyphastrea*.

--- SENTENCE 2569 ---
and tentacles not extended like on *Plesiastrea..*

--- SENTENCE 2570 ---
Echinopora lamellosa* Esper, 1795

This coral forms thin, nearly flat plates which can make very large accumulations of plates.

--- SENTENCE 2571 ---
Corallites are small mounds of tiny spines.

--- SENTENCE 2572 ---
Uniform grey, uncommon, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2573 ---
[](_page_337_Picture_4.jpeg)

--- SENTENCE 2575 ---
*Echinopora hirsutissima* Milne Edwards & Haime, 1849

--- SENTENCE 2576 ---
This coral forms encrusting colonies, usually with some irregular bumps or columns.

--- SENTENCE 2577 ---
The corallites and colony are spinier than other *Echinopora*.

--- SENTENCE 2578 ---
Grey or brown, common, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2581 ---
### *Favites* (pronounced fav-eye-tees)

*Favites* forms encrusting or massive colonies which have corallites about a quarter to half inch in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2582 ---
There is no groove separating corallites, just a ridge.

--- SENTENCE 2583 ---
Browns, yellows, greys, uncommon.

--- SENTENCE 2584 ---
No groove separating corallites unlike *Favia*.

--- SENTENCE 2585 ---
Corallites are usually larger and more irregular than on *Goniastrea*, which usually has a rusty color.

#### 137.

--- SENTENCE 2586 ---
*Favites abdita* (Ellis & Solander, 1786)

--- SENTENCE 2587 ---
This coral forms encrusting or lumpy colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2588 ---
The walls between corallites are relatively sharp, septa small and regular, giving colonies a smooth appearance.

--- SENTENCE 2589 ---
Brown or yellow, may have green mouths, rare, reef flats and slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2592 ---
### *Goniastrea* (pronounced go-nee-ass-tree-a)

*Goniastrea* forms massive or encrusting colonies which have corallites from 2 mm diameter up to a quarter inch or so.

--- SENTENCE 2593 ---
No groove separates corallites, only a ridge that is often thin.

--- SENTENCE 2594 ---
Usually a yellowish cream or pinkish cream or rust colored.

--- SENTENCE 2595 ---
Corallites are united by a single ridge like *Favites*, but usually smaller and may be more uniform than *Favites*, plus they are more often rust colored.

#### 138.

--- SENTENCE 2596 ---
*Goniastrea edwardsi* Chevalier, 1971

--- SENTENCE 2597 ---
This coral forms small massive colonies that have small corallites separated by thick walls.

--- SENTENCE 2598 ---
Corallites are about 4-5 mm in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2599 ---
Brown, rare except in the Ofu pools.

!

--- SENTENCE 2600 ---

--- SENTENCE 2602 ---
*Goniastrea minuta* Veron, 2002

--- SENTENCE 2603 ---
This coral forms large encrusting colonies with small corallites, 2- 3 mm in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2604 ---
Walls separating corallites are thin, and the corallites are shallow.

--- SENTENCE 2605 ---
Colonies commonly host many barnacles.

--- SENTENCE 2606 ---
Brown with white spots where barnacles are located, common to abundant, crest and upper reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2611 ---
This coral forms encrusting lumpy colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2612 ---
Walls between corallites are high and thin, and commonly enclose more than one corallite together.

--- SENTENCE 2613 ---
Pinkish to yellow, can have fluorescent green tentacles, rare, reef slopes.

### *Leptastrea* (pronounced lepta-stree-a)

*Leptastrea* forms encrusting colonies which may be anything from an inch in diameter to several feet.

--- SENTENCE 2616 ---
Corallites are small, about an eighth to quarter inch in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2617 ---
There is no groove that separates corallites on most species, but one species has a ring of tiny spines around each corallite which are the ends of septa.

--- SENTENCE 2618 ---
Browns or sometimes dark green, usually uncommon but sometimes abundant.

--- SENTENCE 2619 ---
Corallites are smaller than most *Favites*, may be less uniform than *Goniastrea*, and are usually not rust colored.

#### 141.

--- SENTENCE 2620 ---
*Leptastrea purpurea* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 2621 ---
This coral forms small encrusting or lumpy colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2622 ---
Corallites vary greatly in size from one part of the colony to another.

--- SENTENCE 2623 ---
There are more septa in large corallites than on other species.

--- SENTENCE 2624 ---
Cream with darker centers, rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2625 ---

--- SENTENCE 2628 ---
This coral produces small encrusting or lump colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2629 ---
Corallites are small, but their centers are relatively large and contrasting color from the rest of the corallite.

--- SENTENCE 2630 ---
Tentacles and flesh are usually extended but may be hard to see.

--- SENTENCE 2631 ---
Brown or grey with light centers, rare, reef slopes.

This coral forms small and medium encrusting colonies.

Corallites are all uniformly small, and the edge of a corallite is made up of the extended ends of septa, which make a ring around the corallite.

--- SENTENCE 2636 ---
There is a thin groove between corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2637 ---
Can be mottled cream and brown, all brown, all green, or brown with green centers, uncommon, reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2650 ---
### *Oulophyllia* (pronounced oo-loh-fill-ee-a)

*Oulophyllia* forms massive colonies with medium size corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2651 ---
Corallites are separated by a ridge.

--- SENTENCE 2652 ---
The ridge usually has a square top to it and may have some small spines that are the ends of septa.

--- SENTENCE 2653 ---
Corallites are about a quarter inch in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2654 ---
Often the centers of the corallites are a different color than the ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2655 ---
In one species corallites are all surrounded by ridges like in *Favites*, in another the ridges are winding with rows of corallites between them, so someone could call it brain coral.

--- SENTENCE 2656 ---
Ridges are a little more bumpy and have a square top more than on *Favites*, but no groove like *Favia*, ridges are smaller than *Symphyllia* which has rounded ridges, but larger than on *Platygyra*.

#### 145.

--- SENTENCE 2657 ---
*Oulophyllia bennettae* Veron, Pichon, & Wijsman-Best, 1977

This coral forms massive colonies with widely spaced thin meandering ridges which enclose mostly just one or two corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2658 ---
Otherwise very similar to *O. crispa*.

--- SENTENCE 2659 ---
Grey or brown, rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2660 ---
[](_page_359_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Oulophyllia bennettae*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2661 ---
[](_page_360_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Oulophyllia bennettae*.

--- SENTENCE 2662 ---
This coral forms massive colonies with widely spaced thin meandering ridges which enclose long valleys as well as short.

--- SENTENCE 2663 ---
The valleys are much wider than on *Platygyra daedalea*, the ridges are a bit larger than *Platygyra daedalea*, and have short uniform septa.

--- SENTENCE 2664 ---
Grey or brown, can have green tints, rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2665 ---
A colony of *Oulophyllia crispa*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2666 ---
[](_page_362_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Oulophyllia crispa*.

--- SENTENCE 2667 ---
### *Platygyra* (pronounced platy-djai-ra)

*Platygyra* form massive colonies, covered with thin ridges that are separated by valleys.

--- SENTENCE 2668 ---
In some species the ridges and valleys are long and meander, and they are called "brain corals."

--- SENTENCE 2669 ---
In others, valleys are short and surrounded by ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2670 ---
Ridges are smaller than on *Symphyllia* or *Oulophyllia*, but larger than on *Leptoria*.

147.

--- SENTENCE 2671 ---
*Platygyra daedalea* (Ellis & Solander, 1786)

--- SENTENCE 2672 ---
"brain coral"

This coral forms massive colonies with thin meandering ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2673 ---
Ridges do not have a sharp upper edge.

--- SENTENCE 2674 ---
Brown, yellow or grey, uncommon, reef slopes and flats.

!

--- SENTENCE 2675 ---
[](_page_363_Picture_5.jpeg)

A colony of *Platygyra daedalea*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2676 ---
[](_page_364_Picture_0.jpeg)

--- SENTENCE 2677 ---
### *Leptoria* (pronounced lep-tor-ee-a)

*Leptoria* forms massive colonies although small colonies may be encrusting.

--- SENTENCE 2678 ---
The surface is covered with tiny ridges that wind around, separated by a very thin valley where the corallites are.

--- SENTENCE 2679 ---
The ridges are uniform in shape and low, giving the colony as a whole a nearly smooth surface.

--- SENTENCE 2680 ---
It has the smallest ridges of any brain coral.

--- SENTENCE 2681 ---
There is only one species in the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 2682 ---
*Leptoria phrygia* (Ellis & Solander)

--- SENTENCE 2683 ---
"brain coral"

This coral forms massive encrusting or lumpy colonies with small meandering ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2684 ---
The ridges are very short, rounded, and can vary in size across the coral, but are very smooth and uniform.

--- SENTENCE 2685 ---
Brown, cream, sometimes fluorescent green between the ridges, common on reef slopes and uncommon on reef flats.

!

--- SENTENCE 2686 ---
[](_page_365_Picture_5.jpeg)

A colony of *Leptoria phyrgia*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2687 ---
[](_page_366_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Leptoria phrygia*.

--- SENTENCE 2688 ---
**Family Euphyllidae** corals are extremely fleshy with large tentacles or bubbles obscuring the skeleton.

### *Euphyllia* (pronounced you-fill-ee-a)

*Euphyllia* has relatively large tentacles that cover the skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 2689 ---
In most colonies here, the tentacles are long and thin and don't branch.

--- SENTENCE 2690 ---
Tentacles usually have a white tip.

--- SENTENCE 2691 ---
Most colonies have tan or brown tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 2692 ---
Some colonies could be mistaken for being massive, because the tentacles cover the skeleton and all you see is the rounded colony shape.

--- SENTENCE 2693 ---
But it is branching, so it could be called "submassive."

--- SENTENCE 2694 ---
In other colonies the branches are widely spaced and you can see that it is branching.

--- SENTENCE 2695 ---
This genus is rare here but easy to identify to genus, as no other coral genus here has tentacles this large.

--- SENTENCE 2696 ---
Can look a bit like an anemone, but it has a hard skeleton and never has clownfish which all large anemones here have.

#### 149.

--- SENTENCE 2697 ---
*Euphyllia glabrescens* (Chamisso & Eysenhardt, 1821)

--- SENTENCE 2698 ---
This coral forms round colonies covered with large anemone-like tentacles which completely obscure the skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 2699 ---
The skeleton felt with a finger has at least finger space between branches.

--- SENTENCE 2700 ---
Thin brown or thicker dark brown tentacles, thin uncommon and in shallow water, thick rare, on the deep silty bottom at Vatia.

!

--- SENTENCE 2701 ---
[](_page_367_Picture_5.jpeg)

A colony of *Euphyllia glabrescens* with thin brown tentacles.

!

--- SENTENCE 2702 ---
[](_page_368_Picture_0.jpeg)

The live polyps and tentacles are at the ends of branches, which are hidden.

--- SENTENCE 2703 ---
Here a piece of the colony reveals the branches.

!

--- SENTENCE 2704 ---
[](_page_369_Picture_0.jpeg)

A colony of *Euphyllia glabrescens* with thicker dark brown tentacles.

#### 150.

--- SENTENCE 2705 ---
*Euphyllia paradivisa* Veron, 1990

--- SENTENCE 2706 ---
This coral forms branching colonies with small tentacles covering the ends of branches.

--- SENTENCE 2707 ---
Tentacles are short and branch, so that even when partly contracted, different size knobs can be seen on tentacles and branches, side branches of the tentacles have smaller knobs.

--- SENTENCE 2708 ---
Light brown to cream, rare, below reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 2709 ---
This species has been proposed for endangered species status.

!

--- SENTENCE 2710 ---
A colony of *Euphyllia paradivisa*.

--- SENTENCE 2711 ---

--- SENTENCE 2712 ---
### *Plerogyra* (pronounced plero-djai-ra)

*Plerogyra* has its surface covered with bubbles of thin grey tissue, unlike any other coral.

--- SENTENCE 2713 ---
The skeleton is either meandering on a massive base, or short branches.

--- SENTENCE 2714 ---
It is very rare here, but easy to identify to genus.

#### 151.

--- SENTENCE 2715 ---
*Plerogyra sinuosa* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 2716 ---
This coral forms rounded corals covered with large thin tissue bubbles.

--- SENTENCE 2717 ---
The bubbles are round or oval, about 1 cm diameter, and nearly clear.

--- SENTENCE 2718 ---
Death of parts of the colony may leave clumps of living coral with bubbles on the formerly all live skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 2719 ---
Cream-clear, very rare, reef slopes.

!

--- SENTENCE 2720 ---
[](_page_372_Picture_4.jpeg)

A colony of *Plerogyra sinuosa* or *P. simplex*

!

--- SENTENCE 2721 ---
[](_page_373_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Plerogyra sinuosa* or *P. simplex*.

--- SENTENCE 2722 ---
**Family Dendrophyllidae** (pronounced den-dro-fill-ee-id-ee) has just one common genus of zooxanthellate (algae-containing) corals, and several genera that do not have zooxanthellae.

### *Turbinaria* (pronounced ter-bin-air-ee-a)

*Turbinaria* forms plates or encrusting colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2723 ---
Corallites are round projections and may be relatively widely spaced.

--- SENTENCE 2724 ---
The space (coenosteum) between the corallites is smooth.

--- SENTENCE 2725 ---
Corallites vary from about one eighth inch diameter to a quarter inch diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2726 ---
Plates can be thin or thick.

--- SENTENCE 2727 ---
Yellow-green, brown, or grey.

--- SENTENCE 2728 ---
Corallites and the surface between them are smoother than on *Montastrea*, *Echinopora*, or *Cyphastrea*, and colonies are often plates or may be encrusting, while *Montastrea* is massive and *Cyphastrea* doesn't form plates

#### 152.

--- SENTENCE 2729 ---
*Turbinaria peltata* (Esper, 1794)

--- SENTENCE 2730 ---
This coral forms large thick plates up to at least 3 feet across.

--- SENTENCE 2731 ---
Corallites are large and usually widely spaced with smooth surface between them, and the tentacles are almost always extended.

--- SENTENCE 2732 ---
Rare, gray, below the reef slope at Amaua.

!

--- SENTENCE 2733 ---
[](_page_374_Picture_5.jpeg)

A colony of *Turbinaria peltata* with tentacles retracted.

!

--- SENTENCE 2734 ---
[](_page_375_Picture_0.jpeg)

A closeup of *Turbinaria peltata* with tentacles extended.

--- SENTENCE 2735 ---
This coral forms thin plates with small low corallites on the upper surfaces; colonies may be large.

--- SENTENCE 2736 ---
Plates are usually curved and may be twisted or coiled such that undersurfaces can be seen.

--- SENTENCE 2737 ---
Polyp tentacles may be extended.

--- SENTENCE 2738 ---
Grey or tan, rare except in the Ofu pools.

!

--- SENTENCE 2739 ---
A large field of colonies of *Turbinaria mesenterina*.

--- SENTENCE 2742 ---
*Turbinaria reniformis* Bernard, 1896

--- SENTENCE 2743 ---
This coral forms thin plates which may form whorls but do not curve or coil such that the underside can be seen.

--- SENTENCE 2744 ---
Corallites are small and low, and the tentacles are usually extended.

--- SENTENCE 2745 ---
Brown-green with yellow polyps, or entirely yellow, fairly common some places like Fagatele Bay, the Ofu pools, and Rose Atoll, but otherwise uncommon.

--- SENTENCE 2749 ---
*Turbinaria stellulata* (Lamarck, 1816)

--- SENTENCE 2750 ---
This coral forms encrusting colonies that may have a little bit of their edge raised as a plate.

--- SENTENCE 2751 ---
The corallites are small but project as short tubes, the tentacles are usually not extended.

--- SENTENCE 2752 ---
Brown or purple with white corallite tips, rare, reef slopes.

### *Tubastraea* (pronounce tube-ass-tree-a)

*Tubastraea* forms small cushions with radiating cylindrical corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2756 ---
Corallites are about an eight to quarter inch diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2757 ---
Colonies are only found in shaded locations like overhangs.

--- SENTENCE 2758 ---
Rare, but uncommon on the vertical wall areas of the eastern side of the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 2759 ---
No other coral forms cushions of radiating cylindrical orange corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2760 ---
*Tubastraea coccinea* Lesson, 1829 "Orange Tube Coral"

This coral forms small clumps of radiating tube-shaped corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2761 ---
Corallites about the diameter of a pencil.

--- SENTENCE 2762 ---
They live on vertical walls or overhangs.

--- SENTENCE 2763 ---
Orange, seen only at Aua, Tutuila, on the reef slope.

!

--- SENTENCE 2766 ---
*Rhizopsammia* forms "runners" or stolons at the base of colonies, from which other colonies bud.

--- SENTENCE 2767 ---
The runners function like "runners" on strawberries.

#### 157.

--- SENTENCE 2768 ---
*Rhizopsammia verrilli* Host, 1926

This coral forms single small corallites which can branch.

--- SENTENCE 2769 ---
The base can have a rounded "runner."

--- SENTENCE 2770 ---
Colonies are orange and the runners are the same color unless covered.

--- SENTENCE 2771 ---
They live on vertical walls or overhangs.

--- SENTENCE 2775 ---
*Endopsammia* is a tiny, solitary (single-polyp) coral that also does not have zooxanthellae.

--- SENTENCE 2776 ---
*Endopsammia regularis* (Gardiner, 1899)

--- SENTENCE 2777 ---
This coral forms tiny single polyps on the undersides of rubble on the reef flat behind the reef crest.

--- SENTENCE 2778 ---
About 5 mm tall and wide, known only from Fagaitua, Tutuila so far.

--- SENTENCE 2779 ---
Previously known only from one location in New Caledonia.

--- SENTENCE 2780 ---
Light yellow, reef flat under rubble.

--- SENTENCE 2784 ---
### **Order Octocorallia**

Octocorals have exactly eight tentacles, and each tentacle has small regular side branches.

--- SENTENCE 2785 ---
Most do not form hard skeletons, but one here does.

--- SENTENCE 2786 ---
Soft corals, gorgonians, and sea pens are common octocorals.

### **Family Helioporidae (**pronounced heel-ee-oh-pore-id-ee)

### *Heliopora* (pronounced heel-ee-oh-pore-a)

*Heliopora* is the only Octocoral that forms a large solid skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 2787 ---
There is only one species, and Manu'a is the most eastern place it is known from.

--- SENTENCE 2788 ---
*Heliopora* forms branching colonies, with branches usually extending vertically.

--- SENTENCE 2789 ---
Branches may be round but usually are flattened, and can be curved or even winding.

--- SENTENCE 2790 ---
Branches are very smooth with no corallites visible, except for tiny pores or holes for the tiny polyps.

--- SENTENCE 2791 ---
If the polyps are out they appear as a white fuzz, and if examined very closely you might be able to see individual polyps with tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 2792 ---
It is a light blue to brown color.

--- SENTENCE 2793 ---
If the skeleton is broken, it is a vivid dark blue.

--- SENTENCE 2794 ---
It is uncommon on reef slopes and in pools on Ofu-Olosega, Swains, and South Bank.

--- SENTENCE 2795 ---
Olosega is the eastern most place it is known from on the planet.

--- SENTENCE 2796 ---
It is actually an Octocoral like the soft corals and gorgonians.

--- SENTENCE 2797 ---
The polyps are smaller and the surface smoother than on any Scleractinian corals, and the blue color of the skeleton is unique.

--- SENTENCE 2798 ---
*Heliopora coerulea* (Pallas, 1776)

--- SENTENCE 2799 ---
"Blue Coral"

This coral forms thin smooth blue-grey vertical paddles and/or columns which may have a thin white fuzz coating of polyps extended.

--- SENTENCE 2800 ---
Blue-grey, not uncommon in the Ofu pools, but very rare or absent elsewhere.

--- SENTENCE 2801 ---
Their skeleton is dark blue.

--- SENTENCE 2802 ---
Fairly common in the Ofu pools, rare on reef slopes in Manu'a, seen on Swains, also present on the seamount called South Bank 40 miles south of Tutuila, at 80+feet deep, not seen elsewhere.

!

--- SENTENCE 2803 ---
[](_page_390_Picture_4.jpeg)

A large colony of *Heliopora coerulea*.

!

--- SENTENCE 2804 ---
[](_page_391_Picture_0.jpeg)

Colonies of *Heliopora coerulea*.

--- SENTENCE 2805 ---
The colonies in the center have their polyps out, the others do not.

### **Class Hydrozoa**

**Hydrocorals** are hydrozoans with calcareous skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 2806 ---
One genus (*Millepora*) is zooxanthellate and a common contributor to coral reefs, and several genera are azooxanthellate, only two of which are on coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 2807 ---
**Family Milleporidae** (pronounced mill-ee-pore-id-ee) **"Fire Corals"** 

### *Millepora* (pronounced mill-ee-pore-a)

*Millepora* means "thousands" (= Mill) of pores.

--- SENTENCE 2808 ---
This is because it has many very tiny polyps and tiny holes or pores in the skeleton where the polyps are.

--- SENTENCE 2809 ---
Unless you look very closely with back lighting or use a magnifying glass, you won't see any corallites or pores.

--- SENTENCE 2810 ---
It is actually a hydrozoan, and reproduces by producing tiny medusae (jellyfish) which are brooded in tiny pockets in the skeleton and then released.

--- SENTENCE 2811 ---
The medusae

quickly produce eggs or sperm, which when united become a larva which settles and becomes a polyp that founds a new colony.

--- SENTENCE 2812 ---
*Millepora* is fairly fast growing.

--- SENTENCE 2813 ---
It is also one of the most sensitive to mass coral bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 2814 ---
*Millepora* can be encrusting, encrusting base with vertical paddles, or branching.

--- SENTENCE 2815 ---
Surfaces may be smooth or bumpy.

--- SENTENCE 2816 ---
Colony shapes are highly variable.

--- SENTENCE 2817 ---
It is most often yellow or brown, but can be light green, pink, or dark reddish-purple.

--- SENTENCE 2818 ---
They have zooxanthellae and are found in light.

--- SENTENCE 2819 ---
Touching it with anything but your finger tips will likely give a sting, and it is the only coral that can sting.

--- SENTENCE 2820 ---
They are called "fire corals" because of their sting.

--- SENTENCE 2821 ---
Other hydrozoans like the feathery hydroids can sting as well, but they do not have skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 2822 ---
The smooth yellow-brown colonies are distinct, and no other hard coral can sting.

#### 160.

--- SENTENCE 2823 ---
*Millepora dichotoma* Forskål, 1775

--- SENTENCE 2824 ---
This coral forms smooth branching colonies that usually form fans and which often have the branches anastomosing, though not always.

--- SENTENCE 2825 ---
Branches are nearly round and usually about 1 cm thick, and are a yellow-tan color.

--- SENTENCE 2826 ---
Found in backreef pools, fairly common in the Ofu pools where they were mostly killed by bleaching, rare on upper reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 2827 ---
A bushy colony of light colored *Millepora dichotoma* in the Ofu pools.
