--- SENTENCE 2830 ---
*Millepora murrayi* Quelch, 1884

--- SENTENCE 2831 ---
This coral forms smooth branching colonies that form mounds of fans of branches projecting in all directions.

--- SENTENCE 2832 ---
Most branches grow upward, but some branches curve downward as they grow outward and have branches extending upward from their upper surface.

--- SENTENCE 2833 ---
Branches are about 0.5 cm diameter, are round and smooth, and an orangeyellow color.

--- SENTENCE 2834 ---
Found so far in only two backreef pools in the harbor.

Hemprich & Ehrenberg, 1834

--- SENTENCE 2839 ---
This coral forms encrusting colonies with thick paddle-shaped columns and plates growing up from them.

--- SENTENCE 2840 ---
The upper edges of paddles and plates are flat or rounded, and they are commonly about 1 cm thick.

--- SENTENCE 2841 ---
Colonies are dark brown with light yellow tops on columns and plates.

--- SENTENCE 2842 ---
Fairly common, on upper slopes and reef crest.

This coral forms bumpy colonies that encrust rubble.

Most colonies are a greenish yellow color with some small spotting, but some colonies are pink-purple, and some colonies have some of both.

--- SENTENCE 2863 ---
**Family Stylasteridae** (pronounced style-ass-terr-id-ee) has several genera in it, all of which are azooxanthellate, and only two of which have species on coral reefs.

### *Stylaster* (pronounced style-aster)

*Stylaster* is a genus with what appears to have only one species in shallow water in the Samoan archipelago, but the Pacific species are in need of taxonomic revision.

--- SENTENCE 2864 ---
*Stylaster* forms little fans of bright pink, red, or purple that are in shaded holes.

--- SENTENCE 2865 ---
The fans have thin, intricate branches.

--- SENTENCE 2866 ---
They do not have zooxanthellae.

165.

--- SENTENCE 2867 ---
"Lace Coral"

This coral form small fans of thin pink to purple to white branches.

--- SENTENCE 2868 ---
Branches are rough with corallites, and often have a zig-zag look.

--- SENTENCE 2869 ---
Colonies are about 1-4 cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 2870 ---
They are exclusively found under overhangs.

--- SENTENCE 2871 ---
Not uncommon in cavities.

--- SENTENCE 2872 ---
The species name is not known.

!

--- SENTENCE 2875 ---
### *Distichopora* (pronounced dis-tee-co-por-a)

*Distichopora* is a genus with only one species found in shallow water in the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 2876 ---
They form very small purple colonies with thick oval branches, in shaded holes.

--- SENTENCE 2877 ---
They do not have zooxanthellae.

166.

--- SENTENCE 2878 ---
*Distichopora violacea* (Pallas, 1766)

--- SENTENCE 2879 ---
This coral forms small smooth fans of purple branches, often with white tips (the name refers to the purple color).

--- SENTENCE 2880 ---
They are almost exclusively found under overhangs.

--- SENTENCE 2881 ---
The branches are much larger and smoother than *Stylaster*, and have rounded tips.

--- SENTENCE 2882 ---
Colonies are about 1-3 cm tall.

--- SENTENCE 2883 ---
Common in places on the slopes if you search cavities.

!

--- SENTENCE 2884 ---
[](_page_411_Picture_4.jpeg)

--- SENTENCE 2885 ---
### **Taxonomic Notes**

For more taxonomic information on each hard coral species including a description of a skeletal sample from American Samoa, see the companion taxonomic monograph, Fenner (in press).

--- SENTENCE 2886 ---
*Pocillopora* is a particularly difficult genus.

--- SENTENCE 2887 ---
The only morphological differences distinguishing the species are branch shapes.

--- SENTENCE 2888 ---
A few species are relatively distinct in American Samoa, particularly *Pocillopora eydouxi* and *Pocillopora setichelli*.

--- SENTENCE 2889 ---
However, there are many colonies that appear to be intermediate between *Pocillopora damicornis* and *Pocillopora verrucosa*, although there are probably more *P. damicornis* colonies that are quite clear and distinct than those that are intermediate.

--- SENTENCE 2890 ---
There are so many colonies intermediate between *P. verrucosa* and *Pocillopora meandrina*, and so few colonies that are clearly one or other of those two species, that there is great doubt whether they are separate species.

--- SENTENCE 2891 ---
*Pocillopora ligulata* appears to have intermediates between it and *P. meandrina*.

--- SENTENCE 2892 ---
The author began work in American Samoa by distinguishing colonies that have branching morphology like *P. meandrina*, but with smaller verrucae, and called them *Pocillopora elegans*.

--- SENTENCE 2893 ---
However, there are intermediates between those two, and an examination of the type specimen of *P. elegans* in the Smithsonian Institution revealed it had large verrucae like *P. meandrina* and otherwise appeared to be the same as *P. meandrina*.

--- SENTENCE 2894 ---
For these reasons *P. elegans* is not distinguished in this guide.

--- SENTENCE 2895 ---
Recent molecular evidence has found that the morphologically distinguished species of *Pocillopora* are not distinct genetically, but there are microscopic features of the skeleton that correspond to genetically distinct species.

--- SENTENCE 2896 ---
Unfortunately, that would leave ecologists and monitoring teams unable to identify *Pocillopora* species on the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 2897 ---
In addition, the observed differences in the morphology of branching remain unexplained by the genetics at this point.

--- SENTENCE 2898 ---
For these reasons, and also because the molecular results are new and changes in the genetics are possible in the future, this guide continues to present the morphospecies.

--- SENTENCE 2899 ---
However, caution should be used in the interpretation of these morphological differences, particularly for the distinction between *P. verrucosa* and *P. meandrina*.

--- SENTENCE 2900 ---
*Pocillopora setichelli* Hoffmeister, 1929 was not recognized by Veron (2000), but these colonies in American Samoa match the description (Randall and Myers, 1983), and they are relatively easy to distinguish from other *Pocillopora* species, and can be seen growing side by side with other *Pocillopora* species.

--- SENTENCE 2901 ---
There are a few colonies that are difficult to place in one species or the other, but they are a small minority.

--- SENTENCE 2902 ---
Veron (2000) shows a photo of several under the name *P. meandrina*.

--- SENTENCE 2903 ---
Among *P. setichelli* colonies, most have flattened curving branches, a few have round branches, and rarely they may resemble *P. damicornis*, so most colonies resemble *P. meandrina*, some resemble *P. verrucosa* and a very few resemble *P. damicornis*.

--- SENTENCE 2904 ---
This raises interesting possibilities that have not been explored.

--- SENTENCE 2905 ---
*Montipora turgescens* Bernard, 1897 and *M. nodosa* (Dana, 1846).

--- SENTENCE 2906 ---
Veron (2000) shows pictures of a purple colony he has under the name *M. nodosa* that corresponds to the colonies shown here under the name *M. turgescens*.

--- SENTENCE 2907 ---
*M. nodosa* has papillae, while *M.* 

*turgescens* does not (Veron and Wallace, 1984).

--- SENTENCE 2908 ---
These colonies never have papillae, and correspond best to *M. turgescens.*

--- SENTENCE 2909 ---
*Montipora vaughani* Hoffmeister, 1925 was described from American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2910 ---
Colonies matching that description are not uncommon here.

--- SENTENCE 2911 ---
It is, however, very close to *M. foveolata* or *M. venosa*, but appears to be distinct enough to be a valid species.

--- SENTENCE 2912 ---
*Acropora abrotanoides* (Lamarck, 1816).

--- SENTENCE 2913 ---
This coral was referred to as *A. danai* in the older literature, as the type specimen of *A. abrotanoides* could not be found.

--- SENTENCE 2914 ---
It has since been found, so the oldest name, *A. abrotanoides*, is once again used.

--- SENTENCE 2915 ---
*Acropora globiceps* (Dana, 1964) and *Acropora humilis* (Dana, 1846).

--- SENTENCE 2916 ---
*Acropora humilis* has long (and often) been reported here, including in Wolstenholm et al. (2003).

--- SENTENCE 2917 ---
However, the axial corallites of colonies in American Samoa are always small and tubular, and never large and dome-shaped.

--- SENTENCE 2918 ---
Other differences between these two species are very slight, but are consistent with colonies here being *A. gobiceps*.

--- SENTENCE 2919 ---
The author has examined the type specimen of *A. globiceps* in the Smithsonian and it has small tubular axial corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2920 ---
The two species are easy to tell apart by the axial corallite.

--- SENTENCE 2921 ---
*Acropora intermedia* (Brook, 1891).

--- SENTENCE 2922 ---
Wallace (1978; 1999), Wallace and Dai (1997) and Wallace and Wolstenholme (1998) use the name *A. intermedia* (Brook, 1891) for this species, while Veron and Wallace (1984) and Veron (1986; 2000) use the name *A. nobilis* for it.

--- SENTENCE 2923 ---
Wallace (1999) states that the type of *A. nobilis* is a member of the robusta group, but not this species.

--- SENTENCE 2924 ---
The author has examined the holotypes of *A. nobilis* and *A. robusta,* and agrees, though has not yet been able to examine the type of *A. intermedia*.

--- SENTENCE 2925 ---
*Acropora pagoensis* Hoffmeister, 1925*.*

--- SENTENCE 2926 ---
Veron (2000) and Wallace (1999) do not recognize this species.

--- SENTENCE 2927 ---
Wallace (1999) indicates it is in the *A. selago* group, and that it is probably a synonym of *A. tenuis*, though the type specimen is too small and it could be *A. yongei*.

--- SENTENCE 2928 ---
Colonies that match Hoffmeister's photo, description, and holotype are fairly common in American Samoa, and they do not appear similar to *A. tenuis* or *A. yongei*.

--- SENTENCE 2929 ---
The type specimen is sufficiently large and clear to make a clear identification.

--- SENTENCE 2930 ---
Their identification as *A. pagoensis* was confirmed by R. Randall who has worked on coral in American Samoa and collected many.

--- SENTENCE 2931 ---
*A. pagoensis* is probably closest to *A. akajimensis* (Veron, 1990) and was identified as such by Fisk and Birkeland (2002), Green et al. (2005) and Birkeland (unpublished).

--- SENTENCE 2932 ---
However, *A. pagoensis* has radial corallites closer together, and with more variation in their shape, more that are rasp or nariform shaped, and probably thicker corallite walls.

--- SENTENCE 2933 ---
The name *A. pagoensis* is senior to *A. akajimensis*, but *A. akajimensis* appears to be a valid species, which the author has seen in New Caledonia.

--- SENTENCE 2934 ---
Wallace (1999) synonymizes *A. akajimensis* with *A. donei*.

--- SENTENCE 2935 ---
The name *A. pagoensis* was reported from American Samoa by Birkeland et al. (1979, 1982, 1985), Lamberts (1983), Green, Birkeland and Randall (1999), Coles et al. (2003), and Green et al. (2005).

--- SENTENCE 2936 ---
R. Randall reports that he has also seen *A. pagoensis* in Taiwan.

--- SENTENCE 2937 ---
It is a very distinct and easily identified valid species.

--- SENTENCE 2938 ---
*Acropora palmerae* Wells, 1954 has an uncertain status since it is indistinguishable from *A. robusta* except for the fact that it is almost entirely encrusting while *A. robusa* only has a small encrusting base and lots of branches.

--- SENTENCE 2939 ---
Colonies of *A. palmerae* can have some branches, usually short.

--- SENTENCE 2940 ---
Although it has been suggested to prefer heavy wave exposure, it appears in the same habitat as *A. robusta* in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2941 ---
Wallace (1999) and Veron (2000) recognize it.

--- SENTENCE 2942 ---
However, the only difference between *A. palmerae* and *A. robusta* is the presence, number, and length of branches, and there appears to be a continuum between the two species.

--- SENTENCE 2943 ---
Most *A. plamerae* here are bright green, while most *A. robusta* are brown, but there are green *A. robusta* and vice versa.

--- SENTENCE 2944 ---
(Gardiner, 1898) and *Isopora palifera* (Lamarck, 1816) are two of several species that have until recently been considered to be in the genus *Acropora*, under the sub-genus *Isopora*.

--- SENTENCE 2945 ---
A recent article reviewed the evidence and reported that not only do they not have an axial corallite, but also they are all brooders while *

--- SENTENCE 2946 ---
Acropora* are all broadcast spawners.

--- SENTENCE 2947 ---
The evidence is convincing that they are a separate genus (Wallace et al. 2007)

--- SENTENCE 2948 ---
Yabe & Sugiyama 1941 is not recognized by Veron (2000), but the colonies match the description (Lamberts, 1982) perfectly, and they are very easy to distinguish from other *Astreopora*, with few if any intermediates, so it is included here as a valid species.

--- SENTENCE 2949 ---
*Porites arnaudi* Reyes-Bonilla and Carricart-Ganivet, 2000 is known from the eastern Pacific, and Veron (2000) indicates that it is in our area but with some morphological differences.

--- SENTENCE 2950 ---
Samples from here are a close match to the original description, and colonies are quite similar to those in the eastern Pacific (Veron, 2000).

--- SENTENCE 2951 ---
*Porites lutea* Milne Edwards & Haime, 1851 is the name applied to this coral in Hawaii by Fenner (2005).

--- SENTENCE 2952 ---
It had long been called *Porites evermanni* Vaughan 1907 there (Maragos, 1977).

--- SENTENCE 2953 ---
Hawaii is the type location of *P. evermanni*.

--- SENTENCE 2954 ---
The two species have essentially the same arrangement of elements in their corallites, and *P. lutea* is the older name so it gets precedence, so it was called that in Fenner (2005).

--- SENTENCE 2955 ---
However there is genetic evidence that there are three genetically different species that all have the same corallite plan as *P. lutea* (Z. Foresman, personal comm.)

--- SENTENCE 2956 ---
The specimen of *P. lutea* in the Paris Museam of Natural History that is labeled as the type is not certain to actually be the type, since sometimes labels get changed, and the original description may not figure it or designate the type.

--- SENTENCE 2957 ---
Also, museum specimens of corals rarely have any tissue which could be used for genetics.

--- SENTENCE 2958 ---
This has not yet been resolved.

--- SENTENCE 2959 ---
*Pavona chiriquensis* Glynn, Mate & Stemann, 2001 is very similar to *P. varians* and has been included in it until recently.

--- SENTENCE 2960 ---
It has separated small bumps instead of long ridges, and usually has white tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 2961 ---
It is always encrusting.

--- SENTENCE 2962 ---
The two are very easy to distinguish in American Samoa, with few if any intermediates.

--- SENTENCE 2963 ---
*Pavona diffluens* (Lamarck, 1816) was known from only the Red Sea.

--- SENTENCE 2964 ---
It has actually been reported from American Samoa previously in species lists.

--- SENTENCE 2965 ---
The living colonies and skeleton match those from the Red Sea (Veron, 2000).

--- SENTENCE 2966 ---
Richard Randall likely was the first to recognize it here.

--- SENTENCE 2967 ---
*Pavona gigantea* Verrill, 1896 is well known from the eastern Pacific, but not the rest of the Pacific, though Veron (2000) indicates it is in the south-central Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 2968 ---
The living colonies and skeleton match that from the eastern Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 2969 ---
*Pavona duerdeni* and *Pavona minuta* were mis-idenfied in the past, *P. duerdeni* was identified as *P. minuta* in Veron (1986) and *P. minuta* was identified as *P. diminuta* in Veron (1990).

--- SENTENCE 2970 ---
The author learned in Hawaii what *P. duerdeni* was and then when the author examined the type specimen of *P. minuta*

--- SENTENCE 2971 ---
it became clear what that species was.

--- SENTENCE 2972 ---
Veron (2000) adopted the author's views once he saw the type specimen of *P. minuta*.

--- SENTENCE 2973 ---
*Herpolitha weberi* is a species which Veron (2000) recognizes, but Hoeksema (1989) does not.

--- SENTENCE 2974 ---
Colonies that have long septa which reach the edge of the colony here are small.

--- SENTENCE 2975 ---
Large colonies which are clearly *Herpolitha limax* usually have longer septa near the central groove than farther to the side.

--- SENTENCE 2976 ---
Thus, it appears that small colonies with long septa are just a growth stage of *H. limax*.

--- SENTENCE 2977 ---
*Echinomorpha nishihirai* (Veron, 1990) has until recently been known from Japan to Australia, but not eastward in the Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 2978 ---
It was rare most places it was known from.

--- SENTENCE 2979 ---
Fenner (2006, 2007) reported it was uncommon but not rare in Fiji, and now this report extends the known range farther eastward.

--- SENTENCE 3333 ---
[](_page_427_Picture_4.jpeg)

Douglas Fenner B.A. Reed College, 1971 Ph.D. University of Pennsylvania, 1976

Born in Michigan, USA, the author has lived in a variety of places in the states, including Florida during his high school years, which stimulated an interest in tropical marine life.

--- SENTENCE 3334 ---
During his years at Reed College in Portland, Oregon, he was introduced to biology, including invertebrate biology, studied sea urchin tube feet and respiration for his thesis and spent two summers in Hawaii studying fish behavior with his professors.

--- SENTENCE 3335 ---
Snorkeling trips to the Caribbean (including to Jamaica just before Hurricane Allen) during graduate school at the University of Pennsylvania were followed by scuba trips to the Caribbean.

--- SENTENCE 3336 ---
Research began with surveys and description of reefs in the Caribbean, including Cozumel, Roatan, Cayman Brac, Little Cayman, and St. Lucia.

--- SENTENCE 3337 ---
It became clear that to do transects you need to know your corals, and existing guides were inadequate, so Caribbean coral identification and taxonomy were next to be studied.

--- SENTENCE 3338 ---
By this time the author lived in Seattle, Washington.

--- SENTENCE 3339 ---
Then the author began to study corals in Hawaii, which led to his identification book for Hawaiian corals.

--- SENTENCE 3340 ---
Following that, he worked in the Philippines for two years, learning many coral species in that area of high diversity.

--- SENTENCE 3341 ---
This was followed by six years of working with Dr. "Charlie" J.E.N. Veron at the Australian Institute of Marine Science on the "Coral ID" electronic key to corals of the world.

--- SENTENCE 3342 ---
At that time, the author began to be invited to study and record corals during brief trips to a variety of places around the Indo-Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 3343 ---
In November, 2003, the author began work at the Dept. Marine & Wildlife Resources, in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 3344 ---
He began working on coral reef monitoring there a year later and has continued with that, and continued to make trips to study corals around the Indo-Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 3345 ---
Currently, the author has been to 14 islands in the Caribbean, and 14 areas of the Indo-Pacific, plus the Mediterranean, studying coral.
